
Spring boot application - scheduler every 10 mins , can we exit after x amount of work done ? 

Yes. A Spring Boot scheduled job can stop processing after X units of work, while the scheduler continues to run every 10 minutes.

1. Process maximum X records per scheduler run

This is usually the best design.

@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class WorkScheduler {

    private static final int MAX_WORK_PER_RUN = 100;

    private final WorkService workService;

    @Scheduled(fixedDelay = 10, timeUnit = TimeUnit.MINUTES)
    public void processWork() {

        int processedCount = 0;

        while (processedCount < MAX_WORK_PER_RUN) {

            Optional<WorkItem> workItem = workService.getNextPendingItem();

            if (workItem.isEmpty()) {
                break; // No more pending work
            }

            try {
                workService.process(workItem.get());
                processedCount++;
            } catch (Exception ex) {
                // Log failure and decide whether to continue
                log.error("Failed to process work item {}", workItem.get().getId(), ex);
            }
        }

        log.info("Scheduler completed. Processed {} items", processedCount);
    }
}

Behaviour:

Scheduler starts.
Processes a maximum of 100 items.
Exits the current execution after 100 items.
Runs again 10 minutes after the previous execution finishes.
2. Fetch only X records from the database

This is generally cleaner than repeatedly fetching one item.

@Scheduled(fixedDelay = 10, timeUnit = TimeUnit.MINUTES)
public void processWork() {

    int batchSize = 100;

    List<WorkItem> items = workRepository
            .findTop100ByStatusOrderByCreatedAtAsc(WorkStatus.PENDING);

    for (WorkItem item : items) {
        try {
            workService.process(item);
        } catch (Exception ex) {
            log.error("Failed to process item {}", item.getId(), ex);
        }
    }

    log.info("Processed {} items", items.size());
}

Repository:

public interface WorkRepository extends JpaRepository<WorkItem, Long> {

    List<WorkItem> findTop100ByStatusOrderByCreatedAtAsc(
            WorkStatus status
    );
}

For a configurable limit:

@Query("""
       SELECT w
       FROM WorkItem w
       WHERE w.status = :status
       ORDER BY w.createdAt
       """)
List<WorkItem> findPendingItems(
        @Param("status") WorkStatus status,
        Pageable pageable
);

Usage:

List<WorkItem> items = workRepository.findPendingItems(
        WorkStatus.PENDING,
        PageRequest.of(0, 100)
);
3. Stop based on both count and execution time

This prevents one scheduler run from taking too long.

@Scheduled(fixedDelay = 10, timeUnit = TimeUnit.MINUTES)
public void processWork() {

    int maxRecords = 100;
    Duration maxDuration = Duration.ofMinutes(5);

    Instant startedAt = Instant.now();
    int processedCount = 0;

    while (processedCount < maxRecords) {

        if (Duration.between(startedAt, Instant.now())
                .compareTo(maxDuration) >= 0) {
            log.info("Stopping because the maximum duration was reached");
            break;
        }

        Optional<WorkItem> nextItem = workService.getNextPendingItem();

        if (nextItem.isEmpty()) {
            log.info("No pending work");
            break;
        }

        workService.process(nextItem.get());
        processedCount++;
    }

    log.info(
            "Run completed. Processed={}, duration={}",
            processedCount,
            Duration.between(startedAt, Instant.now())
    );
}

This run stops when any condition is reached:

100 records processed
Five minutes elapsed
No more pending work
4. Permanently stop future scheduler executions

When the scheduler should stop completely after a total target is reached, use a flag.

private final AtomicBoolean schedulerEnabled = new AtomicBoolean(true);

@Scheduled(fixedDelay = 10, timeUnit = TimeUnit.MINUTES)
public void processWork() {

    if (!schedulerEnabled.get()) {
        return;
    }

    long remainingCount = workService.countPendingItems();

    if (remainingCount == 0) {
        schedulerEnabled.set(false);
        log.info("All work completed. Scheduler disabled.");
        return;
    }

    workService.processBatch(100);
}

However, this flag is only stored in memory. After an application restart, it becomes enabled again. For a production system, store the scheduler status in:

A database scheduler-control table
Redis
Application configuration
A job-control table containing status such as RUNNING, PAUSED, and COMPLETED
5. Do not normally terminate the Spring Boot application

Avoid this merely to stop the scheduled work:

System.exit(0);

That shuts down the complete Spring Boot application, including:

APIs
Database connections
Other schedulers
Event listeners
Kafka or RabbitMQ consumers

Use it only when the Spring Boot application is designed as a temporary batch application.

Recommended design

For your scheduler, I recommend:

@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "${scheduler.delay-ms:600000}")
public void execute() {
    workService.processBatch(100);
}
@Transactional
public void processBatch(int batchSize) {

    List<WorkItem> items = workRepository.findPendingItems(
            WorkStatus.PENDING,
            PageRequest.of(0, batchSize)
    );

    for (WorkItem item : items) {
        processItem(item);
    }
}

This means:

Every 10 minutes, process up to 100 pending records and then finish the current scheduler execution.

Also, prefer fixedDelay when the next run should begin 10 minutes after the previous run finishes. Use fixedRate only when you need execution based on a fixed clock interval, regardless of how long the previous run takes.


Requirement is Db2 I have 4 million data active ( total 15 million) Application doing on demand seamless migration to maria db when users login 3 million already done Now issue is users not logged in so far Plan is use the existing data migration service which have connectivity for both db First step is Db2 - copy the the table structure , export data active data to the new table using query soft delete 0 Next how to proceed This deployed in pcf 6 instances I am thinking every instance have 10 mins scheduler to delete the data from db2 which already migrated Need to build intelligence based on pcf DC and index - offset and limit to fetch the customer id from Maria db and delete in db2 Once done 1 million data will be leftover Need to build intelligence based again and read from db2 and insert to Maria db Is that right approach? Hem much time it take ?


Your overall idea is workable, but I would change the sequencing and partitioning strategy.

The main risks in the current proposal are:

All six PCF instances running the same scheduler and processing duplicate customers.
Using OFFSET/LIMIT while records are being inserted or deleted.
Deleting DB2 records before migration reconciliation is fully proven.
Relying on CF_INSTANCE_INDEX as permanent ownership; instance indexes can change during restart, scaling, or deployment.
Running millions of deletes against the production DB2 table during normal business hours.
Recommended migration flow
Current state
Location	Approximate records
DB2 active records	4 million
Already migrated to MariaDB	3 million
Still missing from MariaDB	Approximately 1 million
DB2 total, including inactive	15 million

Your migration should have two logically separate phases:

Identify and migrate the missing one million.
Archive or delete DB2 data only after validation and approval.

Do not make DB2 deletion the first migration step.

Phase 1: Create a stable DB2 migration snapshot

Creating a separate table containing active customers is reasonable.

CREATE TABLE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_2026
LIKE CUSTOMER;

Then copy the required records:

INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_2026
SELECT *
FROM CUSTOMER
WHERE SOFT_DELETE = 0;

However, copying all columns may be unnecessary. Prefer copying only:

Customer ID
Migration-required data
Last updated timestamp
Migration status fields, when allowed

Example:

CREATE TABLE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_2026 AS
(
    SELECT
        CUSTOMER_ID,
        NAME,
        MOBILE_NUMBER,
        EMAIL,
        LAST_UPDATED_TIMESTAMP
    FROM CUSTOMER
    WHERE 1 = 0
)
WITH NO DATA;

Then:

INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_2026
SELECT
    CUSTOMER_ID,
    NAME,
    MOBILE_NUMBER,
    EMAIL,
    LAST_UPDATED_TIMESTAMP
FROM CUSTOMER
WHERE SOFT_DELETE = 0;

Add an index:

CREATE UNIQUE INDEX IDX_MIGRATION_CUSTOMER_ID
ON CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_2026 (CUSTOMER_ID);

The snapshot gives you a stable set of four million records while normal DB2 customer data continues changing.

But you need to decide what happens to updates occurring after the snapshot:

Existing on-demand migration continues handling users who log in.
Before final cutover, run a delta migration using LAST_UPDATED_TIMESTAMP.
Alternatively, temporarily freeze updates during final reconciliation.
Phase 2: Identify the one million records missing from MariaDB

Your idea is:

Read migrated customer IDs from MariaDB and delete them from the DB2 snapshot, leaving approximately one million.

It can work, but deleting approximately three million DB2 snapshot records is a relatively expensive way to perform a set comparison.

A safer option is to maintain migration status in the DB2 snapshot.

Option A: Status column

Add columns:

ALTER TABLE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_2026
ADD COLUMN MIGRATION_STATUS VARCHAR(20)
DEFAULT 'PENDING';

ALTER TABLE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_2026
ADD COLUMN MIGRATED_AT TIMESTAMP;

ALTER TABLE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_2026
ADD COLUMN RETRY_COUNT INTEGER DEFAULT 0;

Possible statuses:

PENDING
ALREADY_MIGRATED
IN_PROGRESS
MIGRATED
FAILED
RECONCILED

For IDs already found in MariaDB:

UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_2026
SET MIGRATION_STATUS = 'ALREADY_MIGRATED'
WHERE CUSTOMER_ID IN (?, ?, ?, ...);

This is better than physically deleting rows because it preserves:

Audit history
Restart capability
Counts
Failure investigation
Reconciliation evidence

After completion, the snapshot table itself can be dropped.

Option B: Separate control table

This is my preferred enterprise approach.

CREATE TABLE MIGRATION_WORK_ITEM (
    CUSTOMER_ID BIGINT NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    STATUS VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'PENDING',
    CLAIMED_BY VARCHAR(100),
    CLAIMED_AT TIMESTAMP,
    COMPLETED_AT TIMESTAMP,
    RETRY_COUNT INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
    ERROR_MESSAGE VARCHAR(1000)
);

Populate it from the active DB2 customers:

INSERT INTO MIGRATION_WORK_ITEM (CUSTOMER_ID)
SELECT CUSTOMER_ID
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_2026;

The source data and migration control are then separated.

Do not use MariaDB OFFSET/LIMIT

Avoid this:

SELECT CUSTOMER_ID
FROM CUSTOMER
ORDER BY CUSTOMER_ID
LIMIT 1000 OFFSET 500000;

It has several problems:

Large offsets become progressively slower.
Rows may shift when inserts or deletes occur.
Multiple PCF instances may process overlapping pages.
A PCF restart may cause skipped or repeated ranges.
Scaling from six instances to another number changes ownership.

Use keyset pagination instead:

SELECT CUSTOMER_ID
FROM CUSTOMER
WHERE CUSTOMER_ID > :lastCustomerId
ORDER BY CUSTOMER_ID
LIMIT 1000;

Even better, use non-overlapping customer-ID ranges or a database-backed work queue.

Spring Batch partitioning is explicitly designed to divide work into distinct, non-overlapping input ranges and execute them concurrently



PCF six-instance scheduling
Do not let all six instances blindly execute the same scheduler

With a normal Spring annotation:

@Scheduled(fixedDelay = 600000)

all six PCF instances will execute it independently.

That can cause:

Duplicate reads
Duplicate inserts
Duplicate DB2 updates or deletes
Lock contention
Increased DB2 load
Difficult restart behaviour
Recommended solution: database-backed work claiming

Every instance may run the scheduler, but each instance must claim different work.

Cloud Foundry exposes CF_INSTANCE_INDEX, but use it only as worker identification, not as permanent partition ownership.

For example:

String workerId =
    System.getenv("CF_INSTANCE_GUID")
    + "-"
    + System.getenv("CF_INSTANCE_INDEX");

Scheduler:

@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "${migration.scheduler.delay:10000}")
public void runMigrationBatch() {

    List<Long> customerIds =
        migrationWorkRepository.claimNextBatch(workerId, 500);

    if (customerIds.isEmpty()) {
        return;
    }

    migrationService.processBatch(customerIds, workerId);
}

Notice that I used a small delay such as 10 seconds rather than waiting ten minutes. The job itself should regulate workload through batch size and throttling.

A ten-minute gap may make a one-million-record migration unnecessarily slow.

Claiming work safely

The conceptual transaction is:

1. Find 500 PENDING customers.
2. Mark them IN_PROGRESS and assign them to this worker.
3. Commit immediately.
4. Read customer data from DB2.
5. Insert or update MariaDB.
6. Verify successful write.
7. Mark the work item MIGRATED.

No two workers should successfully claim the same work item.

Depending on your DB2 version and supported locking syntax, implement this using:

Row locking with skip-locked semantics, when available
Atomic update and subsequent select by CLAIMED_BY
Pre-created non-overlapping ID ranges
Spring Batch partition metadata

A simple atomic-claim concept:

UPDATE MIGRATION_WORK_ITEM
SET
    STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS',
    CLAIMED_BY = :workerId,
    CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE CUSTOMER_ID IN
(
    SELECT CUSTOMER_ID
    FROM MIGRATION_WORK_ITEM
    WHERE STATUS = 'PENDING'
    ORDER BY CUSTOMER_ID
    FETCH FIRST 500 ROWS ONLY
);

Then:

SELECT CUSTOMER_ID
FROM MIGRATION_WORK_ITEM
WHERE STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND CLAIMED_BY = :workerId;

The exact DB2 SQL and isolation behaviour must be tested for your DB2 edition/version.

Better workflow for your case
Step 1: Snapshot the four million active DB2 records

Create:

CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_SNAPSHOT

Include a snapshot timestamp:

SNAPSHOT_TIME = T0
Step 2: Compare snapshot IDs against MariaDB

Do not issue one MariaDB query per customer.

Read DB2 IDs in batches—for example 2,000 or 5,000—and check MariaDB using:

SELECT CUSTOMER_ID
FROM CUSTOMER
WHERE CUSTOMER_ID IN (?, ?, ?, ...);

Then update the DB2 migration status:

Found in MariaDB     → ALREADY_MIGRATED
Not found in MariaDB → PENDING

This leaves approximately one million PENDING records without deleting the other three million.

Step 3: Migrate the pending one million

For every batch:

Claim 500–2,000 customers.
Read the full DB2 records.
Transform them using the existing migration service.
Bulk-write to MariaDB.
Mark completion.
Record failures separately.

Make the MariaDB write idempotent using a unique customer ID and an explicit duplicate strategy. MariaDB supports INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE; INSERT IGNORE also exists, but it can suppress more warnings than intended, so explicit upsert behaviour is generally clearer.

Example:

INSERT INTO CUSTOMER (
    CUSTOMER_ID,
    NAME,
    MOBILE_NUMBER,
    EMAIL,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT
)
VALUES (?, ?, ?, ?, ?)
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
    NAME = VALUES(NAME),
    MOBILE_NUMBER = VALUES(MOBILE_NUMBER),
    EMAIL = VALUES(EMAIL),
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT = VALUES(SOURCE_UPDATED_AT);
Step 4: Run delta migration

Records may be modified after snapshot time T0.

Query DB2:

SELECT *
FROM CUSTOMER
WHERE SOFT_DELETE = 0
  AND LAST_UPDATED_TIMESTAMP > :snapshotTime;

Apply those changes to MariaDB.

Depending on update volume, repeat delta migration until the difference is sufficiently small.

Step 5: Reconcile

Check at minimum:

DB2 active customer count
MariaDB expected customer count
Missing customer IDs
Unexpected MariaDB-only IDs
Duplicate customer IDs
Per-table child-record counts
Checksum/hash for critical fields
Failed migration count
In-progress records exceeding timeout

Do not rely only on total counts. Two systems can have equal counts but different customers.

Step 6: Stop or reduce on-demand migration

Once bulk migration and reconciliation are complete:

Keep on-demand migration temporarily as a safety net.
Log whenever it finds a DB2 customer absent from MariaDB.
Investigate any such cases.
Disable it only after the observation period.
Step 7: Archive/delete DB2 data separately

Deleting source DB2 records is a different production change from migration.

Recommended sequence:

Migrate
Validate
Business sign-off
Backup/archive
Retention period
Delete in controlled batches
Reorganize/statistics maintenance

Db2 DELETE removes qualifying rows and can cause significant logging, locking and table maintenance when applied to millions of rows.

If your immediate objective is merely to identify the missing one million, do not delete three million source or snapshot rows. Mark them instead.


How much time will it take?

An exact duration cannot be calculated from record count alone. It depends on:

Number and size of tables per customer
Average child records per customer
DB2 query latency
MariaDB write latency
Whether writes are individual or batched
Network latency between PCF, DB2 and MariaDB
DB connection pool sizes
Six-instance concurrency
DB2 and MariaDB CPU/IO capacity
Index availability
Transaction batch size
Rate limiting required to protect live traffic

The basic formula is:

Total time =
total customers / sustained customers processed per second

For one million missing customers:

Sustained combined rate	Approximate processing time
10 customers/sec	27.8 hours
25 customers/sec	11.1 hours
50 customers/sec	5.6 hours
100 customers/sec	2.8 hours
200 customers/sec	1.4 hours
500 customers/sec	33 minutes

These are processing-only estimates. Add time for:

Initial four-million-ID comparison
Retry processing
Delta migration
Reconciliation
Throttling during peak hours
Effect of a ten-minute scheduler

Suppose each PCF instance processes 1,000 customers once every ten minutes.

6 instances × 1,000 = 6,000 customers per ten minutes

For one million:

1,000,000 / 6,000 = 166.7 scheduler cycles
166.7 × 10 minutes = 1,667 minutes
                       ≈ 27.8 hours

Other examples:

Batch per instance every 10 min	Combined per cycle	One-million duration
500	3,000	55.6 hours
1,000	6,000	27.8 hours
2,000	12,000	13.9 hours
5,000	30,000	5.6 hours
10,000	60,000	2.8 hours

This assumes every batch finishes within ten minutes and there are no failures.

A better scheduler pattern is:

@Scheduled(fixedDelay = 5_000)

Each worker:

Claims a controlled batch.
Processes it.
Waits five seconds.
Claims the next batch.
Stops temporarily when the database load threshold is reached.
Exits immediately when no work remains.

You control throughput using batch size and active-worker count, rather than a ten-minute idle interval.

Recommended initial production configuration

Start conservatively:

PCF instances participating: 2
Claim batch size:             500 customers
DB2 read fetch size:          500–1,000
MariaDB write batch:          100–500
Scheduler fixed delay:        5–15 seconds
Maximum run duration:         5 minutes
Retry limit:                  3
Stale claim timeout:          15–30 minutes

Measure for one hour:

Customers processed per second
DB2 CPU and IO
MariaDB CPU and IO
DB connection usage
Lock waits
Average and p95 batch duration
Failure percentage
Live application response time

Then increase workers gradually:

2 instances → 3 → 4 → 6

Do not immediately use all six production instances at maximum speed.

Final recommendation

The best architecture is:

DB2 active data
      │
      ▼
Migration snapshot/control table
      │
      ├── Already in MariaDB → ALREADY_MIGRATED
      │
      └── Missing             → PENDING
                                     │
                       Six PCF workers claim
                       non-overlapping batches
                                     │
                                     ▼
                              MariaDB upsert
                                     │
                                     ▼
                        MIGRATED / FAILED status
                                     │
                                     ▼
                      Delta migration + reconciliation
                                     │
                                     ▼
                         Archive/delete DB2 later

So, yes to using the existing migration service and parallel PCF processing, but:

Do not use OFFSET.
Do not tie permanent ranges to PCF instance index.
Do not delete the already-migrated three million merely to find the remaining one million.
Use a durable work-control table with atomic claiming.
Make MariaDB writes idempotent.
Reconcile before any source deletion.
Estimate completion from an actual performance test of 10,000–50,000 customers; without that benchmark, any time estimate is only a throughput scenario.



Some more info - all the db2 calls (from Kony ) moved to Maria db (eapi) Some redisual calls there, seamles migration call still happening daily around 1500


That additional information simplifies the migration considerably.

Since normal Kony traffic has already moved from DB2 to MariaDB/EAPI, and only about **1,500 seamless migrations per day** still occur, DB2 is now mainly a legacy source rather than an actively used operational database.

## Revised understanding

| Item                        |   Approximate volume |
| --------------------------- | -------------------: |
| DB2 total records           |           15 million |
| DB2 active customers        |            4 million |
| Already migrated to MariaDB |            3 million |
| Remaining active customers  |     Around 1 million |
| Current seamless migrations | Around 1,500 per day |
| Normal application reads    | MariaDB through EAPI |
| Residual DB2 calls          |         Small number |

This means you do not need an overly complicated real-time distributed migration architecture.

## Recommended approach

### Phase 1: Confirm residual DB2 dependencies

Before bulk migration, identify every remaining DB2 usage:

```text
Kony residual calls
Seamless-login migration
Operational support queries
Batch jobs
Reports
Reconciliation processes
Any update/write calls
```

Classify each call:

| Category             | Action                         |
| -------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| Normal customer read | Move to EAPI/MariaDB           |
| Seamless migration   | Keep temporarily               |
| DB2 update/write     | Analyse carefully              |
| Reporting            | Redirect or retain temporarily |
| Unknown usage        | Add logging and trace it       |

This is important because once DB2 records are deleted or archived, even a small forgotten residual call can fail.

---

# Phase 2: Create an active-customer snapshot

Create a separate DB2 table for the four million active customers.

Prefer a migration snapshot containing the customer ID and migration-relevant fields:

```sql
CREATE TABLE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_SNAPSHOT AS
(
    SELECT
        CUSTOMER_ID,
        CUSTOMER_FIELD_1,
        CUSTOMER_FIELD_2,
        CUSTOMER_FIELD_3,
        LAST_UPDATED_TIMESTAMP,
        CURRENT TIMESTAMP AS SNAPSHOT_TIMESTAMP
    FROM CUSTOMER
    WHERE 1 = 0
)
WITH NO DATA;
```

Populate it:

```sql
INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_SNAPSHOT
(
    CUSTOMER_ID,
    CUSTOMER_FIELD_1,
    CUSTOMER_FIELD_2,
    CUSTOMER_FIELD_3,
    LAST_UPDATED_TIMESTAMP,
    SNAPSHOT_TIMESTAMP
)
SELECT
    CUSTOMER_ID,
    CUSTOMER_FIELD_1,
    CUSTOMER_FIELD_2,
    CUSTOMER_FIELD_3,
    LAST_UPDATED_TIMESTAMP,
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP
FROM CUSTOMER
WHERE SOFT_DELETE = 0;
```

Create an index:

```sql
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX IDX_MIGRATION_CUSTOMER_ID
ON CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_SNAPSHOT (CUSTOMER_ID);
```

Record the snapshot timestamp as `T0`.

---

# Phase 3: Do not delete the three million migrated rows immediately

Your proposed method was:

1. Read migrated customer IDs from MariaDB.
2. Delete corresponding records from the DB2 snapshot.
3. Whatever remains is the one million to migrate.

Technically this can work, but updating a status is safer than deleting.

Add a migration status:

```sql
ALTER TABLE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_SNAPSHOT
ADD COLUMN MIGRATION_STATUS VARCHAR(20)
DEFAULT 'PENDING';
```

Statuses:

```text
PENDING
ALREADY_MIGRATED
IN_PROGRESS
MIGRATED
FAILED
RECONCILED
```

For each batch of IDs found in MariaDB:

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_SNAPSHOT
SET MIGRATION_STATUS = 'ALREADY_MIGRATED'
WHERE CUSTOMER_ID IN (?, ?, ?, ...);
```

Then the remaining population is:

```sql
SELECT COUNT(*)
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_SNAPSHOT
WHERE MIGRATION_STATUS = 'PENDING';
```

Expected result: approximately one million.

### Why status is better than deletion

You retain:

* Audit evidence
* Restart support
* Migration counts
* Failure investigation
* Reconciliation information
* The ability to rerun comparison logic

Once the whole migration is complete, the entire snapshot table can be dropped.

---

# Phase 4: Continue seamless migration during bulk migration

The current 1,500 daily seamless migrations should continue.

This is useful because customers who log in during the bulk migration will migrate naturally.

Your bulk job must therefore be idempotent.

When a bulk worker reaches a customer, three possibilities exist:

```text
Customer is absent in MariaDB
    → Perform migration.

Customer already migrated through seamless login
    → Skip or update status as ALREADY_MIGRATED.

Customer migration is currently in progress
    → Use locking/idempotency to prevent duplicate processing.
```

The MariaDB customer ID should have a unique constraint.

Example:

```sql
ALTER TABLE CUSTOMER
ADD CONSTRAINT UK_CUSTOMER_ID UNIQUE (CUSTOMER_ID);
```

Migration logic should behave safely if the customer already exists.

---

# Phase 5: Six PCF instances

You have two reasonable choices.

## Option 1: Use only one PCF scheduler instance

Because only approximately one million customers remain and normal traffic has already moved away from DB2, a single controlled migration worker may be sufficient.

Configure only one instance to run the bulk scheduler:

```java
@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "${migration.scheduler.delay-ms:5000}")
public void migratePendingCustomers() {
    if (!migrationSchedulerEnabled) {
        return;
    }

    migrationService.processNextBatch(1000);
}
```

Enable it in only one PCF instance or deploy a separate migration application.

### Preferred deployment

```text
Existing EAPI application
    ├── 6 production instances
    └── Handles live user traffic

Separate migration worker application
    ├── 1 or 2 instances
    └── Handles bulk migration only
```

This is much safer than making all six customer-facing instances perform bulk migration.

Advantages:

* Easier control
* Easier rollback
* Independent scaling
* No impact on EAPI CPU/thread pools
* Can stop migration without restarting the main application
* Easier monitoring

## Option 2: Use all six PCF instances

Use this only when the single-worker throughput is insufficient.

All workers must use a database-backed claim mechanism. Do not assign records permanently using:

```text
CF_INSTANCE_INDEX 0 → offset 0
CF_INSTANCE_INDEX 1 → offset 1
...
```

That becomes unreliable during:

* Restarts
* Blue-green deployments
* Scaling
* Crashes
* Instance recreation

Use `CF_INSTANCE_GUID` or another worker identifier only for claiming and logging.

---

# Better partitioning without OFFSET

Do not use:

```sql
LIMIT 1000 OFFSET 500000;
```

Use one of these approaches.

## Approach A: Keyset pagination

```sql
SELECT CUSTOMER_ID
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_SNAPSHOT
WHERE MIGRATION_STATUS = 'PENDING'
  AND CUSTOMER_ID > :lastCustomerId
ORDER BY CUSTOMER_ID
FETCH FIRST 1000 ROWS ONLY;
```

This is suitable for a single worker.

## Approach B: Numeric ID ranges

If customer IDs are numeric and reasonably distributed:

```text
Worker 1: 1–1,000,000
Worker 2: 1,000,001–2,000,000
Worker 3: 2,000,001–3,000,000
...
```

Persist the ranges in a control table rather than deriving them dynamically from PCF instance indexes.

Example:

```sql
CREATE TABLE MIGRATION_PARTITION (
    PARTITION_ID INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
    START_CUSTOMER_ID BIGINT,
    END_CUSTOMER_ID BIGINT,
    STATUS VARCHAR(20),
    CLAIMED_BY VARCHAR(100),
    LAST_PROCESSED_ID BIGINT,
    PROCESSED_COUNT BIGINT,
    FAILED_COUNT BIGINT
);
```

## Approach C: Database work queue

Best for multiple workers:

```sql
CREATE TABLE MIGRATION_WORK_ITEM (
    CUSTOMER_ID BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    STATUS VARCHAR(20) DEFAULT 'PENDING',
    CLAIMED_BY VARCHAR(100),
    CLAIMED_AT TIMESTAMP,
    COMPLETED_AT TIMESTAMP,
    RETRY_COUNT INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
    ERROR_MESSAGE VARCHAR(1000)
);
```

For one million records, however, a work item for every customer may be unnecessary. Partition-level control is simpler and smaller.

---

# Recommended design for your situation

Because only around one million remain, I recommend:

```text
1 dedicated migration application
2 PCF instances maximum initially
Range-based partitions
Batch size: 500–2,000 customers
Continuous short-delay scheduler
MariaDB idempotent insert/update
Seamless migration continues in parallel
```

Architecture:

```text
DB2 migration snapshot
        │
        ▼
Migration partition table
        │
        ├── Partition 1
        ├── Partition 2
        ├── Partition 3
        └── ...
                │
        1–2 PCF migration workers
                │
                ▼
        Existing migration service
                │
                ▼
             MariaDB
```

The existing six EAPI instances should continue serving application traffic. They do not all need to run the migration scheduler.

---

# Handling the 1,500 daily seamless migrations

Suppose the bulk migration takes two days.

Approximately:

```text
1,500 × 2 = 3,000
```

customers may migrate through seamless login while the bulk job is running.

This is only 0.3% of the one-million remaining population.

Therefore, you do not need a complex delta solution. Before processing a customer, simply check whether it already exists in MariaDB.

Pseudo-code:

```java
public MigrationResult migrateCustomer(String customerId) {

    if (mariaDbRepository.existsByCustomerId(customerId)) {
        markAlreadyMigrated(customerId);
        return MigrationResult.ALREADY_MIGRATED;
    }

    CustomerData sourceData =
            db2Repository.loadCustomerData(customerId);

    if (sourceData == null) {
        markSourceNotFound(customerId);
        return MigrationResult.SOURCE_NOT_FOUND;
    }

    try {
        mariaDbMigrationRepository.upsertCustomer(sourceData);
        markMigrated(customerId);
        return MigrationResult.MIGRATED;
    } catch (Exception ex) {
        markFailed(customerId, ex);
        throw ex;
    }
}
```

Even better, make the final MariaDB operation atomic and rely on the unique customer ID rather than performing only an `exists` call.

The `exists` check reduces unnecessary work, but the unique constraint is the actual protection against a race condition.

---

# Scheduler timing

A ten-minute scheduler interval is unnecessarily slow for bulk migration.

Instead:

```java
@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "${migration.scheduler.delay-ms:5000}")
public void run() {
    migrationService.processBatch();
}
```

The scheduler:

1. Processes one batch.
2. Finishes.
3. Waits five seconds.
4. Takes the next batch.
5. Stops when no pending records remain.

Control the impact using:

* Batch size
* Maximum workers
* Connection pool limits
* Sleep/delay between batches
* Business-hours throttling

Example:

```java
public void processBatch() {

    Instant deadline = Instant.now().plus(Duration.ofMinutes(5));
    int processed = 0;
    int maximumPerRun = 5_000;

    while (processed < maximumPerRun &&
           Instant.now().isBefore(deadline)) {

        List<String> customerIds =
                partitionService.claimNextBatch(500);

        if (customerIds.isEmpty()) {
            break;
        }

        migrationService.migrate(customerIds);
        processed += customerIds.size();
    }
}
```

This scheduler execution exits after:

* 5,000 customers, or
* Five minutes, or
* No remaining work

It then starts again after the configured delay.

---

# Revised time estimate

## Bulk migration of one million

| Combined sustained rate | Approximate duration |
| ----------------------: | -------------------: |
|        10 customers/sec |           27.8 hours |
|        20 customers/sec |           13.9 hours |
|        30 customers/sec |            9.3 hours |
|        50 customers/sec |            5.6 hours |
|       100 customers/sec |            2.8 hours |

A realistic conservative starting expectation would be approximately:

```text
10–30 customers per second
≈ 9–28 hours
```

This depends heavily on how much associated data each customer has.

If each customer migration involves:

* Multiple DB2 tables
* Several MariaDB tables
* Transformation logic
* Individual inserts rather than batches
* Remote network calls

then 10–20 customers per second may be more realistic.

If it is mostly:

* One DB2 batch read
* Simple transformation
* Batched MariaDB writes

then 50–100 customers per second may be achievable.

## Existing seamless migration alone

At 1,500 customers per day:

```text
1,000,000 / 1,500 = approximately 667 days
```

That is around **1.8 years**, assuming the daily rate remains constant.

Therefore, bulk migration is justified.

---

# What to do with DB2 after migration

Since normal traffic already uses MariaDB, the safer retirement plan is:

```text
Bulk migrate remaining active customers
        ↓
Run reconciliation
        ↓
Observe residual DB2 calls for 2–4 weeks
        ↓
Remove seamless migration fallback
        ↓
Switch DB2 access to read-only
        ↓
Archive DB2 tables
        ↓
Delete/drop only after retention approval
```

I would not immediately delete the three million already-migrated active DB2 records.

First validate that:

* No residual Kony operation still reads them.
* All related child tables were migrated.
* Reconciliation is complete.
* Support teams no longer depend on DB2.
* Retention and audit requirements permit deletion.

## Final recommendation

Use the existing migration service, but deploy the bulk migration as a **separate PCF worker application**, initially with one or two instances.

Do not use six EAPI instances with `OFFSET/LIMIT`.

Use:

```text
DB2 snapshot of four million active customers
Status-based comparison against MariaDB
Approximately one million marked PENDING
Range-based or database-claimed batches
Idempotent MariaDB upsert
Existing seamless migration continues
Final reconciliation
DB2 retirement after observation period
```

With controlled batch processing, a reasonable planning estimate is **one to two days for the bulk run**, followed by reconciliation and an observation period. The actual run time should be calculated from a pilot migration of around 10,000–50,000 customers under production-like load.



Another info - deploying as a separate application - cloned from the existing one


That is the **right deployment model**. Since it is a separate application cloned from the existing migration application, you can reuse the proven migration logic while isolating bulk workload from live EAPI traffic.

## Recommended architecture

```text
Live EAPI application
├── 6 PCF instances
├── Handles Kony/MariaDB traffic
└── Performs seamless migration when required
             │
             │ Same migration logic
             ▼
Bulk Migration application
├── Cloned codebase or same build artifact
├── Separate PCF application name
├── Separate configuration/profile
├── 1–2 instances initially
├── No public API routes required
└── Scheduler performs bulk migration
             │
       DB2 → MariaDB
```

Do **not** automatically deploy six instances merely because the original application has six. Start with one bulk-migration instance and increase only after measuring DB impact.

## Avoid maintaining two diverging codebases

Instead of permanently copying the repository, preferably use:

* The same Git repository
* The same migration service classes
* A separate Spring profile
* A separate PCF manifest
* Scheduler enabled only for the bulk application

For example:

```yaml
# application.yml
migration:
  bulk:
    enabled: false
```

```yaml
# application-bulk.yml
migration:
  bulk:
    enabled: true
    batch-size: 500
    scheduler-delay-ms: 5000
    max-records-per-run: 5000
    max-run-minutes: 5
```

Scheduler:

```java
@Component
@ConditionalOnProperty(
    name = "migration.bulk.enabled",
    havingValue = "true"
)
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class BulkMigrationScheduler {

    private final BulkMigrationService migrationService;

    @Scheduled(
        fixedDelayString =
            "${migration.bulk.scheduler-delay-ms:5000}"
    )
    public void run() {
        migrationService.processPendingCustomers();
    }
}
```

This ensures the scheduler is completely absent from the normal EAPI application unless explicitly enabled.

## Separate PCF manifests

### Live EAPI

```yaml
applications:
  - name: customer-eapi
    instances: 6
    memory: 2G
    path: target/customer-service.jar
    env:
      SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE: production
      MIGRATION_BULK_ENABLED: false
```

### Bulk migration application

```yaml
applications:
  - name: customer-bulk-migration
    instances: 1
    memory: 2G
    path: target/customer-service.jar
    no-route: true
    health-check-type: process
    env:
      SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE: production,bulk
      MIGRATION_BULK_ENABLED: true
      MIGRATION_BATCH_SIZE: 500
      MIGRATION_SCHEDULER_DELAY_MS: 5000
```

Using the same artifact reduces the risk that live seamless migration and bulk migration behave differently.

## Important: separate resources and limits

Even though the code is cloned, configure separate:

* DB connection-pool limits
* Scheduler thread pool
* Logging category
* Metrics
* Application name
* PCF autoscaling policy
* Feature flag
* Batch size
* Retry settings

Example bulk-worker Hikari configuration:

```yaml
spring:
  datasource:
    db2:
      hikari:
        maximum-pool-size: 5
        minimum-idle: 1
    mariadb:
      hikari:
        maximum-pool-size: 5
        minimum-idle: 1
```

Do not copy the larger EAPI pool sizes unless testing proves they are safe. The migration application's pool size is one of the easiest ways to limit its DB impact.

## Recommended processing sequence

Because the bulk app already has both DB connections and existing migration logic:

### Stage 1 — Build snapshot/work population

Create the DB2 active snapshot:

```sql
INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_SNAPSHOT
(
    CUSTOMER_ID,
    MIGRATION_STATUS,
    CREATED_AT
)
SELECT
    CUSTOMER_ID,
    'PENDING',
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP
FROM CUSTOMER
WHERE SOFT_DELETE = 0;
```

You may initially store only IDs instead of copying the entire customer structure. When processing each customer, the existing migration service can read the latest full data from original DB2 tables.

This offers an advantage:

* Snapshot defines the migration population.
* Source data still comes from the current DB2 tables.
* Customers updated after snapshot are migrated using their latest data.

### Stage 2 — Determine whether already migrated

For each claimed customer:

```text
Check MariaDB using customer ID
        │
        ├── Exists → mark ALREADY_MIGRATED
        │
        └── Missing → call existing migration service
```

You do not necessarily need to pre-process all three million already-migrated customers before starting the remaining one million. A single-pass worker can check and decide customer by customer.

However, this means scanning all four million snapshot IDs.

A faster alternative is to build the pending population through bulk comparison, provided your infrastructure supports efficient comparison between the two databases.

## Recommended two-pass approach

### Pass 1: Reconciliation/classification

Process all four million IDs rapidly using only an existence check against MariaDB.

Use batches:

```text
Read 1,000–5,000 DB2 IDs
Query MariaDB for existing IDs using IN (...)
Mark:
    ALREADY_MIGRATED
    PENDING_MIGRATION
```

Avoid making four million individual MariaDB `existsByCustomerId()` calls.

### Pass 2: Full migration

Process only:

```sql
WHERE MIGRATION_STATUS = 'PENDING_MIGRATION'
```

Call the existing customer migration service for approximately one million customers.

This separation makes timing and monitoring much clearer.

## Scheduler and instance handling

For one instance, keyset pagination is sufficient. But design for possible second-instance scaling from the beginning.

Do not store only this in application memory:

```text
lastProcessedCustomerId
```

It would be lost on restart.

Use a partition table:

```sql
CREATE TABLE MIGRATION_PARTITION (
    PARTITION_ID INTEGER NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    START_CUSTOMER_ID BIGINT NOT NULL,
    END_CUSTOMER_ID BIGINT NOT NULL,
    LAST_PROCESSED_CUSTOMER_ID BIGINT,
    STATUS VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL,
    CLAIMED_BY VARCHAR(100),
    CLAIMED_AT TIMESTAMP,
    PROCESSED_COUNT BIGINT DEFAULT 0,
    SKIPPED_COUNT BIGINT DEFAULT 0,
    FAILED_COUNT BIGINT DEFAULT 0
);
```

Example partitions:

```text
Partition 1: customer ID 1–100,000
Partition 2: customer ID 100,001–200,000
...
```

The application instance claims a partition, processes it, and records its checkpoint.

This gives you:

* Safe restart
* Resume support
* Two-instance parallelism
* Progress reporting
* No `OFFSET`
* No dependency on PCF instance index

## Reusing existing migration service safely

The bulk scheduler should call the same business migration method used by seamless migration:

```java
public MigrationResult migrateCustomer(String customerId) {
    // Existing proven migration logic
}
```

Do not duplicate the internal migration implementation inside the scheduler.

Create orchestration around it:

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class BulkMigrationService {

    private final ExistingCustomerMigrationService customerMigrationService;
    private final MigrationControlRepository controlRepository;

    public BatchResult processBatch(List<String> customerIds) {

        BatchResult result = new BatchResult();

        for (String customerId : customerIds) {
            try {
                MigrationResult migrationResult =
                        customerMigrationService.migrateCustomer(customerId);

                controlRepository.markCompleted(
                        customerId,
                        migrationResult.name()
                );

                result.addSuccess(migrationResult);

            } catch (Exception exception) {
                controlRepository.markFailed(
                        customerId,
                        exception.getMessage()
                );

                result.addFailure();
            }
        }

        return result;
    }
}
```

## Seamless and bulk migration race

The live application may migrate a customer while the bulk application processes the same customer.

You must assume this will happen.

Example sequence:

```text
12:00:00 Bulk application checks MariaDB: absent
12:00:01 User logs in
12:00:02 Seamless migration inserts customer
12:00:03 Bulk application attempts insert
```

Protection must be at the MariaDB database level:

```sql
UNIQUE (CUSTOMER_ID)
```

Then the migration operation must be idempotent:

```sql
INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE ...
```

or it should safely interpret duplicate-key as already migrated.

Do not depend solely on:

```java
if (!exists(customerId)) {
    insert(customer);
}
```

That has a race window.

## Should the cloned bulk application have APIs?

Prefer `no-route: true` unless you need an operational endpoint.

You can still expose Actuator internally, depending on your PCF setup:

```yaml
management:
  endpoints:
    web:
      exposure:
        include: health,info,metrics,prometheus
```

Useful operational capabilities:

```text
Pause migration
Resume migration
Change batch size
View progress
Retry failed customers
Stop after current batch
```

These should preferably be controlled through configuration or an internal admin mechanism, not a publicly routed endpoint.

## Suggested starting configuration

| Setting                                   |         Initial value |
| ----------------------------------------- | --------------------: |
| Bulk app instances                        |                     1 |
| Classification batch                      |       2,000–5,000 IDs |
| Migration batch                           |     200–500 customers |
| Scheduler delay                           |             5 seconds |
| Maximum customers per scheduler execution |           2,000–5,000 |
| Maximum execution duration                |             5 minutes |
| DB2 pool                                  |                   3–5 |
| MariaDB pool                              |                   3–5 |
| Retry count                               |                     3 |
| Retry backoff                             | 30 sec, 2 min, 10 min |
| Failed-record handling                    |        Continue batch |
| Scale-up                                  | Maximum 2–3 initially |

## Revised time planning

There are two different workloads.

### Classifying four million customer IDs

If you compare IDs in batches and achieve:

| Classification rate | Time for 4 million |
| ------------------: | -----------------: |
|         500 IDs/sec |          2.2 hours |
|       1,000 IDs/sec |          1.1 hours |
|       2,000 IDs/sec |         33 minutes |
|       5,000 IDs/sec |         13 minutes |

This should be relatively quick because it only compares indexed customer IDs.

### Migrating the remaining one million

| Migration rate |       Time |
| -------------: | ---------: |
|         10/sec | 27.8 hours |
|         20/sec | 13.9 hours |
|         30/sec |  9.3 hours |
|         50/sec |  5.6 hours |

A safe planning window remains approximately **one to two days**, including retries and controlled throttling.

## Recommended final model

```text
Same repository / same JAR
        │
        ├── customer-eapi
        │     ├── 6 instances
        │     ├── bulk scheduler disabled
        │     └── seamless migration enabled
        │
        └── customer-bulk-migration
              ├── 1 instance initially
              ├── no route
              ├── bulk scheduler enabled
              ├── separate connection limits
              ├── durable checkpoints
              └── existing migration service reused
```

So cloning the existing application is appropriate, but avoid maintaining it as a permanently separate code copy. Ideally, keep one codebase and deploy the same artifact twice with different profiles and manifests.


I have only 6 hours window - non peak hours

With only a **6-hour non-peak window**, you should not perform all preparation, comparison, migration, reconciliation and cleanup inside that window.

The six-hour period should be used mainly for the **actual migration of the remaining customers**.

## Required throughput

For approximately one million customers:

```text
1,000,000 ÷ 6 hours
= 166,667 customers/hour
= 2,778 customers/minute
= approximately 46 customers/second
```

Because you need time for startup, failures, retries and final checks, design for at least:

```text
Target sustained rate: 60–75 customers/second
```

At 60 customers/second:

```text
1,000,000 ÷ 60 = 4.63 hours
```

At 75 customers/second:

```text
1,000,000 ÷ 75 = 3.7 hours
```

This leaves approximately 1–2 hours for retry and reconciliation.

## Do not include classification in the six-hour window

Before the migration night, complete these steps:

1. Create the DB2 snapshot/control table.
2. Compare the four million active IDs with MariaDB.
3. Mark approximately three million as already migrated.
4. Prepare approximately one million pending customer IDs.
5. Create indexes.
6. Divide pending customers into durable partitions.
7. Test migration throughput.
8. Confirm DB connection and PCF resource limits.

At the start of the six-hour window, the migration application should already have a ready queue like:

```text
Approximately 1,000,000 records
Status = PENDING
```

It should not spend the first hour discovering which customers need migration.

# Recommended PCF configuration

Since the bulk migration is a separate application, start with multiple controlled instances rather than only one.

A reasonable starting design:

| Setting                              | Initial recommendation |
| ------------------------------------ | ---------------------: |
| Bulk migration PCF instances         |                      3 |
| Required throughput per instance     |    20–25 customers/sec |
| Combined target                      |    60–75 customers/sec |
| Customer batch size                  |                250–500 |
| Scheduler delay                      |            0–2 seconds |
| Maximum batch duration               |            1–3 minutes |
| DB2 connection pool per instance     |                   5–10 |
| MariaDB connection pool per instance |                   5–10 |

Do not use a ten-minute scheduler interval. That wastes too much of the six-hour window.

Use continuous batch processing:

```java
@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "${migration.delay-ms:1000}")
public void migrate() {
    migrationService.processNextBatch();
}
```

Or better, run a controlled worker loop:

```java
public void processPendingCustomers() {

    while (migrationWindowService.isWindowOpen()) {

        List<String> customerIds =
                workClaimService.claimNextBatch(500);

        if (customerIds.isEmpty()) {
            break;
        }

        migrationService.migrateBatch(customerIds);
    }
}
```

The application stops claiming new work when:

* The six-hour window ends.
* No pending work remains.
* A DB performance threshold is exceeded.
* An operational pause flag is enabled.

It should finish its currently claimed small batch and exit cleanly.

# Partitioning for the six-hour window

Do not use:

```text
PCF instance index + OFFSET + LIMIT
```

Use durable partitions or atomic work claiming.

Example:

```text
Partition 1: 50,000 customer IDs
Partition 2: 50,000 customer IDs
...
Partition 20: 50,000 customer IDs
```

Each application instance claims the next available partition.

```text
Instance A → Partition 1
Instance B → Partition 2
Instance C → Partition 3
```

When an instance completes a partition, it claims another.

If one instance crashes, its partition can be released after a timeout and claimed by another instance.

Use a control table:

```sql
CREATE TABLE MIGRATION_PARTITION (
    PARTITION_ID INTEGER NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    START_CUSTOMER_ID BIGINT,
    END_CUSTOMER_ID BIGINT,
    STATUS VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL,
    CLAIMED_BY VARCHAR(100),
    CLAIMED_AT TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_PROCESSED_ID BIGINT,
    PROCESSED_COUNT BIGINT DEFAULT 0,
    FAILED_COUNT BIGINT DEFAULT 0
);
```

Possible statuses:

```text
PENDING
IN_PROGRESS
COMPLETED
FAILED
```

## Batch size versus partition size

These are different:

```text
Partition size: 25,000–50,000 customers
Processing batch: 250–500 customers
```

The partition provides durable ownership. The small processing batch limits transaction size and restart loss.

# Six-hour execution plan

## Before the window

Complete:

```text
DB2 snapshot/control population
MariaDB comparison
Pending customer identification
Partition creation
Indexes and statistics
Dry run
Performance benchmark
Rollback and pause procedure
```

## First 15 minutes

Start with two bulk instances.

Measure:

```text
Customers/second
DB2 CPU
DB2 IO and lock waits
MariaDB CPU
MariaDB write latency
PCF memory and CPU
EAPI response time
Error percentage
```

## After 15 minutes

If systems remain healthy:

```text
Scale from 2 to 3 instances
```

If throughput is still below approximately 60 customers/second and databases remain healthy:

```text
Scale to 4 instances
```

Do not start immediately with six instances without a benchmark.

## Final 30–60 minutes

Stop claiming new records before the six-hour boundary.

Use the remaining time for:

* Complete in-progress batches.
* Retry temporary failures.
* Count pending customers.
* Reconcile DB2 and MariaDB.
* Generate the migration summary.
* Leave permanent failures for investigation.

# Throughput scenarios

| Combined rate | Time for 1 million | Margin within 6 hours |
| ------------: | -----------------: | --------------------: |
|        40/sec |         6.94 hours |            Not enough |
|        46/sec |         6.04 hours |      No useful margin |
|        50/sec |         5.56 hours |            27 minutes |
|        60/sec |         4.63 hours |     1 hour 22 minutes |
|        75/sec |         3.70 hours |    2 hours 18 minutes |
|       100/sec |         2.78 hours |    3 hours 13 minutes |

Your minimum acceptance rate should therefore be approximately:

```text
60 customers/second combined
```

# Essential pilot test

Before scheduling the production migration, migrate a controlled sample such as 20,000 or 50,000 customers using the same infrastructure.

For example:

```text
50,000 customers completed in 15 minutes
= 55.6 customers/second
```

Projected time:

```text
1,000,000 ÷ 55.6
= approximately 5 hours
```

That is possible but leaves limited margin.

Another example:

```text
50,000 customers completed in 10 minutes
= 83.3 customers/second
```

Projected time:

```text
approximately 3 hours 20 minutes
```

That is comfortably within the window.

The pilot should include complete customer migration—not merely customer-ID reading.

# Important performance improvement

Do not process the customer with many individual DB calls where batching is possible.

Avoid a pattern like:

```text
For every customer:
    query DB2 table 1
    query DB2 table 2
    query DB2 table 3
    insert MariaDB table 1
    insert MariaDB table 2
    insert MariaDB table 3
```

For one million customers, even six calls per customer means six million database round trips.

Prefer:

```text
Read 250–500 customer IDs
Bulk-read related DB2 records using IN (...)
Group records by customer
Transform in memory
Batch-insert/upsert into MariaDB
Commit
```

This can make the difference between completing within six hours and missing the window.

# Handling concurrent seamless migration

The live application will still perform approximately 1,500 seamless migrations per day.

During six hours, approximately:

```text
1,500 ÷ 24 × 6
= about 375 customers
```

This volume is very small compared with the one-million bulk migration.

Continue seamless migration, but protect MariaDB with:

* Unique customer identifier.
* Idempotent insert/upsert.
* Duplicate processing treated as success or already migrated.

# What should not happen during this window

Do not perform DB2 source deletion during the same six-hour migration window.

The window should be:

```text
Read DB2
Write MariaDB
Update migration control status
Reconcile
```

DB2 deletion or archival should be a later controlled activity after validation and the observation period.

## Recommended final approach

```text
Before migration night:
    Prepare exact one-million pending population
    Create partitions
    Run performance pilot

During six-hour window:
    2 instances initially
    Scale to 3–4 based on DB health
    Target 60–75 customers/sec
    Process continuously in batches
    Stop new claims before window end
    Retry and reconcile

After window:
    Continue seamless fallback
    Resolve failures
    Observe residual DB2 calls
    Archive/delete DB2 separately
```

The migration is achievable in six hours only if the production-like pilot demonstrates at least **60 customers per second combined**. If the measured rate is below roughly 50 customers per second, one million customers will not complete safely within the window.






Another plan is to migrate only customers logged in within last 3 months 

( may be 3.3 million ) 300K may be need to migrate

To identify join table query to export 

Personalisation table with soft delete and last logon date time from customer table 

Peeisnalistaiontable composite key , one of the key is customer sys gen id - this is the join query criteria 


Yes, this revised plan is much safer for the six-hour window.

Migrating only customers who logged in during the last three months may reduce the remaining workload from approximately **1 million to around 300,000**.

## Expected throughput

To migrate 300,000 customers within six hours:

```text
300,000 ÷ 6 hours
= 50,000 customers/hour
= 833 customers/minute
= approximately 14 customers/second
```

Allowing time for retries and reconciliation, target:

```text
20–25 customers/second combined
```

At 20 customers/second:

```text
300,000 ÷ 20 = 4 hours 10 minutes
```

This leaves nearly two hours for retries, validation and controlled shutdown.

# Step 1: Identify eligible customers in DB2

Assuming:

### Customer table

```text
CUSTOMER
--------
CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
LAST_LOGON_DATETIME
```

### Personalisation table

```text
PERSONALISATION
---------------
CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
PERSONALISATION_TYPE
...other composite-key columns...
SOFT_DELETE
```

Your eligibility criteria are:

```text
Customer logged in within last 3 months
AND
Personalisation record is active: SOFT_DELETE = 0
```

## Query to find distinct eligible customer IDs

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT
       p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION p
INNER JOIN CUSTOMER c
        ON c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
WHERE p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= CURRENT TIMESTAMP - 3 MONTHS;
```

The `DISTINCT` is important because one customer may have multiple personalisation records due to the composite key.

Without `DISTINCT`, the same customer could appear multiple times.

---

# Better query using `EXISTS`

When you only need customer IDs from the customer table, `EXISTS` is usually clearer:

```sql
SELECT
       c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER c
WHERE c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= CURRENT TIMESTAMP - 3 MONTHS
  AND EXISTS
      (
          SELECT 1
          FROM PERSONALISATION p
          WHERE p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
            AND p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
      );
```

Advantages:

* Returns one row per customer naturally.
* Does not require `DISTINCT`.
* Avoids producing multiple joined rows before deduplication.
* Expresses the business rule clearly: customer must have at least one active personalisation row.

For identifying customers, I recommend the `EXISTS` version.

---

# If you need to export all personalisation records

Once eligible customers are identified, export the actual active personalisation records:

```sql
SELECT
       p.*
FROM PERSONALISATION p
INNER JOIN CUSTOMER c
        ON c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
WHERE p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= CURRENT TIMESTAMP - 3 MONTHS;
```

This may return more than 300,000 rows because one customer can have multiple personalisation records.

For example:

```text
300,000 eligible customers
Average 4 personalisation rows per customer
= 1.2 million personalisation rows
```

Therefore, estimate migration duration based on both:

* Number of customers
* Number of personalisation rows

---

# Create a stable export/snapshot table

Do not run the three-month query repeatedly while migration is in progress. The result can change because customers continue logging in.

Choose one fixed cutoff timestamp.

For example:

```text
Migration eligibility cutoff:
2026-07-30 21:00:00
```

Then calculate the start timestamp once:

```text
From:
2026-04-30 21:00:00

To:
2026-07-30 21:00:00
```

Use parameters rather than `CURRENT TIMESTAMP` during the actual migration preparation:

```sql
SELECT
       c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER c
WHERE c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= :threeMonthStartTimestamp
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME <  :migrationCutoffTimestamp
  AND EXISTS
      (
          SELECT 1
          FROM PERSONALISATION p
          WHERE p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
            AND p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
      );
```

This creates a repeatable population.

---

# Recommended migration candidate table

Create a small control table containing only customer IDs and processing status.

```sql
CREATE TABLE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_WORK
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID BIGINT NOT NULL,
    MIGRATION_STATUS VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL
        DEFAULT 'PENDING',
    CLAIMED_BY VARCHAR(100),
    CLAIMED_AT TIMESTAMP,
    COMPLETED_AT TIMESTAMP,
    RETRY_COUNT INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    ERROR_CODE VARCHAR(100),
    ERROR_MESSAGE VARCHAR(1000),
    CREATED_AT TIMESTAMP NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    PRIMARY KEY (CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID)
);
```

Populate it:

```sql
INSERT INTO PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_WORK
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    MIGRATION_STATUS
)
SELECT
       c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
       'PENDING'
FROM CUSTOMER c
WHERE c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= :threeMonthStartTimestamp
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME <  :migrationCutoffTimestamp
  AND EXISTS
      (
          SELECT 1
          FROM PERSONALISATION p
          WHERE p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
            AND p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
      );
```

Because `CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID` is the primary key, duplicate customer IDs cannot enter the work table.

---

# Already migrated customers

Your candidate list may contain customers already migrated through seamless migration.

You have two options.

## Option 1: Check during processing

For every claimed batch:

1. Read 500 candidate customer IDs.
2. Check which IDs already exist in MariaDB.
3. Mark those as `ALREADY_MIGRATED`.
4. Migrate only missing customers.

This is simple and supports the ongoing 1,500 daily seamless migrations.

## Option 2: Pre-classify before the six-hour window

Before migration night:

1. Export the approximately 300,000 eligible DB2 IDs.
2. Compare them against MariaDB.
3. Mark existing IDs as `ALREADY_MIGRATED`.
4. Leave only missing IDs as `PENDING`.

This is preferable because the six-hour window is then used only for real migration work.

---

# Indexes required

The performance of the identification query depends heavily on indexes.

## Customer table

The query filters by login timestamp and retrieves the generated customer ID.

Recommended index:

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_CUSTOMER_LAST_LOGON_ID
ON CUSTOMER
(
    LAST_LOGON_DATETIME,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
);
```

This is appropriate when the first filtering condition is the three-month login range.

## Personalisation table

Although `CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID` is part of the composite key, its position in that key matters.

For example, this composite key:

```text
(PERSONALISATION_TYPE, CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID)
```

may not efficiently support:

```sql
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
```

because `CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID` is not the leading column.

Recommended index:

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_PERSONALISATION_CUSTOMER_ACTIVE
ON PERSONALISATION
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    SOFT_DELETE
);
```

The customer ID should be the first column because that is the join criterion.

If the existing composite primary key begins with `CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID`, such as:

```text
(CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, PERSONALISATION_TYPE)
```

then DB2 may already be able to use it efficiently for the join. The separate index may still help with `SOFT_DELETE`, but verify using the DB2 access plan before adding it.

---

# Validate the population before migrating

Run these counts.

## Number of eligible customers

```sql
SELECT COUNT(*)
FROM CUSTOMER c
WHERE c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= :threeMonthStartTimestamp
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME <  :migrationCutoffTimestamp
  AND EXISTS
      (
          SELECT 1
          FROM PERSONALISATION p
          WHERE p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
            AND p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
      );
```

Expected:

```text
Approximately 3.3 million logged-in customers overall
Approximately 300,000 still missing from MariaDB
```

The DB2 query alone may return 3.3 million eligible customers. It will not automatically identify the missing 300,000 unless the MariaDB comparison is also performed.

## Number of personalisation records

```sql
SELECT COUNT(*)
FROM PERSONALISATION p
INNER JOIN CUSTOMER c
        ON c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
WHERE p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= :threeMonthStartTimestamp
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME <  :migrationCutoffTimestamp;
```

## Average personalisation rows per customer

```sql
SELECT
    DECIMAL(COUNT(*), 18, 2)
    /
    NULLIF(
        COUNT(DISTINCT p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID),
        0
    ) AS AVG_ROWS_PER_CUSTOMER
FROM PERSONALISATION p
INNER JOIN CUSTOMER c
        ON c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
WHERE p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= :threeMonthStartTimestamp
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME <  :migrationCutoffTimestamp;
```

This number is important for the timing estimate.

# Application processing flow

The separate cloned application should process the control table.

```text
DB2 migration work table
        │
        ▼
Claim 250–500 customer IDs
        │
        ▼
Bulk-check IDs in MariaDB
        │
        ├── Exists
        │      └── Mark ALREADY_MIGRATED
        │
        └── Missing
               │
               ▼
      Read active DB2 personalisation
               │
               ▼
       Existing migration service
               │
               ▼
       Insert/upsert into MariaDB
               │
               ▼
          Mark COMPLETED
```

## DB2 batch read

```sql
SELECT
       p.*
FROM PERSONALISATION p
WHERE p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
  AND p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN
      (
          ?, ?, ?, ?, ?
      )
ORDER BY
       p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID;
```

Avoid one DB2 query for every customer.

Instead:

```text
Claim 500 customer IDs
→ One or a few DB2 bulk reads
→ Group rows by customer in Java
→ Batch MariaDB writes
```
