# Scheduler setup

Since only approximately 300,000 customers may remain, begin with two PCF instances.

Suggested initial settings:

| Setting                          |      Starting value |
| -------------------------------- | ------------------: |
| Bulk application instances       |                   2 |
| Claimed customers per batch      |             250–500 |
| Scheduler delay                  |         1–5 seconds |
| Target combined rate             | 20–25 customers/sec |
| DB2 pool per instance            |                   5 |
| MariaDB pool per instance        |                   5 |
| Maximum retry                    |                   3 |
| Stop claiming before window ends |       30–45 minutes |

Do not use `OFFSET/LIMIT`.

Use database-backed claiming or fixed persisted partitions.

---

# Revised six-hour estimate

For 300,000 customers:

| Combined rate |     Migration time |
| ------------: | -----------------: |
|        10/sec | 8 hours 20 minutes |
|        14/sec | 5 hours 57 minutes |
|        20/sec | 4 hours 10 minutes |
|        25/sec | 3 hours 20 minutes |
|        30/sec | 2 hours 47 minutes |
|        50/sec |  1 hour 40 minutes |

Targeting only 14 customers/second is too risky because it provides no margin.

Your operational target should remain:

```text
20–25 customers per second
```

## Recommendation

This three-month-login approach is better for the six-hour window:

```text
Before the window
    Fix migration cutoff timestamp
    Identify three-month active customers
    Compare with MariaDB
    Create exact pending list
    Verify indexes
    Run pilot

During the window
    Process approximately 300,000 missing customers
    Use 2 instances initially
    Target 20–25 customers/sec
    Continue seamless migration safely
    Stop new claims 30–45 minutes before closing

After the window
    Retry failures
    Reconcile counts and IDs
    Keep the remaining older customers in DB2
```

The most important query design point is: use `CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID` as the join column, but ensure it is the **leading column of an index on the personalisation table**.



I have env variable which provide DCE, DCW.. also env variable CF_INSTANCE_INDEX.. can build anything to split / avoid overalping / race condition

Yes. You can combine:

* `DCE` / `DCW` to separate data centres
* `CF_INSTANCE_INDEX` to divide work within each data centre
* A stable hash of `CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID` to assign each customer to exactly one worker

But **environment-variable partitioning alone does not fully prevent race conditions**. You should combine deterministic partitioning with MariaDB idempotency and a small migration-control table.

Cloud Foundry provides `CF_INSTANCE_INDEX` and `CF_INSTANCE_GUID` for each running app instance. The index identifies the instance within the app, while the GUID is better for logging and temporary worker identity. ([Cloud Foundry Docs][1])

# Recommended partition formula

Assume:

```text
DCE = data-centre number 0
DCW = data-centre number 1

3 bulk application instances per data centre
CF_INSTANCE_INDEX = 0, 1, 2
```

Total workers:

```text
2 DCs × 3 instances = 6 workers
```

Calculate a global worker number:

```text
DCE instance 0 → worker 0
DCE instance 1 → worker 1
DCE instance 2 → worker 2

DCW instance 0 → worker 3
DCW instance 1 → worker 4
DCW instance 2 → worker 5
```

Formula:

```java
globalWorkerId =
        (dataCentreIndex * instancesPerDataCentre)
        + cfInstanceIndex;
```

Then assign customers using:

```text
MOD(hash(customerSysGenId), totalWorkers) = globalWorkerId
```

Each customer is assigned to exactly one worker.

---

# Example configuration

## DCE

```yaml
MIGRATION_DC_INDEX: 0
MIGRATION_DC_COUNT: 2
MIGRATION_INSTANCES_PER_DC: 3
```

## DCW

```yaml
MIGRATION_DC_INDEX: 1
MIGRATION_DC_COUNT: 2
MIGRATION_INSTANCES_PER_DC: 3
```

Each instance gets `CF_INSTANCE_INDEX` automatically.

Java configuration:

```java
@Component
@Getter
public class WorkerPartitionConfiguration {

    private final int dcIndex;
    private final int dcCount;
    private final int instancesPerDc;
    private final int instanceIndex;
    private final int totalWorkers;
    private final int globalWorkerId;

    public WorkerPartitionConfiguration(
            @Value("${MIGRATION_DC_INDEX}") int dcIndex,
            @Value("${MIGRATION_DC_COUNT}") int dcCount,
            @Value("${MIGRATION_INSTANCES_PER_DC}") int instancesPerDc,
            @Value("${CF_INSTANCE_INDEX:0}") int instanceIndex) {

        this.dcIndex = dcIndex;
        this.dcCount = dcCount;
        this.instancesPerDc = instancesPerDc;
        this.instanceIndex = instanceIndex;

        this.totalWorkers = dcCount * instancesPerDc;

        this.globalWorkerId =
                dcIndex * instancesPerDc + instanceIndex;
    }
}
```

For two data centres and three instances each:

```text
totalWorkers = 2 × 3 = 6
```

---

# DB2 query using modulo partitioning

When `CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID` is numeric:

```sql
SELECT
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_WORK
WHERE MIGRATION_STATUS = 'PENDING'
  AND MOD(CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, :totalWorkers) = :globalWorkerId
ORDER BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FETCH FIRST 500 ROWS ONLY;
```

Example for DCE instance index 1:

```text
DC index       = 0
Instance index = 1
Global worker  = 1
```

Query condition:

```sql
MOD(CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, 6) = 1
```

Example for DCW instance index 1:

```text
DC index       = 1
Instance index = 1
Global worker  = 4
```

Query condition:

```sql
MOD(CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, 6) = 4
```

There is no overlap between workers 1 and 4.

## If customer ID is a string

Use a stable database hash or create a numeric partition number while building the work table.

The safer option is to calculate it once:

```sql
ALTER TABLE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_WORK
ADD COLUMN PARTITION_ID SMALLINT;
```

During population:

```sql
INSERT INTO PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_WORK
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PARTITION_ID,
    MIGRATION_STATUS
)
SELECT
    c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    MOD(c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, 6),
    'PENDING'
FROM CUSTOMER c
WHERE ...
```

Then each worker queries:

```sql
SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_WORK
WHERE PARTITION_ID = :globalWorkerId
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'PENDING'
ORDER BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FETCH FIRST 500 ROWS ONLY;
```

This is cleaner and more predictable than recalculating a hash on every query.

# Important limitation: scaling must remain fixed

This design assumes:

```text
2 data centres
3 instances per data centre
6 total workers
```

Do not change the instance count while the migration is running.

For example, changing from three to four instances per DC changes:

```text
MOD(customerId, 6)
```

to:

```text
MOD(customerId, 8)
```

Customers will move between partitions, creating duplicate work or gaps.

Therefore, set an explicit fixed value:

```yaml
MIGRATION_TOTAL_PARTITIONS: 6
```

Do not dynamically calculate the partition count from the current number of running PCF instances.

You can run fewer than six workers temporarily, but the missing partition will then remain unprocessed until its worker returns.

---

# Race condition with seamless migration

Deterministic partitioning prevents overlap **between bulk workers**.

It does not prevent overlap between:

```text
Bulk migration application
and
Live seamless migration application
```

Example:

```text
Bulk worker reads customer as missing
User logs in at the same time
Seamless migration inserts customer
Bulk worker then tries to insert the same customer
```

You still need a unique key and idempotent migration logic in MariaDB.

```sql
ALTER TABLE PERSONALISATION
ADD CONSTRAINT UK_PERSONALISATION
UNIQUE
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    OTHER_COMPOSITE_KEY
);
```

Use:

```sql
INSERT INTO PERSONALISATION (...)
VALUES (...)
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
    VALUE_COLUMN = VALUES(VALUE_COLUMN),
    UPDATED_AT = VALUES(UPDATED_AT);
```

Or treat duplicate-key as:

```text
ALREADY_MIGRATED / SUCCESS
```

The database unique constraint is the final protection.

---

# Add status claiming inside each partition

Even with non-overlapping partitions, a PCF instance can restart while processing a batch.

For example:

```text
Worker 2 reads 500 records
Processes 250
PCF restarts
Worker 2 reads the same 500 again
```

That is acceptable only if every migration is idempotent.

A better design is to claim records.

## Work table

```sql
CREATE TABLE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_WORK
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID BIGINT NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    PARTITION_ID SMALLINT NOT NULL,
    MIGRATION_STATUS VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL DEFAULT 'PENDING',
    CLAIMED_BY VARCHAR(150),
    CLAIMED_AT TIMESTAMP,
    COMPLETED_AT TIMESTAMP,
    RETRY_COUNT INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    ERROR_MESSAGE VARCHAR(1000)
);
```

Worker identity:

```java
String workerName =
        environment + "-"
        + dataCentre + "-"
        + instanceIndex + "-"
        + instanceGuid;
```

Example:

```text
PROD-DCE-1-2f4c91...
```

Use:

* `PARTITION_ID` for permanent ownership
* `CF_INSTANCE_GUID` for temporary claim ownership
* `CF_INSTANCE_INDEX` for partition calculation

---

# Processing algorithm

```java
@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "${migration.delay-ms:1000}")
public void migrate() {

    int partitionId = workerConfig.getGlobalWorkerId();

    List<Long> customerIds =
            workRepository.claimNextBatch(
                    partitionId,
                    workerId,
                    500
            );

    if (customerIds.isEmpty()) {
        return;
    }

    for (Long customerId : customerIds) {
        migrateOneCustomer(customerId);
    }
}
```

Conceptual claim:

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_WORK
SET MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS',
    CLAIMED_BY = :workerId,
    CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN
(
    SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
    FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_WORK
    WHERE PARTITION_ID = :partitionId
      AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'PENDING'
    ORDER BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
    FETCH FIRST 500 ROWS ONLY
);
```

Then read:

```sql
SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_WORK
WHERE CLAIMED_BY = :workerId
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

The claim update should run in a short transaction and commit before the actual migration starts.

# Handling a crashed instance

Reset stale work:

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_WORK
SET MIGRATION_STATUS = 'PENDING',
    CLAIMED_BY = NULL,
    CLAIMED_AT = NULL,
    RETRY_COUNT = RETRY_COUNT + 1
WHERE MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND CLAIMED_AT < CURRENT TIMESTAMP - 20 MINUTES;
```

This allows the restarted instance for the same partition to resume the records.

Because MariaDB writes are idempotent, retrying a partially migrated customer remains safe.

---

# DC failure concern

Suppose DCW goes down.

With fixed ownership:

```text
DCE owns partitions 0, 1, 2
DCW owns partitions 3, 4, 5
```

DCE will not process DCW partitions automatically.

For a six-hour window, choose one of these models.

## Model A — Fixed DC ownership

Simpler:

```text
DCE → partitions 0,1,2
DCW → partitions 3,4,5
```

Use when both DCs are expected to remain available.

## Model B — Claim any partition

Better resiliency:

```text
All instances can claim any unclaimed partition
```

The DC and instance variables identify the worker, but do not determine ownership.

A partition-control table decides ownership:

```sql
CREATE TABLE MIGRATION_PARTITION
(
    PARTITION_ID SMALLINT PRIMARY KEY,
    STATUS VARCHAR(20),
    CLAIMED_BY VARCHAR(150),
    CLAIMED_AT TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT TIMESTAMP
);
```

A DCE worker can take over a stale DCW partition.

This is more robust but slightly more complex.

# My recommendation for your six-hour window

Use a hybrid design:

```text
12 or 24 permanent logical partitions
not 6 partitions
```

For example:

```text
PARTITION_ID = MOD(CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, 24)
```

Then six workers dynamically claim logical partitions:

```text
DCE-0 → partition 0
DCE-1 → partition 1
DCE-2 → partition 2
DCW-0 → partition 3
DCW-1 → partition 4
DCW-2 → partition 5
```

When completed, each claims another partition.

Benefits:

* Better workload balancing
* One slow partition does not delay an entire worker
* A failed worker’s partition can be taken over
* Instance scaling does not change customer-to-partition mapping
* No `OFFSET`
* No overlap

Use more logical partitions than workers:

```text
6 workers
24–60 logical partitions
```

For approximately 300,000 customers:

```text
24 partitions ≈ 12,500 customers per partition
60 partitions ≈ 5,000 customers per partition
```

I recommend approximately:

```text
60 logical partitions
6 PCF workers
≈ 5,000 candidate customers per partition
```

The partition formula stays permanently:

```sql
MOD(CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, 60)
```

The six instances claim these 60 partitions dynamically.

# Final architecture

```text
DCE / DCW
    │
    ├── CF_INSTANCE_INDEX
    └── CF_INSTANCE_GUID
             │
             ▼
Unique worker identity
             │
             ▼
Claim one of 60 logical partitions
             │
             ▼
Claim 250–500 records in that partition
             │
             ▼
Check MariaDB / run existing migration
             │
             ▼
Idempotent MariaDB upsert
             │
             ▼
Mark COMPLETED / FAILED
```

So yes, you can use your environment variables, but the strongest design is:

```text
DCE/DCW + CF_INSTANCE_INDEX
    → worker identity

MOD(customer ID, fixed logical partition count)
    → stable data partition

Database partition claiming
    → restart/DC failure protection

MariaDB unique key and upsert
    → seamless-migration race protection
```

Spring’s `@Scheduled` runs in every deployed application instance unless you explicitly coordinate it, so the coordination must be handled by your partitioning or distributed-lock strategy. ([Home][2])

[1]: https://docs.cloudfoundry.org/devguide/deploy-apps/environment-variable.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Cloud Foundry environment variables"
[2]: https://docs.spring.io/spring-framework/reference/integration/scheduling.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Task Execution and Scheduling"




Plan is to start 12 Allowed only 12-6 (so configuration of scheduler will be like that ) will run scheduler every 5 mins for populating the distinct cust sys gen id and status as ONLINE Need to maintain table to offset and limit so other instance will be pickup next offset + limit with start date time limit will be 100K ( not sure will complete within 5 mins ) End of completion update end date time ,!record processed or inserted From 2-5 another Scheduler every 10 mins to migrate the non migrated data and add entry to the distinct cust sys gen id as MIGRATED and failure update as failure Will this plan work ? any improvement needed ? Pros and Cons ?


The plan is workable in principle, but I would change three important parts:

1. Do not coordinate 12 instances using shared `OFFSET + LIMIT`.
2. Do not assume a 100,000-record batch will finish within five minutes.
3. Do not wait for a five- or ten-minute scheduler interval after each batch.

Use fixed chunks or database claiming, durable status, and continuous processing inside the permitted time window.

# Your proposed timeline

```text
12:00 AM–2:00 AM
Populate distinct CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID records
Status = ONLINE

2:00 AM–5:00 AM
Migrate records not yet migrated
Status = MIGRATED or FAILED

5:00 AM–6:00 AM
Validation, retries and shutdown
```

This separation is good. It leaves the last hour for reconciliation and recovery.

However, the migration phase is only **three hours**, not six hours.

For approximately 300,000 customers:

```text
300,000 ÷ 3 hours
= 100,000/hour
= 1,667/minute
= 27.8 customers/second
```

With retries and reconciliation, you should target at least:

```text
35–40 customers/second combined
```

Across 12 instances:

```text
Approximately 3–4 customers/second per instance
```

That may be achievable, but only a production-like pilot can confirm it.

---

# Problem with `OFFSET + LIMIT`

Suppose the common control row contains:

```text
Current offset = 0
Limit = 100,000
```

Two instances may do this:

```text
Instance 1 reads offset 0
Instance 2 reads offset 0
Instance 1 updates offset to 100,000
Instance 2 also updates offset to 100,000
```

Both process the same range.

Even with locking, offset pagination remains problematic because rows can move as statuses change:

```sql
SELECT ...
FROM candidate_table
WHERE status = 'ONLINE'
ORDER BY customer_id
OFFSET 100000 ROWS
FETCH NEXT 100000 ROWS ONLY;
```

After the first 100,000 rows become `MIGRATED`, the remaining result set shrinks. The next offset can skip customers.

Therefore, do not combine:

```text
OFFSET
+
status-changing result set
+
multiple PCF instances
```

That is the highest-risk part of the design.

# Better option: fixed chunk table

Create explicit chunks before processing.

## Batch-control table

```sql
CREATE TABLE MIGRATION_BATCH_CONTROL
(
    BATCH_ID             BIGINT NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    PHASE                VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL,
    START_CUSTOMER_ID    BIGINT,
    END_CUSTOMER_ID      BIGINT,
    STATUS               VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL,
    CLAIMED_BY           VARCHAR(150),
    CLAIMED_AT           TIMESTAMP,
    STARTED_AT           TIMESTAMP,
    COMPLETED_AT         TIMESTAMP,
    EXPECTED_COUNT       INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
    PROCESSED_COUNT      INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
    INSERTED_COUNT       INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
    ALREADY_EXISTS_COUNT INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
    FAILED_COUNT         INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
    LAST_PROCESSED_ID    BIGINT,
    ERROR_MESSAGE        VARCHAR(1000)
);
```

Possible statuses:

```text
PENDING
CLAIMED
IN_PROGRESS
COMPLETED
PARTIAL
FAILED
```

Each of the 12 instances atomically claims one `PENDING` batch.

After finishing, it claims the next batch immediately.

---

# Better candidate table

```sql
CREATE TABLE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_CANDIDATE
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID BIGINT NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,
    MIGRATION_STATUS    VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL,
    BATCH_ID            BIGINT,
    CLAIMED_BY          VARCHAR(150),
    CLAIMED_AT          TIMESTAMP,
    STARTED_AT          TIMESTAMP,
    COMPLETED_AT        TIMESTAMP,
    RETRY_COUNT         INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
    ERROR_CODE          VARCHAR(100),
    ERROR_MESSAGE       VARCHAR(1000),
    CREATED_AT          TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP
);
```

Recommended statuses:

```text
ONLINE
IN_PROGRESS
MIGRATED
ALREADY_MIGRATED
FAILED
```

`ONLINE` is understandable, although `PENDING` or `ELIGIBLE` would be clearer operationally.

I would use:

```text
ELIGIBLE
IN_PROGRESS
MIGRATED
ALREADY_MIGRATED
FAILED
```

---

# Phase 1: 12:00 AM–2:00 AM candidate preparation

Instead of every application instance running the same distinct join query, run candidate population through one controlled coordinator or one database statement.

## Preferred approach

```sql
INSERT INTO PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_CANDIDATE
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    MIGRATION_STATUS,
    CREATED_AT
)
SELECT
    c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    'ELIGIBLE',
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP
FROM CUSTOMER c
WHERE c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= :start_timestamp
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME < :cutoff_timestamp
  AND EXISTS
  (
      SELECT 1
      FROM PERSONALISATION p
      WHERE p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
        AND p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
  );
```

Because `CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID` is the candidate-table primary key, duplicates are automatically prevented.

Depending on DB2 syntax and restart requirements, use an equivalent `MERGE` so rerunning the population is safe.

## Why one bulk statement is preferable

It avoids:

* Twelve instances repeating the same join
* Offset coordination
* Duplicate candidate insertion
* Inconsistent cut-off timestamps
* Repeated scans of the same large tables

If one database statement is too heavy, divide the source into **fixed ID ranges**, not offsets.

Example:

```text
Batch 1: customer ID 1–250,000
Batch 2: customer ID 250,001–500,000
...
```

Each range is immutable, so status updates do not move rows between pages.

---

# Do not use 100,000 as the processing transaction

A batch of 100,000 can be used as a high-level partition, but not as one transaction or one in-memory list.

Use two levels:

```text
Logical partition: 10,000–25,000 customers
Processing batch: 250–1,000 customers
```

For example:

```text
300,000 customers
÷ 24 logical partitions
≈ 12,500 customers per partition
```

With 12 instances:

```text
Each instance starts with one partition
Then claims another when complete
```

Within each partition:

```text
Read 500 customers
Migrate them
Commit
Update checkpoint
Read next 500
```

This gives:

* Faster restart
* Smaller transactions
* Lower memory use
* Lower DB lock/log pressure
* Better workload balancing

A 100,000-customer assignment to one instance can become a long-running hotspot. If that instance crashes after 80,000 records, recovery is much harder.

---

# Scheduler behaviour

## Candidate scheduler

You proposed running it every five minutes.

That is acceptable only as a watchdog, but not as the main pagination mechanism.

Better:

```text
12:00 AM:
Start candidate population once.

Until 2:00 AM:
If population is incomplete, workers continuously claim the next range.

Every 5 minutes:
Watchdog checks for stale/failed batches.
```

## Migration scheduler

You proposed every ten minutes from 2:00 AM to 5:00 AM.

A ten-minute delay is too long.

Suppose each instance processes a batch in two minutes:

```text
2 minutes work
10 minutes waiting
```

Most of the migration window is wasted.

Use one of these patterns:

### Continuous worker loop

```java
while (migrationWindowOpen()) {
    Batch batch = claimNextBatch();

    if (batch == null) {
        sleep(5_000);
        continue;
    }

    processBatch(batch);
}
```

### Short fixed delay

```java
@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "${migration.delay-ms:1000}")
public void runMigration() {
    if (!migrationWindowOpen()) {
        return;
    }

    migrationService.processNextBatch();
}
```

The scheduler fires again one second after the preceding execution completes.

Use the ten-minute scheduler only for:

* Retry of failed batches
* Releasing stale claims
* Progress reporting
* Operational monitoring

---

# Atomic claim is required

Each worker must claim work in one short database transaction.

Conceptually:

```sql
UPDATE MIGRATION_BATCH_CONTROL
SET STATUS = 'CLAIMED',
    CLAIMED_BY = :worker_id,
    CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE BATCH_ID =
(
    SELECT BATCH_ID
    FROM MIGRATION_BATCH_CONTROL
    WHERE PHASE = 'MIGRATION'
      AND STATUS = 'PENDING'
    ORDER BY BATCH_ID
    FETCH FIRST 1 ROW ONLY
);
```

The exact implementation depends on DB2 locking syntax. The key requirement is:

```text
Select and claim must be atomic
```

Worker identity:

```text
DCE/DCW
+ CF_INSTANCE_INDEX
+ CF_INSTANCE_GUID
```

Example:

```text
DCE-3-a8f63...
DCW-5-b91c2...
```

Use:

* `CF_INSTANCE_INDEX` for readable worker numbering
* `CF_INSTANCE_GUID` to distinguish a restarted/recreated instance

---

# Recovery from instance failure

Suppose an instance claims a batch at 2:10 AM and crashes.

A watchdog should release stale work:

```sql
UPDATE MIGRATION_BATCH_CONTROL
SET STATUS = 'PENDING',
    CLAIMED_BY = NULL,
    CLAIMED_AT = NULL
WHERE STATUS IN ('CLAIMED', 'IN_PROGRESS')
  AND CLAIMED_AT < CURRENT TIMESTAMP - 15 MINUTES;
```

But do not reset an active long-running batch merely because it exceeds 15 minutes.

Add a heartbeat:

```text
LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT
```

The worker updates it after each 500-record sub-batch.

Release only when the heartbeat is stale.

---

# Per-customer status handling

Processing flow:

```text
ELIGIBLE
    ↓
IN_PROGRESS
    ├── MariaDB already contains customer
    │       → ALREADY_MIGRATED
    │
    ├── Migration successful
    │       → MIGRATED
    │
    └── Migration failed
            → FAILED
```

Do not add a second candidate row to record `MIGRATED`. Update the existing row.

For example:

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_CANDIDATE
SET MIGRATION_STATUS = 'MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    CLAIMED_BY = :worker_id
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = :customer_id;
```

Your candidate table should have exactly one row per customer.

---

# Race with live seamless migration

Your batch-control design prevents overlap between the 12 bulk workers.

It does not prevent this:

```text
Bulk worker processing customer 123
At the same time customer 123 logs in
Live application performs seamless migration
```

Protect MariaDB using:

* Unique composite key
* Idempotent migration logic
* Upsert or duplicate-key-as-success handling

The final operation should classify the outcome as:

```text
MIGRATED
or
ALREADY_MIGRATED
```

A duplicate-key race should not become a permanent failure.

---

# Processing counts to maintain

Your batch table should maintain at least:

| Column                 | Meaning                         |
| ---------------------- | ------------------------------- |
| `EXPECTED_COUNT`       | Customers assigned to the batch |
| `PROCESSED_COUNT`      | Total attempted                 |
| `INSERTED_COUNT`       | Newly migrated                  |
| `ALREADY_EXISTS_COUNT` | Found in MariaDB                |
| `FAILED_COUNT`         | Failed after processing         |
| `STARTED_AT`           | Actual work start               |
| `COMPLETED_AT`         | Actual finish                   |
| `LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT`    | Worker is still alive           |
| `LAST_PROCESSED_ID`    | Restart checkpoint              |

Validation:

```text
PROCESSED_COUNT
=
INSERTED_COUNT
+ ALREADY_EXISTS_COUNT
+ FAILED_COUNT
```

And at completion:

```text
EXPECTED_COUNT = PROCESSED_COUNT
```

---

# Revised timeline

## 12:00 AM–12:15 AM

* Fix the source cutoff timestamp.
* Start candidate population.
* Begin with controlled parallelism.
* Monitor DB2 query and log impact.

## 12:15 AM–1:30 AM

* Populate candidate rows.
* Create logical migration partitions.
* Pre-check MariaDB existence if possible.
* Confirm candidate counts.

## 1:30 AM–2:00 AM

* Complete candidate reconciliation.
* Confirm no duplicate IDs.
* Verify pending count.
* Prepare migration workers.

## 2:00 AM–4:30 AM

* Twelve workers continuously process claimed batches.
* No ten-minute waiting between successful batches.
* Watchdog handles stale claims every five or ten minutes.

## 4:30 AM–5:00 AM

* Stop claiming large new partitions.
* Finish current small batches.
* Retry transient failures.

## 5:00 AM–6:00 AM

* Reconcile counts.
* Produce missing/failed customer list.
* Reset incomplete claims safely.
* Stop application or disable migration flag.

---

# Pros of your approach

The strong parts are:

* Separate candidate-identification and migration phases
* Fixed execution window
* Durable customer-level statuses
* Start/end timestamps
* Counts for processed and inserted records
* Twelve-instance parallelism
* Dedicated time for reconciliation
* Existing migration service reuse

# Cons and risks

| Risk                                  | Impact                              | Improvement                                        |
| ------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| Shared offset race                    | Duplicate or skipped ranges         | Use fixed ranges or atomic claims                  |
| Status changes with offset pagination | Rows shift and get skipped          | Never offset over a changing result                |
| 100K batch                            | Long transaction/restart cost       | Use 10K–25K partitions and 250–1,000 sub-batches   |
| Five-/ten-minute delays               | Wastes migration window             | Continuous processing with short delay             |
| Twelve simultaneous join queries      | DB2 load spike                      | Single bulk population or controlled range workers |
| No heartbeat                          | Healthy slow batch may be reclaimed | Store `LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT`                          |
| Seamless migration race               | Duplicate insert/failure            | Unique key and idempotent upsert                   |
| Fixed 2–5 migration period            | Only three hours                    | Target at least 35–40 customers/sec                |
| CF instance restart                   | Same index, different process       | Include `CF_INSTANCE_GUID` in claims               |
| One large partition per instance      | Uneven completion                   | More logical partitions than workers               |

# Recommended final design

```text
12 PCF instances
        │
        ▼
Atomic claim of fixed logical partition
        │
        ▼
Process 250–1,000 customers
        │
        ▼
Commit and update checkpoint/heartbeat
        │
        ▼
Continue immediately
        │
        ▼
Claim next partition
```

Recommended sizes for approximately 300,000 candidates:

```text
Logical partitions: 48–120
Customers per partition: 2,500–6,250
Sub-batch size: 250–500
Workers: 12
```

A practical starting configuration:

```yaml
migration:
  candidate:
    start-time: "00:00"
    stop-time: "02:00"
    logical-partitions: 60
    fetch-size: 1000

  execution:
    start-time: "02:00"
    stop-claim-time: "04:30"
    hard-stop-time: "05:00"
    customer-batch-size: 500
    scheduler-delay-ms: 1000

  recovery:
    heartbeat-seconds: 60
    stale-claim-minutes: 10
    max-retries: 3
```

The plan will work after replacing **offset/limit coordination** with **fixed partitions and atomic database claiming**. That change is the main difference between a restart-safe migration and one that can silently skip or duplicate customers.



Batch-control table - how claim , how update status not clear Also BAU is DB2 and new application is Maria db Apart from scheduler will have seperate endpoints to trigger same as scheduler for fall back with input on limit , offset this can be accessed too if needed Can provide full summary and with code for those implementation to understand more


Yes. With the clarification that **DB2 remains the BAU/source database** and **MariaDB is the target for the new application**, I recommend keeping the migration-control tables in **MariaDB**, not DB2.

That gives this flow:

```text
DB2 — BAU/source of customer and personalisation data
   │
   │ Read only
   ▼
Bulk-migration Spring Boot application
   │
   ├── Candidate discovery: 12:00 AM–2:00 AM
   ├── Bulk migration:      2:00 AM–5:00 AM
   └── Retry/reconciliation:5:00 AM–6:00 AM
   │
   ▼
MariaDB
   ├── Migration control tables
   ├── Candidate customer IDs
   └── Migrated business data
```

The migration application can have:

* Scheduled automatic execution
* Manual REST endpoints for fallback
* Twelve PCF instances across DCE/DCW
* Atomic database claiming to prevent overlap
* MariaDB idempotency to handle overlap with live seamless migration

Cloud Foundry supplies `CF_INSTANCE_INDEX` and `CF_INSTANCE_GUID`. Use the index for readable identification and the GUID to distinguish a restarted or recreated instance. ([Cloud Foundry Docs][1])

---

# 1. Main design decision

## Do not use a shared offset counter as the work-claim mechanism

A table like this is unsafe:

```text
CURRENT_OFFSET = 0
LIMIT = 100000
```

Two instances can read the same value before either updates it.

You could lock the row, but a further issue remains: if the query filters by changing status, offset results move when rows are updated.

Instead, use:

```text
Migration run
    └── Fixed candidate population
            └── Fixed logical partitions
                    └── Small claimed customer batches
```

The fallback endpoint may accept `offset` and `limit` for **candidate discovery**, but migration execution should claim rows by status and partition rather than process an arbitrary offset.

---

# 2. Recommended tables in MariaDB

Use three control tables:

```text
MIGRATION_RUN
MIGRATION_PARTITION
MIGRATION_CUSTOMER
```

## 2.1 Migration run

One row represents one complete migration exercise.

```sql
CREATE TABLE migration_run
(
    run_id                    BIGINT NOT NULL AUTO_INCREMENT,
    run_name                  VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,

    source_start_datetime     DATETIME(6) NOT NULL,
    source_cutoff_datetime    DATETIME(6) NOT NULL,

    status                    VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,

    discovery_started_at      DATETIME(6),
    discovery_completed_at    DATETIME(6),

    migration_started_at      DATETIME(6),
    migration_completed_at    DATETIME(6),

    discovered_count          BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    eligible_count            BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    migrated_count            BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    already_migrated_count    BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    failed_count              BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,

    created_at                DATETIME(6) NOT NULL,
    updated_at                DATETIME(6) NOT NULL,

    PRIMARY KEY (run_id)
);
```

Suggested run statuses:

```text
CREATED
DISCOVERING
DISCOVERY_COMPLETED
MIGRATING
MIGRATION_COMPLETED
PARTIAL
FAILED
CANCELLED
```

Example:

```text
RUN_ID: 20260731
SOURCE_START: 2026-04-30 00:00:00
SOURCE_CUTOFF: 2026-07-31 00:00:00
STATUS: CREATED
```

The dates must remain fixed for the entire run. Do not use a fresh `CURRENT TIMESTAMP` every time a scheduler executes.

---

## 2.2 Migration partition

Create more logical partitions than PCF instances.

For 12 instances, I recommend:

```text
60 logical partitions
```

Each partition will contain roughly:

```text
300,000 ÷ 60 = approximately 5,000 customers
```

Table:

```sql
CREATE TABLE migration_partition
(
    run_id                  BIGINT NOT NULL,
    phase                   VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,
    partition_id            INT NOT NULL,

    status                  VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,

    claimed_by              VARCHAR(200),
    claimed_at              DATETIME(6),
    last_heartbeat_at       DATETIME(6),
    completed_at            DATETIME(6),

    expected_count          BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    processed_count         BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    migrated_count          BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    already_migrated_count  BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    failed_count            BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,

    retry_count             INT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    version_no              BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    error_message           VARCHAR(2000),

    PRIMARY KEY (run_id, phase, partition_id),

    CONSTRAINT fk_partition_run
        FOREIGN KEY (run_id)
        REFERENCES migration_run(run_id)
);
```

Partition statuses:

```text
PENDING
CLAIMED
IN_PROGRESS
COMPLETED
PARTIAL
FAILED
```

The `phase` can be:

```text
DISCOVERY
MIGRATION
RETRY
```

---

## 2.3 Customer work table

One row per customer for the run.

```sql
CREATE TABLE migration_customer
(
    run_id                    BIGINT NOT NULL,
    customer_sys_gen_id       BIGINT NOT NULL,
    partition_id              INT NOT NULL,

    migration_status          VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,

    claimed_by                VARCHAR(200),
    claimed_at                DATETIME(6),
    started_at                DATETIME(6),
    completed_at              DATETIME(6),

    retry_count               INT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,

    db2_personalisation_count INT,
    mariadb_inserted_count    INT,

    error_code                VARCHAR(100),
    error_message             VARCHAR(2000),

    created_at                DATETIME(6) NOT NULL,
    updated_at                DATETIME(6) NOT NULL,

    PRIMARY KEY
    (
        run_id,
        customer_sys_gen_id
    ),

    INDEX idx_migration_customer_claim
    (
        run_id,
        migration_status,
        partition_id,
        customer_sys_gen_id
    ),

    INDEX idx_migration_customer_worker
    (
        run_id,
        claimed_by,
        migration_status
    ),

    CONSTRAINT fk_customer_run
        FOREIGN KEY (run_id)
        REFERENCES migration_run(run_id)
);
```

Suggested customer statuses:

```text
ELIGIBLE
IN_PROGRESS
MIGRATED
ALREADY_MIGRATED
FAILED
RETRY_PENDING
SOURCE_NOT_FOUND
```

I recommend `ELIGIBLE` instead of `ONLINE`. `ONLINE` can be confused with application availability or current login status.

---

# 3. Phase 1: Candidate discovery, 12:00 AM–2:00 AM

The goal of this phase is:

```text
Read eligible IDs from DB2
        ↓
Insert unique candidate IDs into MariaDB migration_customer
```

## DB2 source query

Assumptions:

```text
CUSTOMER
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
    LAST_LOGON_DATETIME

PERSONALISATION
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
    SOFT_DELETE
    other composite-key columns
```

Recommended query:

```sql
SELECT
    c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER c
WHERE c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= ?
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME < ?
  AND EXISTS
  (
      SELECT 1
      FROM PERSONALISATION p
      WHERE p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID =
            c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
        AND p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
  )
  AND MOD(c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, ?) = ?
ORDER BY c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
OFFSET ? ROWS
FETCH NEXT ? ROWS ONLY;
```

Parameters:

```text
1. Start timestamp
2. Cutoff timestamp
3. Logical partition count: 60
4. Partition ID: 0–59
5. Offset within that fixed partition
6. Page size
```

Because the source population is fixed by timestamps and DB2 data is not being updated by the migration application, offset is less dangerous here than using it against changing migration statuses.

However, keyset pagination is still better.

## Preferred keyset query

```sql
SELECT
    c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER c
WHERE c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= ?
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME < ?
  AND c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID > ?
  AND MOD(c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, ?) = ?
  AND EXISTS
  (
      SELECT 1
      FROM PERSONALISATION p
      WHERE p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID =
            c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
        AND p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
  )
ORDER BY c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FETCH FIRST ? ROWS ONLY;
```

Parameters include:

```text
lastCustomerId
logicalPartitionCount
partitionId
pageSize
```

This avoids increasingly expensive large offsets.

---

# 4. Candidate insertion into MariaDB

Use a unique key so the scheduler or manual endpoint can safely repeat a discovery page.

```sql
INSERT INTO migration_customer
(
    run_id,
    customer_sys_gen_id,
    partition_id,
    migration_status,
    created_at,
    updated_at
)
VALUES
(
    ?,
    ?,
    ?,
    'ELIGIBLE',
    CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6)
)
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
    updated_at = updated_at;
```

MariaDB supports `INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE`, which is suitable for making repeated discovery or migration operations idempotent. ([MariaDB][2])

Do not overwrite an existing `MIGRATED` status during duplicate discovery.

A safe no-op is:

```sql
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
    customer_sys_gen_id = customer_sys_gen_id;
```

Or use JDBC batch insert and treat duplicate key as an already-discovered record.

---

# 5. How a partition is claimed

This is the part that coordinates the 12 instances.

Each instance asks MariaDB:

```text
Give me one PENDING partition
```

The claim must occur inside a short transaction.

## Repository claim algorithm

```java
@Transactional
public Optional<MigrationPartition> claimNextPartition(
        long runId,
        String phase,
        String workerId) {

    Optional<MigrationPartition> partition =
            partitionRepository.findNextPendingForUpdate(
                    runId,
                    phase
            );

    if (partition.isEmpty()) {
        return Optional.empty();
    }

    MigrationPartition claimed = partition.get();

    claimed.setStatus("CLAIMED");
    claimed.setClaimedBy(workerId);
    claimed.setClaimedAt(Instant.now());
    claimed.setLastHeartbeatAt(Instant.now());
    claimed.setVersionNo(claimed.getVersionNo() + 1);

    partitionRepository.save(claimed);

    return Optional.of(claimed);
}
```

The transaction commits immediately after the status update.

Do not keep the transaction open while migrating thousands of customers.

## JPA locking query

```java
public interface MigrationPartitionRepository
        extends JpaRepository<
            MigrationPartition,
            MigrationPartitionId> {

    @Lock(LockModeType.PESSIMISTIC_WRITE)
    @Query("""
        select p
        from MigrationPartition p
        where p.runId = :runId
          and p.phase = :phase
          and p.status = 'PENDING'
        order by p.partitionId
        """)
    List<MigrationPartition> findPendingForUpdate(
            @Param("runId") long runId,
            @Param("phase") String phase,
            Pageable pageable);
}
```

Service:

```java
@Transactional
public Optional<MigrationPartition> claimNextPartition(
        long runId,
        String phase,
        String workerId) {

    List<MigrationPartition> partitions =
            repository.findPendingForUpdate(
                    runId,
                    phase,
                    PageRequest.of(0, 1)
            );

    if (partitions.isEmpty()) {
        return Optional.empty();
    }

    MigrationPartition partition = partitions.get();

    partition.setStatus("CLAIMED");
    partition.setClaimedBy(workerId);
    partition.setClaimedAt(Instant.now());
    partition.setLastHeartbeatAt(Instant.now());

    repository.save(partition);

    return Optional.of(partition);
}
```

### What happens with 12 simultaneous calls?

```text
Instance 1 locks partition 0
Instance 2 waits or skips partition 0 and locks partition 1
Instance 3 locks partition 2
...
```

Depending on your MariaDB/JPA locking behavior, waiting can occur. With only 12 workers and 60 partitions, the lock transaction should be very short.

An alternative is an atomic update with optimistic version checking.

---

# 6. Optimistic atomic-claim option

First select a candidate partition:

```sql
SELECT
    partition_id,
    version_no
FROM migration_partition
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND phase = ?
  AND status = 'PENDING'
ORDER BY partition_id
LIMIT 1;
```

Then claim it:

```sql
UPDATE migration_partition
SET status = 'CLAIMED',
    claimed_by = ?,
    claimed_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    last_heartbeat_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    version_no = version_no + 1
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND phase = ?
  AND partition_id = ?
  AND status = 'PENDING'
  AND version_no = ?;
```

Check the affected-row count:

```java
int updated = jdbcTemplate.update(...);

if (updated == 1) {
    // This instance successfully claimed it.
} else {
    // Another instance claimed it first.
    // Retry with another partition.
}
```

This is often easier to reason about than holding a pessimistic lock.

## Complete claim service

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class PartitionClaimService {

    private final JdbcTemplate jdbcTemplate;

    @Transactional
    public Optional<ClaimedPartition> claimNext(
            long runId,
            String phase,
            String workerId) {

        for (int attempt = 0; attempt < 10; attempt++) {

            List<PartitionCandidate> candidates =
                    jdbcTemplate.query(
                        """
                        SELECT
                            partition_id,
                            version_no
                        FROM migration_partition
                        WHERE run_id = ?
                          AND phase = ?
                          AND status = 'PENDING'
                        ORDER BY partition_id
                        LIMIT 1
                        """,
                        (rs, rowNum) -> new PartitionCandidate(
                            rs.getInt("partition_id"),
                            rs.getLong("version_no")
                        ),
                        runId,
                        phase
                    );

            if (candidates.isEmpty()) {
                return Optional.empty();
            }

            PartitionCandidate candidate = candidates.get(0);

            int updated = jdbcTemplate.update(
                """
                UPDATE migration_partition
                SET status = 'CLAIMED',
                    claimed_by = ?,
                    claimed_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
                    last_heartbeat_at =
                        CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
                    version_no = version_no + 1
                WHERE run_id = ?
                  AND phase = ?
                  AND partition_id = ?
                  AND status = 'PENDING'
                  AND version_no = ?
                """,
                workerId,
                runId,
                phase,
                candidate.partitionId(),
                candidate.versionNo()
            );

            if (updated == 1) {
                return Optional.of(
                    new ClaimedPartition(
                        runId,
                        phase,
                        candidate.partitionId(),
                        workerId
                    )
                );
            }
        }

        return Optional.empty();
    }
}
```

This prevents two instances from successfully claiming the same partition.

---

# 7. Worker identity

Create a unique worker name from environment variables.

```java
@Component
@Getter
public class WorkerIdentity {

    private final String dataCentre;
    private final String instanceIndex;
    private final String instanceGuid;
    private final String workerId;

    public WorkerIdentity(
            @Value("${MIGRATION_DC:UNKNOWN}")
            String dataCentre,

            @Value("${CF_INSTANCE_INDEX:0}")
            String instanceIndex,

            @Value("${CF_INSTANCE_GUID:LOCAL}")
            String instanceGuid) {

        this.dataCentre = dataCentre;
        this.instanceIndex = instanceIndex;
        this.instanceGuid = instanceGuid;

        this.workerId = String.format(
            "%s-%s-%s",
            dataCentre,
            instanceIndex,
            instanceGuid
        );
    }
}
```

Examples:

```text
DCE-0-a12f...
DCE-1-b31c...
DCW-0-f90a...
```

Do not use only `CF_INSTANCE_INDEX`, because a replaced instance may receive the same index.

---

# 8. Candidate discovery worker

Each discovered logical partition is approximately 5,000 customers, not 100,000.

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class CandidateDiscoveryService {

    private final Db2CandidateRepository db2Repository;
    private final MigrationCustomerRepository customerRepository;
    private final MigrationPartitionRepository partitionRepository;

    @Transactional("mariaDbTransactionManager")
    public DiscoveryResult discoverPartition(
            long runId,
            int partitionId,
            int totalPartitions,
            Instant startDate,
            Instant cutoffDate,
            int pageSize,
            String workerId) {

        long lastCustomerId = 0;
        long discovered = 0;

        while (true) {
            List<Long> customerIds =
                    db2Repository.findEligibleCustomerIds(
                        startDate,
                        cutoffDate,
                        lastCustomerId,
                        totalPartitions,
                        partitionId,
                        pageSize
                    );

            if (customerIds.isEmpty()) {
                break;
            }

            customerRepository.insertCandidates(
                    runId,
                    partitionId,
                    customerIds
            );

            discovered += customerIds.size();
            lastCustomerId =
                    customerIds.get(customerIds.size() - 1);

            partitionRepository.updateHeartbeatAndCount(
                    runId,
                    "DISCOVERY",
                    partitionId,
                    workerId,
                    discovered,
                    lastCustomerId
            );
        }

        partitionRepository.completeDiscoveryPartition(
                runId,
                partitionId,
                workerId,
                discovered
        );

        return new DiscoveryResult(
                partitionId,
                discovered
        );
    }
}
```

In real implementation, do not wrap the entire loop in one MariaDB transaction. Commit each page.

Better structure:

```java
while (...) {
    List<Long> ids = readFromDb2(...);

    insertPageInShortTransaction(ids);

    updateCheckpointInShortTransaction(...);
}
```

---

# 9. Scheduler for discovery

Your scheduler can still run every five minutes, but one execution should claim and process available partitions continuously until its allowed time budget expires.

Spring fixed-delay scheduling measures the delay from the completion of one execution to the start of the next. ([Home][3])

```java
@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@ConditionalOnProperty(
    name = "migration.scheduler.enabled",
    havingValue = "true"
)
public class MigrationScheduler {

    private final MigrationWindowService windowService;
    private final PartitionClaimService claimService;
    private final CandidateDiscoveryService discoveryService;
    private final CustomerMigrationService migrationService;
    private final WorkerIdentity workerIdentity;
    private final ActiveRunService activeRunService;

    @Scheduled(
        fixedDelayString =
            "${migration.discovery.scheduler-delay-ms:300000}"
    )
    public void discoverCandidates() {

        if (!windowService.isDiscoveryWindow()) {
            return;
        }

        MigrationRun run = activeRunService.getActiveRun();

        Instant invocationDeadline =
                Instant.now().plus(Duration.ofMinutes(4));

        while (Instant.now().isBefore(invocationDeadline)) {

            Optional<ClaimedPartition> claimed =
                    claimService.claimNext(
                        run.getRunId(),
                        "DISCOVERY",
                        workerIdentity.getWorkerId()
                    );

            if (claimed.isEmpty()) {
                return;
            }

            discoveryService.process(
                    run,
                    claimed.get(),
                    workerIdentity.getWorkerId()
            );
        }
    }
}
```

This means:

```text
Every five minutes:
    Start scheduler
    Work for up to four minutes
    Claim several partitions if possible
    Exit
```

However, because a partition is approximately 5,000 records, one partition may complete quickly.

---

# 10. Migration phase: 2:00 AM–5:00 AM

The migration scheduler should not wait ten minutes after every customer batch.

It can be invoked every ten minutes, but once invoked it should continuously process for around nine minutes.

```java
@Scheduled(
    fixedDelayString =
        "${migration.execution.scheduler-delay-ms:600000}"
)
public void migrateCandidates() {

    if (!windowService.isMigrationWindow()) {
        return;
    }

    MigrationRun run = activeRunService.getActiveRun();

    Instant invocationDeadline =
            Instant.now().plus(Duration.ofMinutes(9));

    while (Instant.now().isBefore(invocationDeadline)) {

        int processed =
                migrationService.processNextCustomerBatch(
                    run.getRunId(),
                    workerIdentity.getWorkerId(),
                    250
                );

        if (processed == 0) {
            return;
        }
    }
}
```

Still, a shorter fixed delay such as one to five seconds is simpler:

```java
@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "5000")
```

A scheduler run processes one claimed batch and returns.

---

# 11. How customer records are claimed

Each worker claims only a small number, for example 250.

## Step 1: select candidates

```sql
SELECT
    customer_sys_gen_id
FROM migration_customer
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND migration_status IN
      ('ELIGIBLE', 'RETRY_PENDING')
ORDER BY partition_id, customer_sys_gen_id
LIMIT ?;
```

## Step 2: atomically claim them

Use a generated `claimToken`, not only the worker ID.

```java
String claimToken =
        workerId + "-" + UUID.randomUUID();
```

MariaDB claim:

```sql
UPDATE migration_customer
SET migration_status = 'IN_PROGRESS',
    claimed_by = ?,
    claimed_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    started_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    updated_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6)
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND migration_status IN
      ('ELIGIBLE', 'RETRY_PENDING')
ORDER BY partition_id, customer_sys_gen_id
LIMIT ?;
```

This statement can atomically claim rows, but identifying exactly which rows that update changed is easier when using a claim token column.

Add:

```sql
ALTER TABLE migration_customer
ADD COLUMN claim_token VARCHAR(250);
```

Claim:

```sql
UPDATE migration_customer
SET migration_status = 'IN_PROGRESS',
    claimed_by = ?,
    claim_token = ?,
    claimed_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    started_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    updated_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6)
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND migration_status IN
      ('ELIGIBLE', 'RETRY_PENDING')
ORDER BY partition_id, customer_sys_gen_id
LIMIT ?;
```

Then retrieve:

```sql
SELECT customer_sys_gen_id
FROM migration_customer
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND claim_token = ?
  AND migration_status = 'IN_PROGRESS'
ORDER BY customer_sys_gen_id;
```

Because the `UPDATE` is atomic, two workers cannot update the same row from `ELIGIBLE` to `IN_PROGRESS`.

## Claim repository

```java
@Repository
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class MigrationCustomerClaimRepository {

    private final JdbcTemplate jdbcTemplate;

    @Transactional
    public ClaimedCustomerBatch claim(
            long runId,
            String workerId,
            int limit) {

        String claimToken =
                workerId + "-" + UUID.randomUUID();

        int claimedCount = jdbcTemplate.update(
            """
            UPDATE migration_customer
            SET migration_status = 'IN_PROGRESS',
                claimed_by = ?,
                claim_token = ?,
                claimed_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
                started_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
                updated_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6)
            WHERE run_id = ?
              AND migration_status IN
                  ('ELIGIBLE', 'RETRY_PENDING')
            ORDER BY partition_id,
                     customer_sys_gen_id
            LIMIT ?
            """,
            workerId,
            claimToken,
            runId,
            limit
        );

        if (claimedCount == 0) {
            return ClaimedCustomerBatch.empty();
        }

        List<Long> customerIds = jdbcTemplate.query(
            """
            SELECT customer_sys_gen_id
            FROM migration_customer
            WHERE run_id = ?
              AND claim_token = ?
              AND migration_status = 'IN_PROGRESS'
            ORDER BY customer_sys_gen_id
            """,
            (rs, rowNum) ->
                rs.getLong("customer_sys_gen_id"),
            runId,
            claimToken
        );

        return new ClaimedCustomerBatch(
                claimToken,
                customerIds
        );
    }
}
```

This design means you may not even need migration-level partition claiming. Customer-level atomic claiming is sufficient.

Partitions remain useful for:

* Discovery distribution
* Reporting
* Progress monitoring
* Workload balancing

---

# 12. Migration service

The bulk application should invoke the same core migration method used by seamless migration.

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class BulkCustomerMigrationService {

    private final MigrationCustomerClaimRepository claimRepository;
    private final ExistingMigrationService existingMigrationService;
    private final MigrationStatusRepository statusRepository;

    public int processNextCustomerBatch(
            long runId,
            String workerId,
            int batchSize) {

        ClaimedCustomerBatch claimed =
                claimRepository.claim(
                    runId,
                    workerId,
                    batchSize
                );

        if (claimed.customerIds().isEmpty()) {
            return 0;
        }

        for (Long customerId : claimed.customerIds()) {
            migrateOne(
                    runId,
                    customerId,
                    workerId
            );
        }

        return claimed.customerIds().size();
    }

    private void migrateOne(
            long runId,
            Long customerId,
            String workerId) {

        try {
            MigrationOutcome outcome =
                    existingMigrationService
                        .migrateCustomer(customerId);

            switch (outcome) {
                case MIGRATED ->
                    statusRepository.markMigrated(
                        runId,
                        customerId,
                        workerId
                    );

                case ALREADY_MIGRATED ->
                    statusRepository.markAlreadyMigrated(
                        runId,
                        customerId,
                        workerId
                    );

                case SOURCE_NOT_FOUND ->
                    statusRepository.markSourceNotFound(
                        runId,
                        customerId,
                        workerId
                    );
            }

        } catch (TransientDataAccessException exception) {
            statusRepository.markRetryPending(
                    runId,
                    customerId,
                    exception.getMessage()
            );

        } catch (Exception exception) {
            statusRepository.markFailed(
                    runId,
                    customerId,
                    exception.getClass().getSimpleName(),
                    exception.getMessage()
            );
        }
    }
}
```

---

# 13. Status updates

## Successful migration

```sql
UPDATE migration_customer
SET migration_status = 'MIGRATED',
    completed_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    updated_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    mariadb_inserted_count = ?,
    error_code = NULL,
    error_message = NULL
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND customer_sys_gen_id = ?
  AND migration_status = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND claimed_by = ?;
```

## Already migrated

```sql
UPDATE migration_customer
SET migration_status = 'ALREADY_MIGRATED',
    completed_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    updated_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    error_code = NULL,
    error_message = NULL
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND customer_sys_gen_id = ?
  AND migration_status = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

## Retryable failure

```sql
UPDATE migration_customer
SET migration_status =
        CASE
            WHEN retry_count + 1 < 3
                THEN 'RETRY_PENDING'
            ELSE 'FAILED'
        END,
    retry_count = retry_count + 1,
    claim_token = NULL,
    claimed_by = NULL,
    claimed_at = NULL,
    updated_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    error_code = ?,
    error_message = ?
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND customer_sys_gen_id = ?;
```

## Permanent failure

```sql
UPDATE migration_customer
SET migration_status = 'FAILED',
    retry_count = retry_count + 1,
    completed_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    updated_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    error_code = ?,
    error_message = ?
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND customer_sys_gen_id = ?;
```

---

# 14. MariaDB migration idempotency

The bulk process and seamless migration can race.

Example:

```text
2:15:01 Bulk process starts customer 123
2:15:02 Customer logs in
2:15:03 Seamless migration inserts customer 123
2:15:04 Bulk process attempts the same insert
```

The target personalisation table must have its genuine composite unique key.

Example:

```sql
ALTER TABLE personalisation
ADD CONSTRAINT uk_personalisation
UNIQUE
(
    customer_sys_gen_id,
    personalisation_type,
    personalisation_key
);
```

Then:

```sql
INSERT INTO personalisation
(
    customer_sys_gen_id,
    personalisation_type,
    personalisation_key,
    personalisation_value,
    created_at,
    updated_at
)
VALUES
(
    ?, ?, ?, ?, ?, ?
)
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
    personalisation_value =
        VALUES(personalisation_value),
    updated_at =
        VALUES(updated_at);
```

This protects against duplicate inserts at the database layer. ([MariaDB][2])

Be careful about updating newer MariaDB data with older DB2 data. If MariaDB can contain more recent changes, use a source timestamp condition in your migration logic.

For example:

```sql
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
    personalisation_value =
        CASE
            WHEN VALUES(source_updated_at)
                 > source_updated_at
            THEN VALUES(personalisation_value)
            ELSE personalisation_value
        END,
    source_updated_at =
        GREATEST(
            source_updated_at,
            VALUES(source_updated_at)
        );
```

---

# 15. Stale claim recovery

Suppose a PCF instance claims 250 customers and crashes.

Those records remain `IN_PROGRESS`.

A recovery scheduler should reset stale claims:

```java
@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "600000")
public void recoverStaleClaims() {

    if (!windowService.isAnyMigrationWindow()) {
        return;
    }

    recoveryRepository.releaseStaleClaims(
            activeRunService.getActiveRunId(),
            Duration.ofMinutes(15)
    );
}
```

SQL:

```sql
UPDATE migration_customer
SET migration_status =
        CASE
            WHEN retry_count + 1 < 3
                THEN 'RETRY_PENDING'
            ELSE 'FAILED'
        END,
    retry_count = retry_count + 1,
    claimed_by = NULL,
    claim_token = NULL,
    claimed_at = NULL,
    updated_at = CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    error_code = 'STALE_CLAIM',
    error_message =
        'Worker claim expired before completion'
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND migration_status = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND claimed_at <
      CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6) - INTERVAL 15 MINUTE;
```

Use small batches so a normal batch does not legitimately take more than the stale threshold.

---

# 16. Time-window service

```java
@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class MigrationWindowService {

    private final Clock clock;
    private final MigrationProperties properties;

    public boolean isDiscoveryWindow() {
        return isBetween(
            properties.discoveryStart(),
            properties.discoveryEnd()
        );
    }

    public boolean isMigrationWindow() {
        return isBetween(
            properties.migrationStart(),
            properties.migrationEnd()
        );
    }

    public boolean isRetryWindow() {
        return isBetween(
            properties.retryStart(),
            properties.retryEnd()
        );
    }

    private boolean isBetween(
            LocalTime start,
            LocalTime end) {

        LocalTime now = LocalTime.now(clock);

        return !now.isBefore(start)
                && now.isBefore(end);
    }
}
```

Configuration:

```yaml
migration:
  scheduler:
    enabled: true

  timezone: Asia/Singapore

  discovery:
    start: "00:00"
    end: "02:00"
    scheduler-delay-ms: 300000
    logical-partitions: 60
    db2-page-size: 5000

  execution:
    start: "02:00"
    end: "05:00"
    scheduler-delay-ms: 5000
    claim-size: 250

  retry:
    start: "05:00"
    end: "05:30"
    scheduler-delay-ms: 60000
    claim-size: 100

  reconciliation:
    start: "05:30"
    end: "06:00"

  claim:
    stale-after-minutes: 15
    maximum-retries: 3
```

Explicitly configure the timezone:

```java
@Bean
public Clock migrationClock(
        @Value("${migration.timezone}")
        String timezone) {

    return Clock.system(
        ZoneId.of(timezone)
    );
}
```

---

# 17. Manual fallback endpoints

You can expose manual endpoints, but they should invoke the same service layer as the scheduler.

Do not duplicate migration logic inside controllers.

## Recommended endpoints

```text
POST /internal/migration/runs
POST /internal/migration/runs/{runId}/discover
POST /internal/migration/runs/{runId}/migrate
POST /internal/migration/runs/{runId}/retry
POST /internal/migration/runs/{runId}/recover-stale
GET  /internal/migration/runs/{runId}/status
POST /internal/migration/runs/{runId}/pause
POST /internal/migration/runs/{runId}/resume
```

---

## Create run endpoint

```java
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/internal/migration/runs")
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class MigrationController {

    private final MigrationOrchestrator orchestrator;

    @PostMapping
    public ResponseEntity<CreateRunResponse> createRun(
            @Valid @RequestBody CreateRunRequest request) {

        long runId = orchestrator.createRun(request);

        return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.CREATED)
                .body(new CreateRunResponse(runId));
    }
}
```

Request:

```java
public record CreateRunRequest(
    @NotNull Instant sourceStartDatetime,
    @NotNull Instant sourceCutoffDatetime,
    @Min(1) @Max(500) int logicalPartitions
) {
}
```

Example JSON:

```json
{
  "sourceStartDatetime": "2026-04-30T00:00:00Z",
  "sourceCutoffDatetime": "2026-07-31T00:00:00Z",
  "logicalPartitions": 60
}
```

---

## Manual candidate discovery with limit and offset

You requested an endpoint accepting `limit` and `offset`.

Support it as an administrative fallback, but make it idempotent.

```java
public record DiscoveryRequest(
    @Min(0) long offset,
    @Min(1) @Max(10000) int limit,
    @Min(0) int partitionId,
    @Min(1) int totalPartitions
) {
}
```

Controller:

```java
@PostMapping("/{runId}/discover")
public ResponseEntity<DiscoveryResponse> discover(
        @PathVariable long runId,
        @Valid @RequestBody DiscoveryRequest request) {

    DiscoveryResponse response =
            orchestrator.discoverManually(
                runId,
                request
            );

    return ResponseEntity.ok(response);
}
```

Service:

```java
public DiscoveryResponse discoverManually(
        long runId,
        DiscoveryRequest request) {

    MigrationRun run = runRepository
            .findById(runId)
            .orElseThrow(() ->
                new RunNotFoundException(runId));

    List<Long> ids =
            db2CandidateRepository
                .findEligibleCustomerIdsUsingOffset(
                    run.getSourceStartDatetime(),
                    run.getSourceCutoffDatetime(),
                    request.partitionId(),
                    request.totalPartitions(),
                    request.offset(),
                    request.limit()
                );

    int inserted =
            customerRepository.insertCandidates(
                runId,
                request.partitionId(),
                ids
            );

    return new DiscoveryResponse(
        ids.size(),
        inserted,
        ids.size() - inserted,
        request.offset() + ids.size()
    );
}
```

Example response:

```json
{
  "readFromDb2": 5000,
  "insertedIntoControlTable": 4875,
  "alreadyDiscovered": 125,
  "nextOffset": 105000
}
```

### Important restriction

Do not allow `limit = 100000` by default.

Use:

```text
Maximum manual discovery limit: 5,000–10,000 IDs
```

A 100,000-row response can cause:

* High application memory
* Large DB transactions
* Long HTTP request
* Router timeout
* Difficult restart recovery

The endpoint may execute 20 pages of 5,000 rather than one page of 100,000.

---

## Manual migration endpoint

The migration fallback endpoint should accept only a claim size, not an arbitrary offset.

```java
public record MigrationTriggerRequest(
    @Min(1) @Max(1000) int claimSize,
    @Min(1) @Max(100) int maximumBatches
) {
}
```

Controller:

```java
@PostMapping("/{runId}/migrate")
public ResponseEntity<MigrationTriggerResponse> migrate(
        @PathVariable long runId,
        @Valid @RequestBody
        MigrationTriggerRequest request) {

    MigrationTriggerResponse response =
            orchestrator.migrateManually(
                runId,
                request.claimSize(),
                request.maximumBatches()
            );

    return ResponseEntity.ok(response);
}
```

Service:

```java
public MigrationTriggerResponse migrateManually(
        long runId,
        int claimSize,
        int maximumBatches) {

    long attempted = 0;
    long migrated = 0;
    long alreadyMigrated = 0;
    long failed = 0;

    for (int batch = 0;
         batch < maximumBatches;
         batch++) {

        BatchMigrationResult result =
                bulkMigrationService.processNextBatch(
                    runId,
                    workerIdentity.getWorkerId(),
                    claimSize
                );

        if (result.claimedCount() == 0) {
            break;
        }

        attempted += result.claimedCount();
        migrated += result.migratedCount();
        alreadyMigrated +=
                result.alreadyMigratedCount();
        failed += result.failedCount();
    }

    return new MigrationTriggerResponse(
        attempted,
        migrated,
        alreadyMigrated,
        failed
    );
}
```

Because this endpoint uses the same atomic claiming logic, it can be called while schedulers are running without overlapping work.

---

# 18. Endpoint security

The endpoints should be:

* Internal-only
* Protected by authentication and authorization
* Restricted to migration operators
* Audited
* Rate-limited
* Disabled outside the migration period unless an override is supplied

Do not expose unrestricted endpoints that can trigger a large DB2 scan or migration.

Example:

```java
@PreAuthorize("hasAuthority('MIGRATION_OPERATOR')")
@PostMapping("/{runId}/migrate")
public ResponseEntity<?> migrate(...) {
    ...
}
```

Add an idempotency key for administrative requests:

```text
X-Idempotency-Key: migration-run-123-discovery-page-20
```

Store it if there is a meaningful chance that operations tooling will resend requests.

---

# 19. Reconciliation queries

## Status summary

```sql
SELECT
    migration_status,
    COUNT(*) AS customer_count
FROM migration_customer
WHERE run_id = ?
GROUP BY migration_status
ORDER BY migration_status;
```

## Validation equation

```text
Total candidates
=
MIGRATED
+ ALREADY_MIGRATED
+ FAILED
+ SOURCE_NOT_FOUND
+ ELIGIBLE
+ IN_PROGRESS
+ RETRY_PENDING
```

## Remaining work

```sql
SELECT COUNT(*)
FROM migration_customer
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND migration_status IN
      (
        'ELIGIBLE',
        'IN_PROGRESS',
        'RETRY_PENDING'
      );
```

## Failed IDs

```sql
SELECT
    customer_sys_gen_id,
    retry_count,
    error_code,
    error_message
FROM migration_customer
WHERE run_id = ?
  AND migration_status = 'FAILED'
ORDER BY customer_sys_gen_id;
```

## Inconsistent migrated customers

After customer migration:

```text
Compare active DB2 personalisation row count
with
MariaDB personalisation row count
```

Store:

```text
db2_personalisation_count
mariadb_inserted_count
```

But remember that row counts may legitimately differ when MariaDB deduplicates or transforms records. Reconciliation should use your business mapping rules, not only raw counts.

---

# 20. Recommended schedule

## 12:00 AM–2:00 AM: discovery

```text
12 PCF instances available
60 logical discovery partitions
Each instance atomically claims a partition
DB2 page size: 2,000–5,000
Candidate IDs inserted into MariaDB
Unique key prevents duplicate candidates
```

Do not allocate 100,000 IDs to one scheduler call.

## 2:00 AM–4:30 AM: migration

```text
12 workers
Claim 100–250 customers per batch initially
Process continuously
Update every customer status
Record heartbeat and metrics
```

Start with a conservative claim size.

Increase to 500 only after testing.

## 4:30 AM–5:00 AM: completion and transient retry

```text
Stop claiming large batches
Continue small batches
Retry transient failures
Recover stale claims
```

## 5:00 AM–5:30 AM: failed-record retry

```text
Retry only RETRY_PENDING
Do not retry permanent validation errors automatically
```

## 5:30 AM–6:00 AM: reconciliation

```text
Disable new claims
Complete in-progress records
Generate status counts
Export failed IDs
Validate target records
Mark run completed or partial
```

---

# 21. Pros and cons of the revised implementation

## Pros

| Benefit                                 | Explanation                                                 |
| --------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- |
| No bulk-worker overlap                  | MariaDB status claim is atomic                              |
| Safe scheduler and endpoint coexistence | Both use the same claim service                             |
| Safe PCF restart                        | Stale `IN_PROGRESS` records can be reclaimed                |
| DB2 remains BAU                         | Migration control writes do not affect DB2                  |
| Replayable discovery                    | Unique candidate key prevents duplicate rows                |
| Live seamless migration supported       | MariaDB unique keys/upserts provide protection              |
| Detailed audit trail                    | Every run, partition and customer has timestamps and status |
| Easy operational fallback               | Endpoints can trigger controlled batches                    |
| No dependency on fixed instance indexes | Any healthy instance can claim available work               |
| Better six-hour utilisation             | Small continuous batches avoid long idle periods            |

## Cons

| Cost or risk                             | Mitigation                                     |
| ---------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- |
| Three additional MariaDB control tables  | Keep them in a separate migration schema       |
| More application code                    | Isolate it in orchestration/control packages   |
| Status writes add MariaDB load           | Batch updates and proper indexes               |
| DB2/MariaDB cannot share one transaction | Make operations idempotent and retryable       |
| A customer may partially migrate         | Use customer-level transactional target writes |
| HTTP fallback can be misused             | Limit size, secure and audit endpoints         |
| Candidate query may be heavy             | Correct DB2 indexes and fixed partitions       |
| Twelve workers may overload databases    | Pilot and throttle claim size/concurrency      |
