# Final recommended implementation

```text
DB2
    ├── Customer table
    ├── Personalisation table
    └── Read-only from migration app
              │
              ▼
Candidate discovery
    ├── Fixed three-month start and cutoff
    ├── 60 logical partitions
    ├── DB2 page size 2,000–5,000
    └── Insert unique customer IDs into MariaDB
              │
              ▼
MariaDB migration_customer
    ├── ELIGIBLE
    ├── IN_PROGRESS
    ├── MIGRATED
    ├── ALREADY_MIGRATED
    ├── RETRY_PENDING
    └── FAILED
              │
              ▼
12 PCF workers
    ├── Atomic claims of 100–250 customers
    ├── Existing migration service reused
    ├── Unique-key/upsert target writes
    ├── Status updated per customer
    └── Stale claims automatically recovered
```

The key rule is:

```text
Scheduler and manual endpoint
        ↓
Same orchestration service
        ↓
Same atomic claiming mechanism
        ↓
No offset-based migration execution
```

Use `offset/limit` only as a controlled fallback for discovering a fixed DB2 population. For actual migration, use `ELIGIBLE → IN_PROGRESS` atomic claims, which allows all 12 schedulers and manual endpoint requests to run without processing the same customer twice.

[1]: https://docs.cloudfoundry.org/devguide/deploy-apps/environment-variable.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Cloud Foundry environment variables"
[2]: https://mariadb.com/docs/server/reference/sql-statements/data-manipulation/inserting-loading-data/insert-on-duplicate-key-update?utm_source=chatgpt.com "INSERT ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE | Server - MariaDB"
[3]: https://docs.spring.io/spring-framework/docs/current/javadoc-api/org/springframework/scheduling/annotation/Scheduled.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com "Annotation Interface Scheduled"



I was thinking distinct customer read from maria db and add those entry in db2 table with ONLINE as migration status (customer migration status table) Also we will have overall 3.8 data to be migrated will be in a table personalisation to be migrated table ( personalisation + customer table join and check for soft delete 0 and last login date time within last 3 months ) When scheduler run for migration it look data not in Customer not migrated ( from customer migration status table) but in to be migrated table Will able any issue in this approach ? Pros and Cons ?

Yes, this approach can work, and it is simpler than maintaining customer-level claims in MariaDB. But you need to protect against **stale status, partial migration, duplicate processing, and seamless-migration races**.

## Your proposed model

### DB2 source tables

```text
CUSTOMER
PERSONALISATION
```

### DB2 migration tables

```text
CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
    STATUS = ONLINE / MIGRATED / FAILED

PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
    Snapshot of eligible personalisation rows
    created using:
        PERSONALISATION
        JOIN CUSTOMER
        SOFT_DELETE = 0
        LAST_LOGON within 3 months
```

### Preparation

1. Read distinct migrated customer IDs from MariaDB.
2. Insert those IDs into the DB2 `CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS` table with `ONLINE`.
3. Build `PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED`.
4. Migration scheduler selects customers present in `PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED` but absent from `CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS`.
5. After migration, insert or update status as `MIGRATED`.
6. On failure, update status as `FAILED`.

Conceptually:

```text
Eligible customers in DB2 snapshot
MINUS
Customers already present in MariaDB
=
Customers requiring bulk migration
```

That is valid.

# Main concern: status meaning

`ONLINE` is unclear. It appears to mean:

```text
Already exists in MariaDB before bulk migration
```

That is different from:

```text
Successfully migrated by the bulk process
```

Use separate statuses:

```text
ALREADY_MIGRATED
IN_PROGRESS
MIGRATED
FAILED
RETRY_PENDING
```

Recommended interpretation:

| Status             | Meaning                                                |
| ------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| `ALREADY_MIGRATED` | Customer already existed in MariaDB during preparation |
| `IN_PROGRESS`      | Bulk worker currently processing                       |
| `MIGRATED`         | Bulk migration completed successfully                  |
| `FAILED`           | Migration failed permanently                           |
| `RETRY_PENDING`    | Temporary failure, eligible for retry                  |

Using `ONLINE` can create confusion during audit and reconciliation.

# Recommended table structures

## Customer migration status

```sql
CREATE TABLE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
(
    RUN_ID                  BIGINT NOT NULL,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID     BIGINT NOT NULL,
    MIGRATION_STATUS        VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,

    CLAIMED_BY              VARCHAR(150),
    CLAIMED_AT              TIMESTAMP,
    STARTED_AT              TIMESTAMP,
    COMPLETED_AT            TIMESTAMP,

    RETRY_COUNT             INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    SOURCE_ROW_COUNT        INTEGER,
    TARGET_ROW_COUNT        INTEGER,

    ERROR_CODE              VARCHAR(100),
    ERROR_MESSAGE           VARCHAR(1000),

    CREATED_AT              TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                            DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT              TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                            DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    PRIMARY KEY
    (
        RUN_ID,
        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
    )
);
```

## Personalisation migration snapshot

```sql
CREATE TABLE PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
AS
(
    SELECT
        p.*,
        c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME,
        CAST(NULL AS BIGINT) AS RUN_ID
    FROM PERSONALISATION p
    INNER JOIN CUSTOMER c
        ON c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID =
           p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
    WHERE p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
      AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >=
          :THREE_MONTH_START
      AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME <
          :FIXED_CUTOFF
)
WITH NO DATA;
```

Then populate with a fixed `RUN_ID` and cutoff.

However, copying all personalisation columns may be unnecessary. You can instead create a customer candidate table and read the current personalisation rows from the original DB2 tables during migration.

That is usually better unless you specifically need an immutable source snapshot.

# Will the anti-join work?

Yes.

Use `NOT EXISTS`, not `NOT IN`.

## Find customers requiring migration

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT
       p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED p
WHERE p.RUN_ID = :RUN_ID
  AND NOT EXISTS
      (
          SELECT 1
          FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS s
          WHERE s.RUN_ID = p.RUN_ID
            AND s.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID =
                p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
            AND s.MIGRATION_STATUS IN
                (
                    'ALREADY_MIGRATED',
                    'MIGRATED',
                    'IN_PROGRESS'
                )
      );
```

Avoid:

```sql
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID NOT IN (...)
```

because a `NULL` in the subquery can cause unexpected results.

# Important problem: multiple personalisation rows per customer

You said approximately **3.8 million data rows** are in the personalisation snapshot.

That may not mean 3.8 million customers.

For example:

```text
3.8 million personalisation rows
300,000 distinct customers
```

The scheduler must claim and migrate by:

```text
CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
```

not by individual personalisation row.

Otherwise, two workers may migrate different rows for the same customer at the same time.

Recommended candidate query:

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT
       CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
WHERE RUN_ID = :RUN_ID;
```

Better still, create a separate candidate table:

```sql
CREATE TABLE CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID              BIGINT NOT NULL,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID BIGINT NOT NULL,
    PARTITION_ID        INTEGER NOT NULL,

    PRIMARY KEY
    (
        RUN_ID,
        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
    )
);
```

Populate it from the 3.8-million-row snapshot:

```sql
INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PARTITION_ID
)
SELECT DISTINCT
       :RUN_ID,
       CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
       MOD(CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, 60)
FROM PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
WHERE RUN_ID = :RUN_ID;
```

Then the scheduler works from the much smaller customer table.

# Main race condition

Your preparation may happen like this:

```text
12:30 AM:
Read migrated customers from MariaDB
Insert them into DB2 status table

1:30 AM:
Customer logs in
Seamless migration migrates them to MariaDB

2:15 AM:
Bulk scheduler sees no status row in DB2
Bulk scheduler migrates the same customer
```

Your DB2 status table does not know about that seamless migration unless you synchronise again.

This is not fatal, but MariaDB migration must be idempotent.

Before or during migration:

```text
Check whether customer exists in MariaDB
```

Even after checking, retain:

* MariaDB unique key
* Upsert
* Duplicate-key treated as success

Because the customer can still be migrated between the existence check and insert.

After detecting the customer already exists, update DB2 status to:

```text
ALREADY_MIGRATED
```

# Another race: 12 bulk workers

This query alone is unsafe:

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
WHERE customer is not in status table
FETCH FIRST 500 ROWS ONLY;
```

Twelve instances may all read the same first 500 customers before any status records are inserted.

You must claim the customer before migration.

## Claim process

### Step 1: Insert candidate status as `IN_PROGRESS`

For every selected customer, attempt:

```sql
INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    MIGRATION_STATUS,
    CLAIMED_BY,
    CLAIMED_AT,
    CREATED_AT,
    UPDATED_AT
)
VALUES
(
    :RUN_ID,
    :CUSTOMER_ID,
    'IN_PROGRESS',
    :WORKER_ID,
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP
);
```

Because the primary key is:

```text
RUN_ID + CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
```

only one instance can successfully insert the claim.

Other instances receive duplicate-key and skip that customer.

This is the simplest safe claim mechanism for your design.

## Claim using `MERGE`

DB2 example conceptually:

```sql
MERGE INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS target
USING
(
    VALUES
    (
        :RUN_ID,
        :CUSTOMER_ID,
        :WORKER_ID
    )
) source
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    WORKER_ID
)
ON
(
    target.RUN_ID = source.RUN_ID
    AND target.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID =
        source.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
)
WHEN NOT MATCHED THEN
    INSERT
    (
        RUN_ID,
        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
        MIGRATION_STATUS,
        CLAIMED_BY,
        CLAIMED_AT,
        CREATED_AT,
        UPDATED_AT
    )
    VALUES
    (
        source.RUN_ID,
        source.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
        'IN_PROGRESS',
        source.WORKER_ID,
        CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
        CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
        CURRENT TIMESTAMP
    );
```

After execution:

* Inserted row means claim succeeded.
* No insert means another worker or prior migration already owns it.

# Recommended scheduler flow

```text
1. Read 500 distinct candidate customer IDs
   from CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED.

2. For each customer, attempt to insert
   status = IN_PROGRESS.

3. Keep only successfully claimed customers.

4. Check those customer IDs in MariaDB in one batch.

5. Existing customers:
       update DB2 status = ALREADY_MIGRATED.

6. Missing customers:
       read personalisation rows from DB2 snapshot.

7. Migrate to MariaDB using existing migration service.

8. Success:
       update status = MIGRATED.

9. Temporary failure:
       update status = RETRY_PENDING.

10. Permanent failure:
       update status = FAILED.
```

# Better batch SQL flow

## Read candidate IDs

```sql
SELECT c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED c
WHERE c.RUN_ID = :RUN_ID
  AND c.PARTITION_ID = :PARTITION_ID
  AND NOT EXISTS
      (
          SELECT 1
          FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS s
          WHERE s.RUN_ID = c.RUN_ID
            AND s.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID =
                c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
      )
ORDER BY c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FETCH FIRST 500 ROWS ONLY;
```

Then claim them through batch insert.

Because there is a unique primary key, overlap is prevented even when multiple schedulers read the same IDs.

# Failure and retry issue

If a failed customer already has a row in the status table, a pure `NOT EXISTS` query will never select them again.

That means retry must be a separate query:

```sql
SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
WHERE RUN_ID = :RUN_ID
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'RETRY_PENDING'
  AND RETRY_COUNT < 3
ORDER BY UPDATED_AT
FETCH FIRST 100 ROWS ONLY;
```

To claim a retry safely:

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
SET MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS',
    CLAIMED_BY = :WORKER_ID,
    CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE RUN_ID = :RUN_ID
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = :CUSTOMER_ID
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'RETRY_PENDING';
```

Check that exactly one row was updated.

# Stale `IN_PROGRESS` issue

If a PCF instance crashes after claiming customers, they remain `IN_PROGRESS`.

Add a recovery scheduler:

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
SET MIGRATION_STATUS =
        CASE
            WHEN RETRY_COUNT < 3
                THEN 'RETRY_PENDING'
            ELSE 'FAILED'
        END,
    RETRY_COUNT = RETRY_COUNT + 1,
    CLAIMED_BY = NULL,
    CLAIMED_AT = NULL,
    ERROR_CODE = 'STALE_CLAIM',
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE RUN_ID = :RUN_ID
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND CLAIMED_AT <
      CURRENT TIMESTAMP - 15 MINUTES;
```

# Snapshot consistency issue

Your `PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED` table represents a snapshot.

That is good for stable migration counts, but it means later BAU changes in DB2 may not be included.

For example:

```text
1:00 AM snapshot captures personalisation A

1:30 AM customer changes personalisation A in DB2

2:30 AM bulk migration copies old snapshot value
```

You must decide whether the bulk migration should use:

## Option A: Snapshot data

```text
Read from PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
```

Pros:

* Consistent source
* Repeatable migration
* Easier reconciliation

Cons:

* May migrate stale data
* Snapshot table is large

## Option B: Snapshot only customer IDs

```text
PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED contains only customer IDs
Migration reads current records from original DB2 tables
```

Pros:

* Latest BAU data is migrated
* Much smaller snapshot
* Less DB2 storage

Cons:

* Exact migrated dataset may change during the window
* Reconciliation is slightly more complex

Because DB2 remains BAU, I would normally recommend:

```text
Snapshot eligible customer IDs
Read the latest personalisation data from DB2
at the time each customer is migrated
```

That avoids migrating an old personalisation snapshot.

# Pros of your approach

| Advantage                                                    | Explanation                                        |
| ------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------- |
| DB2 controls migration                                       | Easy for BAU operations to inspect                 |
| MariaDB migrated IDs are preloaded                           | Quickly excludes most existing customers           |
| Stable eligible population                                   | Three-month cutoff creates a clear migration scope |
| Easy anti-join                                               | Candidate minus status identifies remaining work   |
| Simple audit                                                 | One status row per customer                        |
| Supports scheduler and endpoint                              | Both can use the same DB2 control tables           |
| Easy reconciliation                                          | Compare candidate count against final statuses     |
| Avoids writing migration control into target business tables | Separation of concerns                             |
| Can run without distributed scheduler lock                   | Unique claim row prevents overlap                  |

# Cons and risks

| Risk                                    | Impact                                             | Improvement                                                               |
| --------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| MariaDB status export becomes stale     | Seamless-migrated customers may be processed again | Recheck MariaDB during migration and use idempotent writes                |
| 3.8-million-row snapshot is large       | Storage and query load                             | Snapshot only distinct customer IDs unless row-level snapshot is required |
| Multiple rows per customer              | Same customer may be selected many times           | Create distinct customer candidate table                                  |
| Twelve workers can read same candidates | Duplicate processing                               | Insert `IN_PROGRESS` claim with unique key before migration               |
| Failed row exists in status table       | `NOT EXISTS` will never retry it                   | Separate retry query using `RETRY_PENDING`                                |
| Worker crash leaves `IN_PROGRESS`       | Customer remains stuck                             | Stale-claim recovery scheduler                                            |
| Snapshot may become stale               | Old personalisation values migrate                 | Read current DB2 data when processing                                     |
| DB2 status table adds write load        | BAU DB2 receives migration writes                  | Small status table with proper indexes and batch commits                  |
| Cross-database transaction unavailable  | MariaDB may succeed before DB2 status update       | Idempotency and reconciliation                                            |
| `ONLINE` status is ambiguous            | Reporting confusion                                | Use `ALREADY_MIGRATED`                                                    |

# Recommended refined design

```text
DB2 BAU tables
    CUSTOMER
    PERSONALISATION
          │
          ▼
CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED
    Distinct customer IDs
    Fixed 3-month eligibility cutoff
          │
          ▼
CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
    ALREADY_MIGRATED
    IN_PROGRESS
    MIGRATED
    RETRY_PENDING
    FAILED
          │
          ▼
12 migration workers
    Claim through unique status insert
    Batch-check MariaDB
    Read latest DB2 personalisation
    Upsert into MariaDB
    Update final DB2 status
```

## My recommendation

Your design is valid after these adjustments:

1. Store **distinct customer IDs**, not only the 3.8 million personalisation rows.
2. Change `ONLINE` to `ALREADY_MIGRATED`.
3. Before migration, atomically insert `IN_PROGRESS` to claim the customer.
4. Recheck MariaDB after claiming.
5. Use MariaDB unique keys and idempotent upsert.
6. Add `RETRY_PENDING` and stale-claim recovery.
7. Prefer reading current personalisation records from BAU DB2 rather than migrating an old snapshot.
8. Use a fixed `RUN_ID` and fixed three-month cutoff for repeatable reconciliation.

With these protections, the anti-join approach is simpler and should work well for your six-hour migration window.


Customer migration status will populated by the scheduler we discussing earlier on 12-2 And migration scheduler 2-5 Can come up with details architecture and plan with code ?



Below is a complete design based on your confirmed approach:

```text
12:00 AM–2:00 AM
Populate CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS in DB2
using distinct migrated customers read from MariaDB.

2:00 AM–5:00 AM
Migrate eligible DB2 personalisation customers that are not already
marked as migrated in CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS.

5:00 AM–6:00 AM
Retry failures, recover stale claims and reconcile results.
```

The key improvement is to use the DB2 status table for both:

* Already migrated customers found in MariaDB
* New bulk-migration processing and outcomes

# 1. Overall architecture

```text
                         ┌──────────────────────────┐
                         │      MariaDB target      │
                         │                          │
                         │ Existing migrated users  │
                         │ New personalisation data │
                         └────────────┬─────────────┘
                                      │
                         Read existing customer IDs
                           between 12:00–2:00 AM
                                      │
                                      ▼
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                         DB2 — BAU source                         │
│                                                                  │
│  CUSTOMER                                                        │
│  PERSONALISATION                                                 │
│                                                                  │
│  PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED                                  │
│  CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS                                       │
│  MIGRATION_RUN                                                   │
│  MIGRATION_BATCH_CONTROL                                         │
└───────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┘
                                │
                                │ Read customer and personalisation
                                ▼
                 ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
                 │ Spring Boot migration app       │
                 │                                 │
                 │ 12 PCF application instances    │
                 │ DCE/DCW                         │
                 │ CF_INSTANCE_INDEX               │
                 │ CF_INSTANCE_GUID                │
                 │                                 │
                 │ Scheduler + fallback endpoints  │
                 └───────────────┬─────────────────┘
                                 │
                                 │ Idempotent insert/upsert
                                 ▼
                         MariaDB target tables
```

---

# 2. Data model

Use four DB2 migration-control tables.

## 2.1 Migration run

One row represents one migration execution.

```sql
CREATE TABLE MIGRATION_RUN
(
    RUN_ID                    BIGINT NOT NULL,
    RUN_STATUS                VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,

    ELIGIBILITY_START_TIME    TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,
    ELIGIBILITY_CUTOFF_TIME   TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,

    STATUS_LOAD_STARTED_AT    TIMESTAMP,
    STATUS_LOAD_COMPLETED_AT  TIMESTAMP,

    MIGRATION_STARTED_AT      TIMESTAMP,
    MIGRATION_COMPLETED_AT    TIMESTAMP,

    TOTAL_ELIGIBLE_CUSTOMERS  BIGINT DEFAULT 0,
    ALREADY_MIGRATED_COUNT    BIGINT DEFAULT 0,
    MIGRATED_COUNT            BIGINT DEFAULT 0,
    FAILED_COUNT              BIGINT DEFAULT 0,

    CREATED_AT                TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                              DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT                TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                              DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    PRIMARY KEY (RUN_ID)
);
```

Suggested statuses:

```text
CREATED
LOADING_EXISTING_STATUS
STATUS_LOAD_COMPLETED
MIGRATING
MIGRATION_COMPLETED
PARTIAL
FAILED
CANCELLED
```

---

## 2.2 Personalisation to be migrated

This contains the eligible source records based on:

* Personalisation soft delete = 0
* Customer last logon within three months
* Fixed start and cutoff timestamp

```sql
CREATE TABLE PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID                       BIGINT NOT NULL,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID          BIGINT NOT NULL,

    PERSONALISATION_TYPE         VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY          VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE        VARCHAR(2000),

    SOURCE_CREATED_AT            TIMESTAMP,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT            TIMESTAMP,

    PARTITION_ID                 INTEGER NOT NULL,

    CREATED_AT                   TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                                 DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    PRIMARY KEY
    (
        RUN_ID,
        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
        PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
        PERSONALISATION_KEY
    )
);
```

Recommended indexes:

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_PTBM_RUN_CUSTOMER
ON PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
);

CREATE INDEX IDX_PTBM_RUN_PARTITION_CUSTOMER
ON PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID,
    PARTITION_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
);
```

For approximately 3.8 million rows, do not repeatedly run `DISTINCT` directly over this table during every migration scheduler execution.

Create a separate distinct customer table.

---

## 2.3 Distinct eligible customer table

```sql
CREATE TABLE CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID                  BIGINT NOT NULL,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID     BIGINT NOT NULL,
    PARTITION_ID            INTEGER NOT NULL,

    CREATED_AT              TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                            DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    PRIMARY KEY
    (
        RUN_ID,
        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
    )
);
```

Index:

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_CTBM_RUN_PARTITION_CUSTOMER
ON CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID,
    PARTITION_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
);
```

Populate it once:

```sql
INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PARTITION_ID
)
SELECT DISTINCT
       RUN_ID,
       CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
       MOD(CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, 60)
FROM PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
WHERE RUN_ID = :RUN_ID;
```

For 300,000 customers:

```text
60 logical partitions
≈ 5,000 customers per partition
```

---

## 2.4 Customer migration status

This is the main coordination table.

```sql
CREATE TABLE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
(
    RUN_ID                  BIGINT NOT NULL,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID     BIGINT NOT NULL,

    MIGRATION_STATUS        VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,

    CLAIMED_BY              VARCHAR(200),
    CLAIMED_AT              TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT       TIMESTAMP,

    STARTED_AT              TIMESTAMP,
    COMPLETED_AT            TIMESTAMP,

    RETRY_COUNT             INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,

    SOURCE_RECORD_COUNT     INTEGER,
    INSERTED_RECORD_COUNT   INTEGER,
    UPDATED_RECORD_COUNT    INTEGER,

    ERROR_CODE              VARCHAR(100),
    ERROR_MESSAGE           VARCHAR(2000),

    CREATED_AT              TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                            DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT              TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                            DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    PRIMARY KEY
    (
        RUN_ID,
        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
    )
);
```

Indexes:

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_CMS_RUN_STATUS
ON CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
(
    RUN_ID,
    MIGRATION_STATUS,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
);

CREATE INDEX IDX_CMS_STALE
ON CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
(
    RUN_ID,
    MIGRATION_STATUS,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT
);
```

Recommended statuses:

```text
ALREADY_MIGRATED
IN_PROGRESS
MIGRATED
RETRY_PENDING
FAILED
SOURCE_NOT_FOUND
```

Do not use `ONLINE` unless that term is already fixed by your business terminology. `ALREADY_MIGRATED` is much clearer.

---

# 3. Migration phases

# Phase A: Prepare eligible source data

This should preferably happen before the six-hour window.

```sql
INSERT INTO PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
    SOURCE_CREATED_AT,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT,
    PARTITION_ID
)
SELECT
    :RUN_ID,
    p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    p.PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    p.PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    p.PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
    p.CREATED_AT,
    p.UPDATED_AT,
    MOD(p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, 60)
FROM PERSONALISATION p
INNER JOIN CUSTOMER c
    ON c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID =
       p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
WHERE p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= :START_TIME
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME <  :CUTOFF_TIME;
```

Then create the distinct customer list:

```sql
INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PARTITION_ID
)
SELECT DISTINCT
       RUN_ID,
       CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
       PARTITION_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
WHERE RUN_ID = :RUN_ID;
```

If this work must also happen within 12:00–2:00 AM, monitor DB2 carefully because building 3.8 million rows can consume a significant portion of the window.

---

# Phase B: 12:00 AM–2:00 AM — populate existing migration status

During this period:

1. Read distinct existing customer IDs from MariaDB.
2. Insert those IDs into DB2 `CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS`.
3. Status is `ALREADY_MIGRATED`.
4. Repeated execution must be safe.

## MariaDB source query

Use keyset pagination rather than offset where possible.

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT
       CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM TARGET_PERSONALISATION
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID > ?
ORDER BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
LIMIT ?;
```

Suggested page size:

```text
5,000–20,000 customer IDs
```

Do not start with 100,000 until memory, network and DB testing proves it safe.

## Insert into DB2

```sql
MERGE INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS target
USING
(
    VALUES
    (
        CAST(? AS BIGINT),
        CAST(? AS BIGINT)
    )
) source
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
)
ON
(
    target.RUN_ID = source.RUN_ID
    AND target.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID =
        source.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
)
WHEN NOT MATCHED THEN
    INSERT
    (
        RUN_ID,
        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
        MIGRATION_STATUS,
        CREATED_AT,
        UPDATED_AT
    )
    VALUES
    (
        source.RUN_ID,
        source.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
        'ALREADY_MIGRATED',
        CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
        CURRENT TIMESTAMP
    );
```

If the row already exists as `MIGRATED`, `FAILED` or `IN_PROGRESS`, do not overwrite it.

---

# 4. Scheduler 1: existing-status loader

Configuration:

```yaml
migration:
  timezone: Asia/Singapore

  status-load:
    enabled: true
    start: "00:00"
    end: "02:00"
    scheduler-delay-ms: 300000
    page-size: 10000
```

Scheduler:

```java
@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class ExistingStatusLoadScheduler {

    private final MigrationWindowService windowService;
    private final ExistingStatusLoadService loadService;
    private final ActiveMigrationRunService runService;
    private final WorkerIdentity workerIdentity;

    @Scheduled(
        fixedDelayString =
            "${migration.status-load.scheduler-delay-ms:300000}"
    )
    public void loadExistingStatuses() {

        if (!windowService.isStatusLoadWindow()) {
            return;
        }

        long runId = runService.getActiveRunId();

        loadService.loadNextPage(
                runId,
                workerIdentity.getWorkerId()
        );
    }
}
```

---

# 5. Avoid overlap between 12 instances during status loading

Do not allow all 12 instances to fetch the same MariaDB page.

Use a batch-control table.

```sql
CREATE TABLE MIGRATION_BATCH_CONTROL
(
    RUN_ID                   BIGINT NOT NULL,
    PHASE                    VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,
    BATCH_ID                 BIGINT NOT NULL,

    START_CUSTOMER_ID        BIGINT NOT NULL,
    END_CUSTOMER_ID          BIGINT,

    STATUS                   VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,

    CLAIMED_BY               VARCHAR(200),
    CLAIMED_AT               TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT        TIMESTAMP,
    COMPLETED_AT             TIMESTAMP,

    READ_COUNT               BIGINT DEFAULT 0,
    INSERTED_COUNT           BIGINT DEFAULT 0,
    ERROR_MESSAGE            VARCHAR(2000),

    VERSION_NO               BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,

    PRIMARY KEY
    (
        RUN_ID,
        PHASE,
        BATCH_ID
    )
);
```

Suggested status-loader batches:

```text
Batch 1: customer ID > 0 and <= 1,000,000
Batch 2: customer ID > 1,000,000 and <= 2,000,000
...
```

Or create smaller fixed ranges.

A worker claims a pending batch atomically.

## Claim SQL

First select:

```sql
SELECT
    BATCH_ID,
    VERSION_NO
FROM MIGRATION_BATCH_CONTROL
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND PHASE = 'STATUS_LOAD'
  AND STATUS = 'PENDING'
ORDER BY BATCH_ID
FETCH FIRST 1 ROW ONLY;
```

Then atomically update:

```sql
UPDATE MIGRATION_BATCH_CONTROL
SET STATUS = 'CLAIMED',
    CLAIMED_BY = ?,
    CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    VERSION_NO = VERSION_NO + 1
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND PHASE = 'STATUS_LOAD'
  AND BATCH_ID = ?
  AND STATUS = 'PENDING'
  AND VERSION_NO = ?;
```

If affected rows = 1, this worker owns the batch.

If affected rows = 0, another worker claimed it first.

---

# 6. Status-load service code

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class ExistingStatusLoadService {

    private final BatchClaimRepository batchClaimRepository;
    private final MariaDbExistingCustomerRepository mariaRepository;
    private final Db2MigrationStatusRepository statusRepository;
    private final Db2BatchControlRepository batchRepository;

    public StatusLoadResult loadNextPage(
            long runId,
            String workerId) {

        Optional<MigrationBatch> optionalBatch =
                batchClaimRepository.claimNext(
                    runId,
                    "STATUS_LOAD",
                    workerId
                );

        if (optionalBatch.isEmpty()) {
            return StatusLoadResult.noWork();
        }

        MigrationBatch batch = optionalBatch.get();

        try {
            batchRepository.markInProgress(
                    runId,
                    batch.batchId(),
                    workerId
            );

            long lastCustomerId =
                    batch.startCustomerId();

            long readCount = 0;
            long insertedCount = 0;

            while (true) {
                List<Long> customerIds =
                        mariaRepository.findCustomerIds(
                            lastCustomerId,
                            batch.endCustomerId(),
                            10_000
                        );

                if (customerIds.isEmpty()) {
                    break;
                }

                int inserted =
                        statusRepository.insertAlreadyMigrated(
                            runId,
                            customerIds
                        );

                readCount += customerIds.size();
                insertedCount += inserted;

                lastCustomerId =
                        customerIds.get(customerIds.size() - 1);

                batchRepository.updateHeartbeat(
                        runId,
                        batch.batchId(),
                        workerId,
                        lastCustomerId,
                        readCount,
                        insertedCount
                );
            }

            batchRepository.markCompleted(
                    runId,
                    batch.batchId(),
                    workerId,
                    readCount,
                    insertedCount
            );

            return new StatusLoadResult(
                    batch.batchId(),
                    readCount,
                    insertedCount
            );

        } catch (Exception exception) {
            batchRepository.markFailed(
                    runId,
                    batch.batchId(),
                    workerId,
                    exception.getMessage()
            );

            throw exception;
        }
    }
}
```

Important: commit each page of 10,000 IDs. Do not keep the entire range in one transaction.

---

# 7. Phase C: 2:00 AM–5:00 AM migration scheduler

The migration scheduler must identify:

```text
Customer exists in CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED
AND
there is no final status row in CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
```

But the selection alone is not enough. Twelve instances may select the same customer.

Each worker must first claim the customer by inserting `IN_PROGRESS`.

---

# 8. Select candidate customers

```sql
SELECT
    c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED c
WHERE c.RUN_ID = ?
  AND NOT EXISTS
  (
      SELECT 1
      FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS s
      WHERE s.RUN_ID = c.RUN_ID
        AND s.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID =
            c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
  )
ORDER BY
    c.PARTITION_ID,
    c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FETCH FIRST ? ROWS ONLY;
```

Suggested initial read size:

```text
500 candidate IDs
```

All 12 workers may occasionally read some of the same IDs. That is acceptable because the claim insert determines ownership.

---

# 9. Customer claim logic

For each candidate, try:

```sql
INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    MIGRATION_STATUS,
    CLAIMED_BY,
    CLAIMED_AT,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT,
    STARTED_AT,
    CREATED_AT,
    UPDATED_AT
)
VALUES
(
    ?,
    ?,
    'IN_PROGRESS',
    ?,
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP
);
```

Because the primary key is:

```text
RUN_ID + CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
```

only one worker can insert successfully.

Result:

```text
Insert succeeds
    → this instance owns the customer

Duplicate key
    → another worker or previous process already owns/completed it
```

This is the simplest race-condition protection.

---

# 10. Batch claim service code

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class CustomerClaimService {

    private final Db2CandidateCustomerRepository candidateRepository;
    private final Db2MigrationStatusRepository statusRepository;

    public List<Long> claimNextCustomers(
            long runId,
            String workerId,
            int readLimit,
            int claimLimit) {

        List<Long> candidates =
                candidateRepository.findUnclaimedCustomers(
                    runId,
                    readLimit
                );

        List<Long> claimed = new ArrayList<>();

        for (Long customerId : candidates) {
            try {
                int inserted =
                        statusRepository.insertInProgress(
                            runId,
                            customerId,
                            workerId
                        );

                if (inserted == 1) {
                    claimed.add(customerId);
                }

                if (claimed.size() >= claimLimit) {
                    break;
                }

            } catch (DuplicateKeyException ignored) {
                // Another instance claimed or completed the customer.
            }
        }

        return claimed;
    }
}
```

Recommended values:

```text
Read limit: 500–1,000
Claim limit: 100–250
```

Do not claim 100,000 customers at once.

---

# 11. Recheck MariaDB after claim

Your `ALREADY_MIGRATED` status was loaded between 12:00–2:00 AM.

A customer may be seamlessly migrated at 2:15 AM after that snapshot.

Therefore, after claiming customers:

```text
Batch-check MariaDB again
```

Query:

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM TARGET_PERSONALISATION
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN
(
    ?, ?, ?, ...
);
```

For those already present:

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
SET MIGRATION_STATUS = 'ALREADY_MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?;
```

Only the remaining customers proceed to migration.

---

# 12. Read source personalisation rows

For claimed missing customers:

```sql
SELECT
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
    SOURCE_CREATED_AT,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT
FROM PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN
  (
      ?, ?, ?, ...
  )
ORDER BY
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY;
```

Read in batches of approximately 100–250 customers, depending on average personalisation rows per customer.

Group results in Java:

```java
Map<Long, List<SourcePersonalisation>> byCustomer =
        rows.stream()
            .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
                SourcePersonalisation::customerSysGenId
            ));
```

---

# 13. Migration service

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class BulkMigrationService {

    private final CustomerClaimService claimService;
    private final MariaDbExistingCustomerRepository targetCheckRepository;
    private final Db2PersonalisationMigrationRepository sourceRepository;
    private final ExistingCustomerMigrationService migrationService;
    private final Db2MigrationStatusRepository statusRepository;

    public BatchMigrationResult processNextBatch(
            long runId,
            String workerId,
            int claimSize) {

        List<Long> claimed =
                claimService.claimNextCustomers(
                    runId,
                    workerId,
                    claimSize * 4,
                    claimSize
                );

        if (claimed.isEmpty()) {
            return BatchMigrationResult.empty();
        }

        Set<Long> alreadyExists =
                targetCheckRepository.findExistingCustomers(
                    claimed
                );

        for (Long customerId : alreadyExists) {
            statusRepository.markAlreadyMigrated(
                    runId,
                    customerId,
                    workerId
            );
        }

        List<Long> missingCustomers =
                claimed.stream()
                    .filter(id -> !alreadyExists.contains(id))
                    .toList();

        if (missingCustomers.isEmpty()) {
            return BatchMigrationResult.fromAlreadyMigrated(
                    claimed.size()
            );
        }

        Map<Long, List<SourcePersonalisation>> sourceData =
                sourceRepository.findByCustomerIds(
                    runId,
                    missingCustomers
                );

        int migrated = 0;
        int failed = 0;

        for (Long customerId : missingCustomers) {
            List<SourcePersonalisation> personalisations =
                    sourceData.getOrDefault(
                        customerId,
                        List.of()
                    );

            if (personalisations.isEmpty()) {
                statusRepository.markSourceNotFound(
                        runId,
                        customerId,
                        workerId
                );
                continue;
            }

            try {
                MigrationOutcome outcome =
                        migrationService.migrateCustomer(
                            customerId,
                            personalisations
                        );

                statusRepository.markMigrated(
                        runId,
                        customerId,
                        workerId,
                        personalisations.size(),
                        outcome.insertedCount(),
                        outcome.updatedCount()
                );

                migrated++;

            } catch (TransientDataAccessException exception) {
                statusRepository.markRetryPending(
                        runId,
                        customerId,
                        workerId,
                        exception.getClass().getSimpleName(),
                        safeMessage(exception)
                );

                failed++;

            } catch (Exception exception) {
                statusRepository.markFailed(
                        runId,
                        customerId,
                        workerId,
                        exception.getClass().getSimpleName(),
                        safeMessage(exception)
                );

                failed++;
            }
        }

        return new BatchMigrationResult(
                claimed.size(),
                migrated,
                alreadyExists.size(),
                failed
        );
    }

    private String safeMessage(Exception exception) {
        String message = exception.getMessage();

        if (message == null) {
            return exception.getClass().getSimpleName();
        }

        return message.substring(
            0,
            Math.min(message.length(), 1900)
        );
    }
}
```

---

# 14. Migration scheduler

Configuration:

```yaml
migration:
  execution:
    enabled: true
    start: "02:00"
    stop-claim: "04:30"
    hard-stop: "05:00"
    scheduler-delay-ms: 5000
    claim-size: 200
```

Scheduler:

```java
@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class BulkMigrationScheduler {

    private final MigrationWindowService windowService;
    private final ActiveMigrationRunService runService;
    private final WorkerIdentity workerIdentity;
    private final BulkMigrationService migrationService;

    @Scheduled(
        fixedDelayString =
            "${migration.execution.scheduler-delay-ms:5000}"
    )
    public void migrate() {

        if (!windowService.canClaimMigrationWork()) {
            return;
        }

        long runId = runService.getActiveRunId();

        migrationService.processNextBatch(
                runId,
                workerIdentity.getWorkerId(),
                200
        );
    }
}
```

With `fixedDelay`, the next scheduler execution starts after the previous execution finishes plus the configured delay.

Do not use a ten-minute idle delay for the primary worker. A five-second delay is safer for the short window.

A separate ten-minute scheduler can be used for monitoring and recovery.

---

# 15. MariaDB write must be idempotent

The final target table must have its true business composite unique key.

```sql
ALTER TABLE TARGET_PERSONALISATION
ADD CONSTRAINT UK_TARGET_PERSONALISATION
UNIQUE
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY
);
```

Write:

```sql
INSERT INTO TARGET_PERSONALISATION
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT
)
VALUES
(
    ?, ?, ?, ?, ?
)
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE =
        CASE
            WHEN VALUES(SOURCE_UPDATED_AT) >= SOURCE_UPDATED_AT
            THEN VALUES(PERSONALISATION_VALUE)
            ELSE PERSONALISATION_VALUE
        END,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT =
        GREATEST(
            SOURCE_UPDATED_AT,
            VALUES(SOURCE_UPDATED_AT)
        );
```

This protects against:

* Bulk worker duplication
* Seamless migration race
* Manual endpoint retrigger
* Retry after partial success
* PCF instance restart

---

# 16. Status update SQL

## Migrated

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
SET MIGRATION_STATUS = 'MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    SOURCE_RECORD_COUNT = ?,
    INSERTED_RECORD_COUNT = ?,
    UPDATED_RECORD_COUNT = ?,
    ERROR_CODE = NULL,
    ERROR_MESSAGE = NULL
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?;
```

## Retry pending

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
SET MIGRATION_STATUS =
        CASE
            WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < 3
            THEN 'RETRY_PENDING'
            ELSE 'FAILED'
        END,
    RETRY_COUNT = RETRY_COUNT + 1,
    CLAIMED_BY = NULL,
    CLAIMED_AT = NULL,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = NULL,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = ?,
    ERROR_MESSAGE = ?
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

## Already migrated

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
SET MIGRATION_STATUS = 'ALREADY_MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?;
```

---

# 17. Retry scheduler: 5:00 AM–5:30 AM

Retry candidates:

```sql
SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'RETRY_PENDING'
  AND RETRY_COUNT < 3
ORDER BY UPDATED_AT
FETCH FIRST ? ROWS ONLY;
```

Claim retry atomically:

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
SET MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS',
    CLAIMED_BY = ?,
    CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'RETRY_PENDING';
```

Only process the customer when affected rows = 1.

---

# 18. Stale claim recovery

If an instance crashes after claiming customers:

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
SET MIGRATION_STATUS =
        CASE
            WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < 3
            THEN 'RETRY_PENDING'
            ELSE 'FAILED'
        END,
    RETRY_COUNT = RETRY_COUNT + 1,
    CLAIMED_BY = NULL,
    CLAIMED_AT = NULL,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = NULL,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = 'STALE_CLAIM',
    ERROR_MESSAGE =
        'Migration worker stopped before completion'
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT <
      CURRENT TIMESTAMP - 15 MINUTES;
```

Run this recovery every ten minutes.

---

# 19. Worker identity

```java
@Component
@Getter
public class WorkerIdentity {

    private final String workerId;

    public WorkerIdentity(
            @Value("${MIGRATION_DC:LOCAL}")
            String dataCentre,

            @Value("${CF_INSTANCE_INDEX:0}")
            String instanceIndex,

            @Value("${CF_INSTANCE_GUID:LOCAL}")
            String instanceGuid) {

        this.workerId = String.format(
            "%s-%s-%s",
            dataCentre,
            instanceIndex,
            instanceGuid
        );
    }
}
```

Examples:

```text
DCE-0-f39a...
DCE-1-a21b...
DCW-0-73ce...
```

The environment variables identify the worker, but DB2 unique keys and conditional status updates prevent overlap.

---

# 20. Manual fallback endpoints

The endpoints should call the same services used by the scheduler.

## Status-load endpoint

```java
@PostMapping("/runs/{runId}/load-existing")
public StatusLoadResult loadExisting(
        @PathVariable long runId,
        @RequestBody StatusLoadRequest request) {

    return statusLoadService.loadRange(
            runId,
            request.startCustomerId(),
            request.endCustomerId(),
            request.pageSize(),
            workerIdentity.getWorkerId()
    );
}
```

Request:

```java
public record StatusLoadRequest(
        long startCustomerId,
        Long endCustomerId,
        @Min(1) @Max(20_000) int pageSize) {
}
```

Use ID range and page size rather than offset where possible.

---

## Migration endpoint

```java
@PostMapping("/runs/{runId}/migrate")
public ManualMigrationResponse migrate(
        @PathVariable long runId,
        @RequestBody ManualMigrationRequest request) {

    int totalClaimed = 0;
    int migrated = 0;
    int alreadyMigrated = 0;
    int failed = 0;

    for (int i = 0; i < request.maximumBatches(); i++) {
        BatchMigrationResult result =
                migrationService.processNextBatch(
                    runId,
                    workerIdentity.getWorkerId(),
                    request.claimSize()
                );

        if (result.claimedCount() == 0) {
            break;
        }

        totalClaimed += result.claimedCount();
        migrated += result.migratedCount();
        alreadyMigrated += result.alreadyMigratedCount();
        failed += result.failedCount();
    }

    return new ManualMigrationResponse(
            totalClaimed,
            migrated,
            alreadyMigrated,
            failed
    );
}
```

Request:

```java
public record ManualMigrationRequest(
        @Min(1) @Max(500) int claimSize,
        @Min(1) @Max(20) int maximumBatches) {
}
```

Because this endpoint uses the same claim logic, it can run while schedulers are active without intentionally overlapping work.

---

# 21. Should the endpoint accept offset and limit?

For the 12:00–2:00 AM MariaDB status load:

```text
Accepting limit is fine.
Prefer lastCustomerId/range over offset.
```

For the 2:00–5:00 AM actual migration:

```text
Do not accept arbitrary offset.
Accept claimSize and maximumBatches.
```

Using offset against a changing status result can skip records.

Unsafe:

```sql
WHERE status is absent
OFFSET 100000
FETCH NEXT 100000
```

After earlier customers are inserted into the status table, the result set shrinks and the offset can jump over unprocessed customers.

---

# 22. Final operational timeline

## Before 12:00 AM

* Create migration run.
* Fix three-month start and cutoff timestamps.
* Populate `PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED`.
* Populate `CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED`.
* Create status-loader batch ranges.
* Validate counts and indexes.

## 12:00 AM–2:00 AM

* Twelve instances claim status-loader batches.
* Read existing migrated IDs from MariaDB.
* Insert `ALREADY_MIGRATED` into DB2.
* Commit every 5,000–20,000 IDs.
* Reconcile status-loader batches.

## 2:00 AM–4:30 AM

* Twelve instances continuously claim 100–250 customers.
* Recheck MariaDB.
* Read DB2 source personalisation rows.
* Upsert into MariaDB.
* Update DB2 status to `MIGRATED`, `ALREADY_MIGRATED` or `RETRY_PENDING`.

## 4:30 AM–5:00 AM

* Stop claiming large new batches.
* Reduce claim size to 50–100.
* Complete current work.
* Recover stale claims.

## 5:00 AM–5:30 AM

* Retry transient failures.
* Do not retry permanent validation failures automatically.

## 5:30 AM–6:00 AM

* Stop new claims.
* Reconcile all statuses.
* Produce failed-customer report.
* Mark run as `MIGRATION_COMPLETED`, `PARTIAL` or `FAILED`.

---

# 23. Reconciliation queries

## Overall status

```sql
SELECT
    MIGRATION_STATUS,
    COUNT(*) AS CUSTOMER_COUNT
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
GROUP BY MIGRATION_STATUS;
```

## Eligible customers without any status

```sql
SELECT COUNT(*)
FROM CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED c
WHERE c.RUN_ID = ?
  AND NOT EXISTS
  (
      SELECT 1
      FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS s
      WHERE s.RUN_ID = c.RUN_ID
        AND s.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID =
            c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
  );
```

## Stuck customers

```sql
SELECT
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    CLAIMED_BY,
    CLAIMED_AT,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

## Failed customers

```sql
SELECT
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    RETRY_COUNT,
    ERROR_CODE,
    ERROR_MESSAGE
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'FAILED'
ORDER BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID;
```

Validation equation:

```text
Total distinct eligible customers
=
ALREADY_MIGRATED
+ MIGRATED
+ FAILED
+ SOURCE_NOT_FOUND
+ RETRY_PENDING
+ IN_PROGRESS
+ customers without status
```

At final completion, ideally:

```text
IN_PROGRESS = 0
RETRY_PENDING = 0
customers without status = 0
```

# Recommended starting configuration

```yaml
migration:
  timezone: Asia/Singapore

  status-load:
    start: "00:00"
    end: "02:00"
    scheduler-delay-ms: 300000
    page-size: 10000

  execution:
    start: "02:00"
    stop-claim: "04:30"
    hard-stop: "05:00"
    scheduler-delay-ms: 5000
    candidate-read-size: 1000
    claim-size: 200

  retry:
    start: "05:00"
    end: "05:30"
    scheduler-delay-ms: 30000
    claim-size: 50
    maximum-retries: 3

  recovery:
    scheduler-delay-ms: 600000
    stale-claim-minutes: 15

  logical-partitions: 60
```

## Final conclusion

Your architecture is valid with this pattern:

```text
12:00–2:00
MariaDB existing IDs
    → DB2 CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
    → ALREADY_MIGRATED

2:00–5:00
DB2 CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED
    MINUS
DB2 CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
    → atomically claim as IN_PROGRESS
    → recheck MariaDB
    → migrate DB2 personalisation to MariaDB
    → MIGRATED / ALREADY_MIGRATED / RETRY_PENDING / FAILED
```

The two most important controls are:

```text
Unique primary key on RUN_ID + CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
and
Conditional status transitions before processing
```

These allow 12 schedulers and manual fallback endpoints to use the same migration services without normally processing the same customer concurrently.


Plan is 2 or 3 instances on both DC Is this achievable

Yes—**2 or 3 instances in each DC is achievable**.

That means:

| Configuration | DCE | DCW | Total workers |
| ------------- | --: | --: | ------------: |
| Minimum       |   2 |   2 |             4 |
| Recommended   |   3 |   3 |             6 |

Based on approximately:

```text
300,000 distinct customers
3.8 million personalisation records
Migration window: 2:00 AM–5:00 AM
```

## Required combined throughput

For customers:

```text
300,000 ÷ 3 hours
≈ 27.8 customers/second
```

For personalisation rows:

```text
3,800,000 ÷ 3 hours
≈ 352 rows/second
```

### With four instances

Each instance needs approximately:

```text
6.9 customers/second
88 personalisation rows/second
```

### With six instances

Each instance needs approximately:

```text
4.6 customers/second
59 personalisation rows/second
```

These figures are generally reasonable for a properly batched Spring Boot migration application, assuming DB2, MariaDB, network connectivity, and the existing migration logic can support the load.

# Recommended deployment

Use:

```text
DCE: 3 instances
DCW: 3 instances
Total: 6 instances
```

This gives more safety for:

* One instance restarting
* Uneven customer record sizes
* Temporary DB slowdown
* Retry processing
* Network latency between DC and database
* Seamless migration continuing concurrently

Start the migration at lower concurrency and increase gradually.

```text
2:00 AM
Start 2 instances in DCE + 2 instances in DCW

After 10–15 minutes
Check throughput, DB CPU, connection pools and failures

If stable
Enable the third instance in each DC
```

However, if all six instances are already deployed, you can control whether they actively process using configuration.

# Instance coordination

Do not assign permanent customer ranges directly based only on `CF_INSTANCE_INDEX`.

For example, avoid relying solely on:

```text
DCE-0 handles one range
DCE-1 handles another range
DCW-0 handles another range
```

If an instance crashes, its assigned range may remain idle.

Instead:

```text
All 4–6 instances
    ↓
Read available customer candidates
    ↓
Attempt atomic IN_PROGRESS insert
    ↓
Only successful claimant processes customer
```

The DB2 primary key prevents overlap:

```sql
PRIMARY KEY
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
)
```

Claim:

```sql
INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    MIGRATION_STATUS,
    CLAIMED_BY,
    CLAIMED_AT,
    CREATED_AT,
    UPDATED_AT
)
VALUES
(
    ?,
    ?,
    'IN_PROGRESS',
    ?,
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    CURRENT TIMESTAMP
);
```

Only one of the six workers can successfully insert that customer.

The others receive a duplicate-key result and move to the next candidate.

# Worker identity

Use:

```text
DC name
+ CF_INSTANCE_INDEX
+ CF_INSTANCE_GUID
```

For example:

```text
DCE-0-7db93...
DCE-1-4e201...
DCE-2-6aa82...

DCW-0-04f7c...
DCW-1-b98ae...
DCW-2-31ef0...
```

Configuration:

```yaml
MIGRATION_DC: DCE
```

or:

```yaml
MIGRATION_DC: DCW
```

Java:

```java
@Component
@Getter
public class WorkerIdentity {

    private final String workerId;

    public WorkerIdentity(
            @Value("${MIGRATION_DC:LOCAL}")
            String dc,

            @Value("${CF_INSTANCE_INDEX:0}")
            String instanceIndex,

            @Value("${CF_INSTANCE_GUID:LOCAL}")
            String instanceGuid) {

        this.workerId = String.format(
                "%s-%s-%s",
                dc,
                instanceIndex,
                instanceGuid
        );
    }
}
```

# Scheduler behaviour with six instances

Spring scheduler executes independently in every instance.

That is acceptable because claiming happens in DB2.

```java
@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "${migration.execution.delay-ms:3000}")
public void migrate() {

    if (!migrationWindowService.canClaimWork()) {
        return;
    }

    migrationService.processNextBatch(
            activeRunService.getActiveRunId(),
            workerIdentity.getWorkerId(),
            100
    );
}
```

Each instance:

1. Reads perhaps 300–500 unclaimed candidates.
2. Attempts to claim up to 100.
3. Processes only successfully claimed customers.
4. Updates their statuses.
5. Runs again after a short delay.

# Recommended batch sizes

Start conservatively:

```yaml
migration:
  execution:
    candidate-read-size: 400
    claim-size: 100
    scheduler-delay-ms: 3000
```

For six instances, maximum concurrently claimed customers:

```text
6 × 100 = 600 customers
```

But this does not necessarily mean 600 simultaneous database transactions. Each instance can process customers sequentially or with limited internal concurrency.

## Recommended internal concurrency

Start with:

```text
One scheduler thread per application instance
Customer processing threads per instance: 2
```

With six app instances:

```text
6 × 2 = 12 concurrent customer migrations
```

Later, increase to:

```text
3–4 processing threads per instance
```

only after checking database load.

Do not immediately configure:

```text
6 instances × 20 threads
```

That could create 120 simultaneous DB2/MariaDB operations.

# Connection pool starting configuration

Per application instance:

```yaml
DB2:
  maximum-pool-size: 8
  minimum-idle: 2

MariaDB:
  maximum-pool-size: 8
  minimum-idle: 2
```

For six instances:

```text
Maximum DB2 connections: 48
Maximum MariaDB connections: 48
```

Verify that both databases permit this safely.

If each instance uses two migration threads, a pool of 6–8 is generally a reasonable starting point because connections are also needed for:

* Scheduler queries
* Status updates
* MariaDB existence checks
* Health checks
* Retry processing

# Status-load phase: 12:00 AM–2:00 AM

The same 4–6 instances can be used.

However, reading all existing customer IDs from MariaDB and inserting them into DB2 can be database-intensive.

Use fixed batch-control ranges.

Example:

```text
60 status-load ranges
4–6 workers dynamically claim ranges
```

A range could be:

```text
Customer ID 1–500,000
Customer ID 500,001–1,000,000
...
```

Each worker:

```text
Claim range
    ↓
Read MariaDB IDs in pages of 10,000
    ↓
Batch MERGE into DB2
    ↓
Commit
    ↓
Update range checkpoint
    ↓
Claim next range
```

A 100,000 ID range is acceptable as a **logical assignment**, but process it internally in pages:

```text
Logical range: 100,000 customers
Page size: 5,000–10,000 customers
```

Do not read all 100,000 IDs into memory or commit them as one transaction.

# Estimated throughput by configuration

## Four total instances

Required per instance:

```text
Approximately 7 customers/second
```

This can work, but there is limited safety margin.

If one instance stops:

```text
Three remaining instances
≈ 9.3 customers/second each
```

That may still work, but pressure increases.

## Six total instances

Required per instance:

```text
Approximately 4.6 customers/second
```

If one instance stops:

```text
Five remaining instances
≈ 5.6 customers/second each
```

This is much safer.

Therefore:

```text
2 + 2 instances: technically achievable
3 + 3 instances: recommended production configuration
```

# Cross-DC considerations

You need to verify database connectivity from both DCs.

Check:

| Check                 | Requirement                                            |
| --------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| DB2 connectivity      | Both DCE and DCW can read and update control tables    |
| MariaDB connectivity  | Both DCs can read and write target tables              |
| Latency               | Comparable and low enough from both DCs                |
| Firewall              | Required DB ports open                                 |
| DB connection limits  | Support total pools from all instances                 |
| Clock synchronisation | All PCF cells and DB servers use synchronised time     |
| DNS/failover          | Both DCs resolve the same database endpoints correctly |

If MariaDB is physically closer to one DC, the other DC may process more slowly. Dynamic claiming naturally compensates: faster instances claim more customers.

# Failure scenario

Suppose:

```text
DCE instance 1 claims customer 123
MariaDB migration succeeds
Instance crashes before DB2 status becomes MIGRATED
```

The customer remains:

```text
IN_PROGRESS
```

Recovery scheduler later changes it to:

```text
RETRY_PENDING
```

When retried:

* MariaDB already has the data
* Existing-customer check detects it, or upsert handles it
* DB2 status becomes `ALREADY_MIGRATED` or `MIGRATED`

Therefore, MariaDB writes must remain idempotent.

# Practical success criteria

Before migration night, run a pilot of at least:

```text
10,000–25,000 customers
```

Measure:

```text
Customers/second
Personalisation rows/second
Average and p95 customer migration time
DB2 CPU and query time
MariaDB CPU and insert/update time
Connection-pool usage
Failure percentage
Average personalisation rows/customer
```

For six instances, the minimum acceptable combined pilot throughput should be:

```text
At least 35 customers/second
```

Prefer:

```text
40–50 customers/second
```

At 40 customers/second:

```text
300,000 ÷ 40
= 7,500 seconds
≈ 2 hours 5 minutes
```

That provides useful time for retries before 5:00 AM.

# Final recommendation

Deploy:

```text
DCE: 3 instances
DCW: 3 instances
```

Configure:

```text
Scheduler delay: 3–5 seconds
Claim size: 100 initially
Candidate read size: 400–500
Processing threads: 2 per instance initially
DB pools: approximately 8 per database per instance
Status-load page size: 5,000–10,000
Stale claim timeout: 10–15 minutes
Maximum retries: 3
```

Use all instances as equal dynamic workers:

```text
No permanent DC range ownership
No migration offset ownership
No dependency on instance index for data splitting
```

The combination of **DB2 atomic customer claims, idempotent MariaDB writes, small batches, and six distributed workers** makes the target achievable. The only part that cannot be confirmed from design alone is actual throughput; a production-like pilot must prove that the system can maintain at least approximately **35–40 customers per second combined**.


Now share the implementation code for this approach

Below is a Spring Boot implementation skeleton for:

```text
DB2 = BAU/source + migration control tables
MariaDB = new target application database

12:00 AM–2:00 AM
Load customers already present in MariaDB into DB2 status table

2:00 AM–5:00 AM
Claim remaining eligible customers from DB2 and migrate to MariaDB

5:00 AM–6:00 AM
Retry, stale-claim recovery and reconciliation
```

The implementation uses `JdbcTemplate` rather than JPA because it makes two-database transaction boundaries and DB2 claim SQL easier to control.

# 3. DB2 migration tables

## 3.1 Migration run

```sql
CREATE TABLE MIGRATION_RUN
(
    RUN_ID                    BIGINT NOT NULL,
    RUN_STATUS                VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,

    ELIGIBILITY_START_TIME    TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,
    ELIGIBILITY_CUTOFF_TIME   TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,

    STATUS_LOAD_STARTED_AT    TIMESTAMP,
    STATUS_LOAD_COMPLETED_AT  TIMESTAMP,

    MIGRATION_STARTED_AT      TIMESTAMP,
    MIGRATION_COMPLETED_AT    TIMESTAMP,

    TOTAL_ELIGIBLE_CUSTOMERS  BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    ALREADY_MIGRATED_COUNT    BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    MIGRATED_COUNT            BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    RETRY_PENDING_COUNT       BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    FAILED_COUNT              BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,

    CREATED_AT                TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                              DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT                TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                              DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    PRIMARY KEY (RUN_ID)
);
```

---

## 3.2 Personalisation snapshot

Replace the sample business columns with the actual personalisation composite key and data columns.

```sql
CREATE TABLE PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID                    BIGINT NOT NULL,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID       BIGINT NOT NULL,

    PERSONALISATION_TYPE      VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY       VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE     VARCHAR(4000),

    SOURCE_CREATED_AT         TIMESTAMP,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT         TIMESTAMP,

    PARTITION_ID              INTEGER NOT NULL,

    CREATED_AT                TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                              DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    PRIMARY KEY
    (
        RUN_ID,
        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
        PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
        PERSONALISATION_KEY
    )
);

CREATE INDEX IDX_PTBM_RUN_CUSTOMER
ON PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
);

CREATE INDEX IDX_PTBM_RUN_PARTITION
ON PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID,
    PARTITION_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
);
```

## 3.4 Customer migration status

```sql
CREATE TABLE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
(
    RUN_ID                  BIGINT NOT NULL,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID     BIGINT NOT NULL,

    MIGRATION_STATUS        VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,

    CLAIMED_BY              VARCHAR(200),
    CLAIMED_AT              TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT       TIMESTAMP,

    STARTED_AT              TIMESTAMP,
    COMPLETED_AT            TIMESTAMP,

    RETRY_COUNT             INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,

    SOURCE_RECORD_COUNT     INTEGER,
    INSERTED_RECORD_COUNT   INTEGER,
    UPDATED_RECORD_COUNT    INTEGER,

    ERROR_CODE              VARCHAR(100),
    ERROR_MESSAGE           VARCHAR(2000),

    CREATED_AT              TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                            DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT              TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                            DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    PRIMARY KEY
    (
        RUN_ID,
        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
    )
);

CREATE INDEX IDX_CMS_RUN_STATUS
ON CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
(
    RUN_ID,
    MIGRATION_STATUS,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
);

CREATE INDEX IDX_CMS_STALE
ON CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_STATUS
(
    RUN_ID,
    MIGRATION_STATUS,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT
);
```

Recommended statuses:

```text
ALREADY_MIGRATED
IN_PROGRESS
MIGRATED
RETRY_PENDING
FAILED
SOURCE_NOT_FOUND
```

---

## 3.5 Batch control for 12:00 AM–2:00 AM

```sql
CREATE TABLE MIGRATION_BATCH_CONTROL
(
    RUN_ID                   BIGINT NOT NULL,
    PHASE                    VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,
    BATCH_ID                 BIGINT NOT NULL,

    START_CUSTOMER_ID        BIGINT NOT NULL,
    END_CUSTOMER_ID          BIGINT,

    LAST_PROCESSED_ID        BIGINT,

    BATCH_STATUS             VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL,

    CLAIMED_BY               VARCHAR(200),
    CLAIMED_AT               TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT        TIMESTAMP,
    COMPLETED_AT             TIMESTAMP,

    READ_COUNT               BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    INSERTED_COUNT           BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,

    RETRY_COUNT              INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
    VERSION_NO               BIGINT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,

    ERROR_MESSAGE            VARCHAR(2000),

    PRIMARY KEY
    (
        RUN_ID,
        PHASE,
        BATCH_ID
    )
);

CREATE INDEX IDX_MBC_PENDING
ON MIGRATION_BATCH_CONTROL
(
    RUN_ID,
    PHASE,
    BATCH_STATUS,
    BATCH_ID
);
```

Statuses:

```text
PENDING
CLAIMED
IN_PROGRESS
COMPLETED
FAILED
```

# 4. Build the eligible source population

This can run before midnight or as a separate preparation activity.

```sql
INSERT INTO PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
    SOURCE_CREATED_AT,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT,
    PARTITION_ID
)
SELECT
    :RUN_ID,
    p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    p.PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    p.PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    p.PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
    p.CREATED_AT,
    p.UPDATED_AT,
    MOD(p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, 60)
FROM PERSONALISATION p
INNER JOIN CUSTOMER c
    ON c.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID =
       p.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
WHERE p.SOFT_DELETE = 0
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME >= :START_TIME
  AND c.LAST_LOGON_DATETIME < :CUTOFF_TIME;
```

Create the distinct customer list:

```sql
INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_TO_BE_MIGRATED
(
    RUN_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PARTITION_ID
)
SELECT DISTINCT
       RUN_ID,
       CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
       PARTITION_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION_TO_BE_MIGRATED
WHERE RUN_ID = :RUN_ID;
```

    username: ${DB2_USERNAME}
    password: ${DB2_PASSWORD}

    minimum-idle: 2

  mariadb:

    username: ${MARIADB_USERNAME}
    password: ${MARIADB_PASSWORD}

    minimum-idle: 2

      exposure:
        include: health,info,metrics,prometheus

# 7. Dual data-source configuration

```java
package com.company.personalisationmigration.config;

import com.zaxxer.hikari.HikariDataSource;
import javax.sql.DataSource;

import org.springframework.beans.factory.annotation.Qualifier;
import org.springframework.boot.context.properties.ConfigurationProperties;
import org.springframework.boot.jdbc.DataSourceBuilder;
import org.springframework.context.annotation.Bean;
import org.springframework.context.annotation.Configuration;
import org.springframework.jdbc.core.JdbcTemplate;
import org.springframework.jdbc.datasource.DataSourceTransactionManager;
import org.springframework.transaction.PlatformTransactionManager;

@Configuration
public class DataSourceConfiguration {

    @Bean(name = "db2DataSource")
    @ConfigurationProperties(prefix = "datasource.db2")
    public DataSource db2DataSource() {
        return DataSourceBuilder
            .create()
            .type(HikariDataSource.class)
            .build();
    }

    @Bean(name = "mariaDbDataSource")
    @ConfigurationProperties(prefix = "datasource.mariadb")
    public DataSource mariaDbDataSource() {
        return DataSourceBuilder
            .create()
            .type(HikariDataSource.class)
            .build();
    }

    @Bean(name = "db2JdbcTemplate")
    public JdbcTemplate db2JdbcTemplate(
            @Qualifier("db2DataSource")
            DataSource dataSource) {

        return new JdbcTemplate(dataSource);
    }

    @Bean(name = "mariaDbJdbcTemplate")
    public JdbcTemplate mariaDbJdbcTemplate(
            @Qualifier("mariaDbDataSource")
            DataSource dataSource) {

        return new JdbcTemplate(dataSource);
    }

    @Bean(name = "db2TransactionManager")
    public PlatformTransactionManager db2TransactionManager(
            @Qualifier("db2DataSource")
            DataSource dataSource) {

        return new DataSourceTransactionManager(dataSource);
    }

    @Bean(name = "mariaDbTransactionManager")
    public PlatformTransactionManager mariaDbTransactionManager(
            @Qualifier("mariaDbDataSource")
            DataSource dataSource) {

        return new DataSourceTransactionManager(dataSource);
    }
}
```

There is deliberately no distributed XA transaction. Recovery depends on:

* Idempotent MariaDB writes
* DB2 status reconciliation
* Retry handling
