
Compare with `SOURCE_ROW_COUNT`.

But row count alone may still be unreliable if transformation or deduplication occurs.

## Best check

Use a customer migration marker in MariaDB.

```sql
CREATE TABLE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_MARKER
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID    BIGINT NOT NULL,
    MIGRATION_VERSION      VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL,
    MIGRATED_AT            DATETIME(6) NOT NULL,
    SOURCE_ROW_COUNT       INTEGER,
    PRIMARY KEY
    (
        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
        MIGRATION_VERSION
    )
);
```

After all customer personalisation rows commit successfully, insert:

```sql
INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_MARKER
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    MIGRATION_VERSION,
    MIGRATED_AT,
    SOURCE_ROW_COUNT
)
VALUES
(
    ?,
    ?,
    CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6),
    ?
)
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
    MIGRATED_AT = VALUES(MIGRATED_AT),
    SOURCE_ROW_COUNT =
        VALUES(SOURCE_ROW_COUNT);
```

Then “already migrated” means the marker exists, not just any personalisation row.

This is much safer.

# 11. MariaDB migration transaction

Migrate one customer in one MariaDB transaction.

```java
@Transactional(
    transactionManager = "mariaDbTransactionManager"
)
public MigrationResult migrateCustomer(
        long customerId,
        List<StagePersonalisation> rows,
        String migrationVersion) {

    int processed = 0;

    for (StagePersonalisation row : rows) {
        mariaDbJdbcTemplate.update(
            """
            INSERT INTO TARGET_PERSONALISATION
            (
                CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
                PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
                PERSONALISATION_KEY,
                PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
                SOURCE_UPDATED_AT
            )
            VALUES (?, ?, ?, ?, ?)
            ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
                PERSONALISATION_VALUE =
                    CASE
                        WHEN VALUES(SOURCE_UPDATED_AT)
                             >= SOURCE_UPDATED_AT
                        THEN VALUES(
                            PERSONALISATION_VALUE
                        )
                        ELSE PERSONALISATION_VALUE
                    END,
                SOURCE_UPDATED_AT =
                    GREATEST(
                        SOURCE_UPDATED_AT,
                        VALUES(SOURCE_UPDATED_AT)
                    )
            """,
            customerId,
            row.type(),
            row.key(),
            row.value(),
            Timestamp.from(row.sourceUpdatedAt())
        );

        processed++;
    }

    mariaDbJdbcTemplate.update(
        """
        INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_MARKER
        (
            CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
            MIGRATION_VERSION,
            MIGRATED_AT,
            SOURCE_ROW_COUNT
        )
        VALUES (?, ?, CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6), ?)
        ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
            MIGRATED_AT = VALUES(MIGRATED_AT),
            SOURCE_ROW_COUNT =
                VALUES(SOURCE_ROW_COUNT)
        """,
        customerId,
        migrationVersion,
        rows.size()
    );

    return new MigrationResult(processed);
}
```

The migration marker and all personalisation rows commit together.

# 12. Status update after processing

## Already migrated

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
SET MIGRATION_STATUS = 'ALREADY_MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = NULL,
    ERROR_MESSAGE = NULL
WHERE MIGRATION_RUN_ID = ?
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND CLAIM_BATCH_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?;
```

## Migrated

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
SET MIGRATION_STATUS = 'MIGRATED',
    TARGET_ROW_COUNT = ?,
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = NULL,
    ERROR_MESSAGE = NULL
WHERE MIGRATION_RUN_ID = ?
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND CLAIM_BATCH_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?;
```

## Retryable failure

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
SET MIGRATION_STATUS =
    CASE
        WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < 3
        THEN 'RETRY_PENDING'
        ELSE 'FAILED'
    END,
    RETRY_COUNT = RETRY_COUNT + 1,
    CLAIM_BATCH_ID = NULL,
    CLAIMED_BY = NULL,
    CLAIMED_AT = NULL,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = NULL,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = ?,
    ERROR_MESSAGE = ?
WHERE MIGRATION_RUN_ID = ?
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

# 13. Scheduler

Do not create a new migration run every X minutes.

Create one run once, then let the scheduler claim batches from that run.

```java
@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class PersonalisationMigrationScheduler {

    private final ActiveMigrationRunService runService;
    private final CustomerWorkClaimService claimService;
    private final MigrationBatchService migrationService;
    private final WorkerIdentity workerIdentity;
    private final MigrationProperties properties;

    @Scheduled(
        fixedDelayString =
            "${migration.scheduler-delay-ms:3000}"
    )
    public void migrate() {

        if (!runService.isMigrationEnabled()) {
            return;
        }

        long runId = runService.getActiveRunId();

        ClaimedBatch claimed =
                claimService.claimNextBatch(
                    runId,
                    workerIdentity.getWorkerId(),
                    properties.claimSize()
                );

        if (claimed.customerIds().isEmpty()) {
            return;
        }

        migrationService.processBatch(
            runId,
            claimed.claimBatchId(),
            workerIdentity.getWorkerId(),
            claimed.customerIds()
        );
    }
}
```

Recommended scheduler delay:

```text
3–5 seconds
```

The scheduler should wait until the previous execution finishes because `fixedDelay` is used.

# 14. Batch processing service

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class MigrationBatchService {

    private final Db2StageRepository stageRepository;
    private final MariaDbMigrationMarkerRepository markerRepository;
    private final MariaDbCustomerMigrationService migrationService;
    private final CustomerWorkStatusRepository statusRepository;

    public BatchResult processBatch(
            long runId,
            String claimBatchId,
            String workerId,
            List<Long> customerIds) {

        Set<Long> alreadyMigrated =
                markerRepository.findMigratedCustomers(
                    customerIds,
                    "PERSONALISATION_V1"
                );

        for (Long customerId : alreadyMigrated) {
            statusRepository.markAlreadyMigrated(
                runId,
                customerId,
                claimBatchId,
                workerId
            );
        }

        List<Long> toMigrate =
                customerIds.stream()
                    .filter(id ->
                        !alreadyMigrated.contains(id))
                    .toList();

        Map<Long, List<StagePersonalisation>> rowsByCustomer =
                stageRepository
                    .findByCustomerIds(runId, toMigrate)
                    .stream()
                    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
                        StagePersonalisation::
                            customerSysGenId
                    ));

        for (Long customerId : toMigrate) {
            List<StagePersonalisation> rows =
                    rowsByCustomer.getOrDefault(
                        customerId,
                        List.of()
                    );

            if (rows.isEmpty()) {
                statusRepository.markFailed(
                    runId,
                    customerId,
                    claimBatchId,
                    workerId,
                    "SOURCE_EMPTY",
                    "No staging records found"
                );
                continue;
            }

            try {
                MigrationResult result =
                        migrationService.migrateCustomer(
                            customerId,
                            rows,
                            "PERSONALISATION_V1"
                        );

                statusRepository.markMigrated(
                    runId,
                    customerId,
                    claimBatchId,
                    workerId,
                    result.processedCount()
                );

            } catch (TransientDataAccessException ex) {
                statusRepository.markRetryPending(
                    runId,
                    customerId,
                    claimBatchId,
                    workerId,
                    ex.getClass().getSimpleName(),
                    ex.getMessage()
                );

            } catch (Exception ex) {
                statusRepository.markFailed(
                    runId,
                    customerId,
                    claimBatchId,
                    workerId,
                    ex.getClass().getSimpleName(),
                    ex.getMessage()
                );
            }
        }

        return new BatchResult(customerIds.size());
    }
}
```

# 15. Stale claim recovery

If an instance crashes after claiming customers:

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
SET MIGRATION_STATUS =
    CASE
        WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < 3
        THEN 'RETRY_PENDING'
        ELSE 'FAILED'
    END,
    RETRY_COUNT = RETRY_COUNT + 1,
    CLAIM_BATCH_ID = NULL,
    CLAIMED_BY = NULL,
    CLAIMED_AT = NULL,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = NULL,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = 'STALE_CLAIM',
    ERROR_MESSAGE =
        'Worker stopped before completing migration'
WHERE MIGRATION_RUN_ID = ?
  AND MIGRATION_STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT < ?;
```

Calculate the cutoff in Java:

```java
Timestamp staleBefore =
        Timestamp.from(
            Instant.now().minus(
                Duration.ofMinutes(15)
            )
        );
```

This is safer than embedding vendor-specific interval syntax.

# 16. Should status also be updated on each staging row?

You can, but it is usually unnecessary.

## Option A: Status only on customer work table

Recommended.

```text
CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
    MIGRATED
```

The staging rows remain immutable source data.

Pros:

* Fewer DB2 updates
* Lower locking
* Smaller indexes
* Easier customer-level transaction semantics

## Option B: Status on every personalisation staging row

```text
PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
    MIGRATED
```

Pros:

* Exact row-level audit
* Easy row-level failure reporting

Cons:

* Millions of additional updates
* Larger indexes
* Greater transaction-log volume
* Customer can become partially marked

For your case, keep status primarily at customer level.

Optionally add:

```text
CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK.SOURCE_ROW_COUNT
CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK.TARGET_ROW_COUNT
```

That provides sufficient reconciliation without updating every stage row.

# 17. Optimization recommendations

## A. Claim by customer, not row

Most important optimization.

Avoid splitting the same customer's records across workers.

## B. Batch MariaDB existence checks

One query per 50–100 customers rather than one query per customer.

## C. Use a migration marker

Do not decide `ALREADY_MIGRATED` only because one target row exists.

## D. Use keyset population

When initially exporting data into staging, process source rows by increasing source key rather than large offsets.

## E. Keep staging immutable

Do not repeatedly update 3.8 million staging records. Put changing statuses in the smaller customer work table.

## F. MariaDB batch insert/upsert

Within each customer transaction, use JDBC batch operations if customers have many rows.

```java
jdbcTemplate.batchUpdate(
    sql,
    rows,
    500,
    setter
);
```

## G. Avoid huge `IN` clauses

Claim 50–100 customers initially.

If your DB2 environment supports temporary tables efficiently, a session temporary table can be used later for larger batches.

## H. Fixed thread count

For six app instances:

```text
Start with one migration thread per instance
Then test two threads per instance
```

Do not use `parallelStream()`.

## I. Keep transactions short

MariaDB transaction:

```text
One customer
```

DB2 status transaction:

```text
One claim batch or one status update
```

Do not create one cross-customer transaction.

# 18. Throughput considerations

Assuming:

```text
3.8 million personalisation rows
300,000 customers
3-hour migration period
```

Required:

```text
27.8 customers/second
352 personalisation rows/second
```

With six instances:

```text
4.6 customers/second per instance
59 personalisation rows/second per instance
```

If a customer migration averages 200 ms:

```text
One sequential thread:
≈ 5 customers/second
```

Six sequential instances may approximately reach the target, but there is little safety margin.

Therefore:

```text
Start with 1 thread per instance
Test actual throughput
Move to 2 threads per instance if required
```

# 19. Pros

| Advantage                  | Explanation                                               |
| -------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------- |
| Stable source population   | Six-month eligible data is captured once                  |
| Easy restart               | Pending and retry records remain in DB2                   |
| Clear audit                | Run-level and customer-level outcomes are recorded        |
| Multi-instance safe        | Conditional status claims prevent overlap                 |
| Scheduler-friendly         | Each invocation claims a small unit of work               |
| Manual fallback supported  | Endpoint can call the same claim service                  |
| MariaDB idempotency        | Upsert and migration marker protect retries               |
| BAU isolation              | Source business tables are not updated                    |
| Predictable reconciliation | Candidate customer count can be matched to final statuses |
| Flexible scaling           | Two or three instances per DC can claim dynamically       |

# 20. Cons

| Disadvantage                              | Impact                                                 |
| ----------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------ |
| Large staging table                       | Additional DB2 storage for all exported rows           |
| Export can be expensive                   | Large join and insert may stress DB2                   |
| Snapshot may become stale                 | Changes after export may not be represented            |
| Extra work table required                 | More schema and orchestration code                     |
| No cross-database transaction             | MariaDB success and DB2 status update can diverge      |
| Additional indexes consume storage        | Necessary for fast claims and retrieval                |
| MariaDB existence check can be misleading | Use migration marker rather than any-row existence     |
| Retry complexity                          | Requires status transitions and stale recovery         |
| Data retention required                   | Old staging runs must eventually be archived or purged |
| Row-level snapshot may be unnecessary     | Customer-ID-only staging can be more efficient         |

# 21. Comparison with customer-ID-only staging

## Full personalisation snapshot

Your proposed design:

```text
Copy every personalisation row into staging
```

Best when:

* The source must be frozen
* Exact audit/replay is required
* DB2 BAU data may change during migration
* Migration must reproduce the exact extracted data

## Customer-ID-only snapshot

Alternative:

```text
Stage distinct eligible customers
Read live personalisation from BAU during migration
```

Best when:

* Latest source data is preferred
* DB2 storage must be minimized
* Source queries are fast
* Exact snapshot replay is not required

For 3.8 million rows, full staging is valid but more expensive.

# Final recommendation

Your design will work, but implement it as:

```text
One overall MIGRATION_RUN_ID
        │
        ├── PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
        │       Immutable six-month source snapshot
        │
        └── CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
                One row per distinct customer
                PENDING
                IN_PROGRESS
                MIGRATED
                ALREADY_MIGRATED
                RETRY_PENDING
                FAILED
```

Each scheduler execution should:

```text
1. Create CLAIM_BATCH_ID
2. Claim 50–100 customers atomically
3. Batch-check MariaDB migration markers
4. Mark completed customers ALREADY_MIGRATED
5. Read all staging rows for missing customers
6. Upsert one customer per MariaDB transaction
7. Insert MariaDB migration marker
8. Mark DB2 customer status MIGRATED
9. Mark failures RETRY_PENDING or FAILED
10. Release stale claims after timeout
```

This is significantly safer and faster than assigning a run ID to the first X arbitrary personalisation rows and processing them directly.


Run id is for the worker for the scheduler ( ideally dce|dcw_<cf instance index>_current date time

Understood. In your design, `RUN_ID` is **not the overall migration execution ID**. It is a **temporary worker/batch claim ID** generated by each scheduler instance.

Example:

```text
DCE_0_20260731T220415123
DCE_1_20260731T220415681
DCW_0_20260731T220416020
```

This can work. However, I recommend naming it `CLAIM_ID` or `BATCH_ID`, because technically it identifies one worker execution, not the overall migration run.

## Recommended naming

| Column         | Purpose                                                        |
| -------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `MIGRATION_ID` | Optional identifier for the whole six-month migration exercise |
| `CLAIM_ID`     | Unique value generated by one scheduler execution              |
| `CLAIMED_BY`   | Stable PCF worker identity                                     |
| `STATUS`       | Current processing state                                       |
| `CLAIMED_AT`   | Time the rows were claimed                                     |

If your existing terminology requires `RUN_ID`, you can continue using it. Functionally, treat it as a `CLAIM_ID`.

# Your intended workflow

```text
Scheduler starts on DCE instance 0
        │
        ▼
Generate worker run ID
DCE_0_20260731T220415123
        │
        ▼
Update first X unclaimed staging rows:
RUN_ID = generated value
STATUS = IN_PROGRESS
        │
        ▼
Read staging records where RUN_ID = generated value
        │
        ▼
Group records by CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
        │
        ▼
Check customer in MariaDB
        │
        ├── Exists → ALREADY_MIGRATED
        │
        ├── Not exists → migrate → MIGRATED
        │
        └── Error → FAILED / RETRY_PENDING
```

This pattern is achievable across:

```text
DCE: 2–3 instances
DCW: 2–3 instances
```

Each scheduler invocation generates a different `RUN_ID`.

# Recommended worker run ID

Do not use only:

```text
DCE_0_current-date-time
```

Use:

```text
DC + instance index + instance GUID fragment + timestamp + random suffix
```

Example:

```text
DCE_0_8F31A2_20260731T220415123_7B29
```

This prevents collision when:

* The same instance triggers twice within the same millisecond
* An app restarts and reuses the same instance index
* Multiple scheduler threads execute unexpectedly
* DCE and DCW clocks differ slightly

## Java generator

```java
@Component
public class WorkerRunIdGenerator {

    private static final DateTimeFormatter FORMATTER =
        DateTimeFormatter.ofPattern("yyyyMMdd'T'HHmmssSSS")
            .withZone(ZoneOffset.UTC);

    private final String dc;
    private final String instanceIndex;
    private final String instanceGuidFragment;

    public WorkerRunIdGenerator(
            @Value("${MIGRATION_DC:LOCAL}") String dc,
            @Value("${CF_INSTANCE_INDEX:0}") String instanceIndex,
            @Value("${CF_INSTANCE_GUID:LOCAL}") String instanceGuid) {

        this.dc = sanitize(dc);
        this.instanceIndex = sanitize(instanceIndex);

        String normalizedGuid = sanitize(instanceGuid);

        this.instanceGuidFragment =
            normalizedGuid.substring(
                0,
                Math.min(normalizedGuid.length(), 8)
            );
    }

    public String generate() {
        String timestamp = FORMATTER.format(Instant.now());

        String randomSuffix = UUID.randomUUID()
            .toString()
            .replace("-", "")
            .substring(0, 6)
            .toUpperCase(Locale.ROOT);

        return String.format(
            "%s_%s_%s_%s_%s",
            dc,
            instanceIndex,
            instanceGuidFragment,
            timestamp,
            randomSuffix
        );
    }

    private static String sanitize(String value) {
        return value == null
            ? "UNKNOWN"
            : value.replaceAll("[^A-Za-z0-9]", "")
                .toUpperCase(Locale.ROOT);
    }
}
```

Example:

```text
DCE_0_8F31A2B4_20260731T140415123_7B29AC
```

The timestamp above is UTC. You can use Singapore time instead, but UTC is generally cleaner for cross-DC processing.

# Important correction: do not claim arbitrary personalisation rows

The main concern remains the same.

Suppose:

```text
Customer 1001 has 15 personalisation records
```

Scheduler instance DCE-0 may claim the first 10 rows:

```text
RUN_ID = DCE_0_...
```

Scheduler instance DCW-1 may claim the remaining 5 rows:

```text
RUN_ID = DCW_1_...
```

Then both instances process customer 1001 simultaneously.

Therefore, the claim must include **all records belonging to the selected customers**.

# Option 1: Recommended customer work table

Maintain:

```text
PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
    One row per personalisation record

CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
    One row per customer
```

The scheduler claims customers from `CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK`.

Then it reads all stage rows belonging to those customers.

This is the safest and most efficient design.

## Customer work table

```sql
CREATE TABLE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID     BIGINT NOT NULL,

    STATUS                  VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL
                            DEFAULT 'PENDING',

    RUN_ID                  VARCHAR(100),

    CLAIMED_BY              VARCHAR(150),
    CLAIMED_AT              TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT       TIMESTAMP,

    RETRY_COUNT             INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,

    SOURCE_ROW_COUNT        INTEGER,
    TARGET_ROW_COUNT        INTEGER,

    COMPLETED_AT            TIMESTAMP,

    ERROR_CODE              VARCHAR(100),
    ERROR_MESSAGE           VARCHAR(2000),

    CREATED_AT              TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                            DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    UPDATED_AT              TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                            DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    PRIMARY KEY (CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID)
);
```

If you expect multiple separate migration exercises, include a global migration identifier in the primary key:

```sql
PRIMARY KEY
(
    MIGRATION_VERSION,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
)
```

For one-time migration, customer ID alone may be sufficient.

## Indexes

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_CMW_STATUS_CUSTOMER
ON CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
(
    STATUS,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
);
```

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_CMW_RUN_ID
ON CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
(
    RUN_ID,
    STATUS,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
);
```

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_CMW_STALE
ON CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
(
    STATUS,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT
);
```

# Populate the customer work table

```sql
INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    STATUS,
    SOURCE_ROW_COUNT
)
SELECT
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    'PENDING',
    COUNT(*)
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
GROUP BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID;
```

Use a duplicate-safe insert if this preparation step can be restarted.

# Scheduler claim flow

Each scheduler invocation:

```text
Generate RUN_ID
    ↓
Find next candidate customers
    ↓
Conditionally update each customer:
PENDING → IN_PROGRESS
    ↓
Read customers successfully updated by this RUN_ID
    ↓
Read all personalisation rows for those customers
    ↓
Process
```

# Claim implementation

## Step 1: find candidates

```sql
SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
WHERE STATUS IN ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
ORDER BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FETCH FIRST ? ROWS ONLY;
```

Because 4–6 instances can run this simultaneously, the same candidate IDs may be read by multiple workers. That is acceptable.

## Step 2: conditionally claim

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
SET STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS',
    RUN_ID = ?,
    CLAIMED_BY = ?,
    CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND STATUS IN ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING');
```

Only the worker receiving update count `1` owns the customer.

Other workers receive update count `0` and skip it.

## Java claim service

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class CustomerClaimService {

    private final JdbcTemplate db2JdbcTemplate;
    private final WorkerRunIdGenerator runIdGenerator;

    public ClaimedWork claimNextCustomers(
            String workerId,
            int claimSize) {

        String runId = runIdGenerator.generate();

        List<Long> candidates =
            db2JdbcTemplate.queryForList(
                """
                SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
                FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
                WHERE STATUS IN
                    ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
                ORDER BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
                FETCH FIRST ? ROWS ONLY
                """,
                Long.class,
                claimSize * 3
            );

        List<Long> claimedCustomers =
            new ArrayList<>(claimSize);

        for (Long customerId : candidates) {
            int updated = db2JdbcTemplate.update(
                """
                UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
                SET STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS',
                    RUN_ID = ?,
                    CLAIMED_BY = ?,
                    CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
                    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT =
                        CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
                    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
                WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
                  AND STATUS IN
                      ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
                """,
                runId,
                workerId,
                customerId
            );

            if (updated == 1) {
                claimedCustomers.add(customerId);
            }

            if (claimedCustomers.size() >= claimSize) {
                break;
            }
        }

        return new ClaimedWork(
            runId,
            workerId,
            claimedCustomers
        );
    }

    public record ClaimedWork(
        String runId,
        String workerId,
        List<Long> customerIds
    ) {}
}
```

# Read the claimed customers using run ID

You can retrieve ownership directly:

```sql
SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
ORDER BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID;
```

However, the Java method already has the successfully claimed IDs. Re-querying is optional.

Re-querying is useful when:

* The application crashes after claiming
* A manual recovery endpoint resumes a known run ID
* The claimed list is not retained in memory

# Read corresponding personalisation records

```sql
SELECT
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
    SOURCE_CREATED_AT,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN
(
    ?, ?, ?, ...
)
ORDER BY
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY;
```

Alternatively, join using the worker `RUN_ID`:

```sql
SELECT
    s.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    s.PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    s.PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    s.PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
    s.SOURCE_CREATED_AT,
    s.SOURCE_UPDATED_AT
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE s
INNER JOIN CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK w
    ON w.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID =
       s.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
WHERE w.RUN_ID = ?
  AND w.CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND w.STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
ORDER BY
    s.CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    s.PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    s.PERSONALISATION_KEY;
```

This lets the database resolve the entire scheduler batch based on `RUN_ID`.

# Scheduler implementation

```java
@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@Slf4j
public class PersonalisationMigrationScheduler {

    private final CustomerClaimService claimService;
    private final MigrationBatchService migrationBatchService;
    private final WorkerIdentity workerIdentity;
    private final MigrationWindowService windowService;
    private final MigrationProperties properties;

    @Scheduled(
        fixedDelayString =
            "${migration.scheduler-delay-ms:3000}"
    )
    public void execute() {

        if (!windowService.canMigrate()) {
            return;
        }

        var claimedWork =
            claimService.claimNextCustomers(
                workerIdentity.getWorkerId(),
                properties.claimSize()
            );

        if (claimedWork.customerIds().isEmpty()) {
            return;
        }

        log.info(
            "Worker {} claimed run {} with {} customers",
            claimedWork.workerId(),
            claimedWork.runId(),
            claimedWork.customerIds().size()
        );

        migrationBatchService.process(
            claimedWork.runId(),
            claimedWork.workerId(),
            claimedWork.customerIds()
        );
    }
}
```

# Batch service

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class MigrationBatchService {

    private final StageRepository stageRepository;
    private final MariaDbMigrationRepository mariaRepository;
    private final CustomerWorkRepository workRepository;

    public void process(
            String runId,
            String workerId,
            List<Long> customerIds) {

        Set<Long> existingCustomers =
            mariaRepository.findCompletedCustomers(
                customerIds
            );

        for (Long customerId : existingCustomers) {
            workRepository.markAlreadyMigrated(
                customerId,
                runId,
                workerId
            );
        }

        List<Long> customersToMigrate =
            customerIds.stream()
                .filter(id -> !existingCustomers.contains(id))
                .toList();

        Map<Long, List<StagePersonalisation>> dataByCustomer =
            stageRepository
                .findByCustomerIds(customersToMigrate)
                .stream()
                .collect(
                    Collectors.groupingBy(
                        StagePersonalisation::customerId
                    )
                );

        for (Long customerId : customersToMigrate) {
            List<StagePersonalisation> records =
                dataByCustomer.getOrDefault(
                    customerId,
                    List.of()
                );

            if (records.isEmpty()) {
                workRepository.markFailed(
                    customerId,
                    runId,
                    workerId,
                    "NO_STAGE_DATA",
                    "No staged personalisation data found"
                );
                continue;
            }

            try {
                int migratedRows =
                    mariaRepository.migrateCustomer(
                        customerId,
                        records
                    );

                workRepository.markMigrated(
                    customerId,
                    runId,
                    workerId,
                    migratedRows
                );

            } catch (TransientDataAccessException ex) {
                workRepository.markRetryPending(
                    customerId,
                    runId,
                    workerId,
                    ex
                );

            } catch (Exception ex) {
                workRepository.markFailed(
                    customerId,
                    runId,
                    workerId,
                    ex.getClass().getSimpleName(),
                    ex.getMessage()
                );
            }
        }
    }
}
```

# Status updates must include run ID ownership

## Migrated

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
SET STATUS = 'MIGRATED',
    TARGET_ROW_COUNT = ?,
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = NULL,
    ERROR_MESSAGE = NULL
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

## Already migrated

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
SET STATUS = 'ALREADY_MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

## Failure

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
SET STATUS =
    CASE
        WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < 3
            THEN 'RETRY_PENDING'
        ELSE 'FAILED'
    END,
    RETRY_COUNT = RETRY_COUNT + 1,
    COMPLETED_AT =
    CASE
        WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 >= 3
            THEN CURRENT TIMESTAMP
        ELSE NULL
    END,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = ?,
    ERROR_MESSAGE = ?
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

For `RETRY_PENDING`, clear the claim ownership:

```sql
RUN_ID = NULL,
CLAIMED_BY = NULL,
CLAIMED_AT = NULL,
LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = NULL
```

Otherwise, the next scheduler cannot claim the customer cleanly.

# Stale run recovery

Since your `RUN_ID` identifies a scheduler worker invocation, it is useful for recovering a whole abandoned batch.

Find stale runs:

```sql
SELECT
    RUN_ID,
    CLAIMED_BY,
    COUNT(*) AS CUSTOMER_COUNT,
    MIN(CLAIMED_AT) AS FIRST_CLAIMED_AT,
    MAX(LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT) AS LAST_HEARTBEAT
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
WHERE STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT < ?
GROUP BY
    RUN_ID,
    CLAIMED_BY;
```

Reset stale rows:

```sql
UPDATE CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
SET STATUS =
    CASE
        WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < 3
            THEN 'RETRY_PENDING'
        ELSE 'FAILED'
    END,
    RETRY_COUNT = RETRY_COUNT + 1,
    RUN_ID = NULL,
    CLAIMED_BY = NULL,
    CLAIMED_AT = NULL,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = NULL,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = 'STALE_WORKER_RUN',
    ERROR_MESSAGE =
        'Worker invocation stopped before completion'
WHERE STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT < ?;
```

# Could you use `RUN_ID IS NULL` instead of status?

Yes, but status should remain the main workflow indicator.

Avoid using only:

```sql
WHERE RUN_ID IS NULL
```

because final rows might also have a cleared run ID.

Use:

```sql
WHERE STATUS IN ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
```

The `RUN_ID` indicates ownership; `STATUS` indicates workflow state.

Recommended state rules:

| Status             | Run ID                |
| ------------------ | --------------------- |
| `PENDING`          | `NULL`                |
| `IN_PROGRESS`      | Mandatory             |
| `RETRY_PENDING`    | `NULL`                |
| `MIGRATED`         | Preserve final run ID |
| `ALREADY_MIGRATED` | Preserve final run ID |
| `FAILED`           | Preserve last run ID  |

Preserving `RUN_ID` for final statuses helps identify which scheduler execution completed or failed each customer.

# Recommended final model

```text
PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
    Immutable six-month personalisation data
    No worker RUN_ID required

CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK
    One row per customer
    RUN_ID = scheduler invocation/claim identifier
    STATUS = customer migration state
    CLAIMED_BY = stable PCF worker identity
```

Your worker `RUN_ID` format is valid:

```text
DCE|DCW + instance index + current timestamp
```

A stronger version is:

```text
DCE_0_CF-GUID-FRAGMENT_UTC-TIMESTAMP_RANDOM
```

The most important design decision is that this worker-generated `RUN_ID` should be assigned at the **customer work level**, not independently to the first arbitrary personalisation rows. This ensures all personalisation records for one customer are processed together by exactly one scheduler invocation.


What is the purpose of CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK table ? Will it not complicate ? One additional to maintain


Yes, it adds one more table. Its purpose is mainly to avoid splitting one customer’s personalisation rows across different workers.

But for your design, it is **not mandatory**.

You can keep only the staging personalisation table, provided the claiming SQL claims **all rows belonging to selected customers**, rather than simply updating the first X rows.

## Why I suggested `CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK`

Assume staging contains:

| Customer | Personalisation rows |
| -------- | -------------------: |
| 1001     |                   15 |
| 1002     |                    8 |
| 1003     |                   20 |

With this approach:

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET RUN_ID = ?
WHERE RUN_ID IS NULL
FETCH FIRST 20 ROWS ONLY;
```

the result could be:

```text
Worker DCE-0:
Customer 1001 → all 15 rows
Customer 1002 → first 5 rows

Worker DCW-0:
Customer 1002 → remaining 3 rows
Customer 1003 → first 17 rows
```

Now two workers may process customer `1002`.

`CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK` prevents this because there is only one row per customer to claim.

```text
Customer 1001 → DCE-0
Customer 1002 → DCW-0
Customer 1003 → DCE-1
```

After that, each worker reads all personalisation rows for its claimed customers.

## Does it complicate maintenance?

A little. You must:

* Populate the customer work table.
* Maintain customer-level status.
* Join it with the staging table.
* Clean up two tables after migration.

But it also simplifies:

* Claiming
* Retry
* Failure handling
* Customer-level status
* Multi-instance coordination
* Reconciliation

Because migration is customer-based, the control row naturally belongs at customer level.

---

# Simpler design without the extra table

You can safely avoid `CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK` by keeping the worker columns directly in the personalisation staging table.

The important condition is:

> Select X distinct customers first, then assign the same `RUN_ID` to all staging rows belonging to those customers.

Do not claim X arbitrary rows.

## Staging table

```sql
CREATE TABLE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
(
    STAGE_ID                  BIGINT NOT NULL
                              GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,

    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID       BIGINT NOT NULL,

    PERSONALISATION_TYPE      VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY       VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE     VARCHAR(4000),

    SOURCE_CREATED_AT         TIMESTAMP,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT         TIMESTAMP,

    STATUS                    VARCHAR(30) NOT NULL
                              DEFAULT 'PENDING',

    RUN_ID                    VARCHAR(120),

    CLAIMED_BY                VARCHAR(150),
    CLAIMED_AT                TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT         TIMESTAMP,

    RETRY_COUNT               INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,

    COMPLETED_AT              TIMESTAMP,

    ERROR_CODE                VARCHAR(100),
    ERROR_MESSAGE             VARCHAR(2000),

    CREATED_AT                TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                              DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    UPDATED_AT                TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
                              DEFAULT CURRENT TIMESTAMP,

    PRIMARY KEY (STAGE_ID)
);
```

## Indexes

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_PMS_CLAIM
ON PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
(
    STATUS,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
);
```

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_PMS_RUN
ON PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
(
    RUN_ID,
    STATUS,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
);
```

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_PMS_STALE
ON PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
(
    STATUS,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT
);
```

```sql
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX UK_PMS_SOURCE
ON PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY
);
```

If multiple migration exercises will use the same staging table, include a migration version or snapshot ID in this unique key.

---

# Safe claiming without the customer table

## Step 1: select X distinct customers

```sql
SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM
(
    SELECT
        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
        MIN(STAGE_ID) AS FIRST_STAGE_ID
    FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
    WHERE STATUS IN ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
    GROUP BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
)
ORDER BY FIRST_STAGE_ID
FETCH FIRST ? ROWS ONLY;
```

For example:

```text
Claim size = 100 customers
```

This returns customer IDs, not personalisation rows.

## Step 2: conditionally claim all rows for each customer

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS',
    RUN_ID = ?,
    CLAIMED_BY = ?,
    CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND STATUS IN ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING');
```

But there is still a subtle race.

Two workers may select customer `1002` simultaneously. Both can attempt the update. If the update works at row level, one worker should change all matching rows first. The second worker’s update then affects zero rows because the status is no longer pending.

However, the first worker should confirm that it claimed **all expected rows**.

A safer claim transaction is:

1. Count pending rows for customer.
2. Update all pending rows conditionally.
3. Compare the update count with expected count.
4. Accept ownership only when the counts match.

## Java claim code

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class StageCustomerClaimService {

    private final JdbcTemplate db2JdbcTemplate;
    private final WorkerRunIdGenerator runIdGenerator;

    @Transactional(transactionManager = "db2TransactionManager")
    public ClaimedStageBatch claimNextCustomers(
            String workerId,
            int customerLimit) {

        String runId = runIdGenerator.generate();

        List<Long> candidates =
            db2JdbcTemplate.queryForList(
                """
                SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
                FROM
                (
                    SELECT
                        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
                        MIN(STAGE_ID) AS FIRST_STAGE_ID
                    FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
                    WHERE STATUS IN
                        ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
                    GROUP BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
                )
                ORDER BY FIRST_STAGE_ID
                FETCH FIRST ? ROWS ONLY
                """,
                Long.class,
                customerLimit * 3
            );

        List<Long> claimedCustomers =
            new ArrayList<>(customerLimit);

        for (Long customerId : candidates) {
            int updated = db2JdbcTemplate.update(
                """
                UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
                SET STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS',
                    RUN_ID = ?,
                    CLAIMED_BY = ?,
                    CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
                    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT =
                        CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
                    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
                WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
                  AND STATUS IN
                      ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
                """,
                runId,
                workerId,
                customerId
            );

            if (updated > 0) {
                claimedCustomers.add(customerId);
            }

            if (claimedCustomers.size() >= customerLimit) {
                break;
            }
        }

        return new ClaimedStageBatch(
            runId,
            workerId,
            claimedCustomers
        );
    }
}
```

Because the update changes all eligible rows for one customer, another worker should receive update count zero.

---

# Read the batch using `RUN_ID`

```sql
SELECT
    STAGE_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
    SOURCE_CREATED_AT,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
ORDER BY
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    STAGE_ID;
```

Group them in Java:

```java
Map<Long, List<StagePersonalisation>> rowsByCustomer =
    stageRows.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
            StagePersonalisation::customerSysGenId
        ));
```

---

# Status updates without the work table

## Already migrated

Update all rows belonging to that customer and worker run:

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET STATUS = 'ALREADY_MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = NULL,
    ERROR_MESSAGE = NULL
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

## Migrated

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET STATUS = 'MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = NULL,
    ERROR_MESSAGE = NULL
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

## Retry

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET STATUS =
    CASE
        WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < 3
            THEN 'RETRY_PENDING'
        ELSE 'FAILED'
    END,

    RETRY_COUNT = RETRY_COUNT + 1,

    RUN_ID =
    CASE
        WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < 3
            THEN NULL
        ELSE RUN_ID
    END,

    CLAIMED_BY =
    CASE
        WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < 3
            THEN NULL
        ELSE CLAIMED_BY
    END,

    CLAIMED_AT =
    CASE
        WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < 3
            THEN NULL
        ELSE CLAIMED_AT
    END,

    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = NULL,

    COMPLETED_AT =
    CASE
        WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 >= 3
            THEN CURRENT TIMESTAMP
        ELSE NULL
    END,

    ERROR_CODE = ?,
    ERROR_MESSAGE = ?,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

This updates every staged personalisation row for the customer.

---

# Main downside of using only the staging table

Suppose one customer has 25 rows.

When migrated, DB2 performs:

```text
25 status-row updates
```

With the work table, it performs:

```text
1 customer status update
```

For approximately 3.8 million staging rows, using only the staging table means millions of updates for:

* Claiming
* Heartbeat
* Success
* Retry
* Failure

That increases:

* DB2 transaction log volume
* Index maintenance
* Locking
* Table growth
* Status-query cost

With a customer table, perhaps only 300,000 control rows are repeatedly updated.

---

# Comparison

| Area                            | Only staging table                  | Staging + customer work    |
| ------------------------------- | ----------------------------------- | -------------------------- |
| Tables                          | One                                 | Two                        |
| Schema simplicity               | Better                              | Slightly more complex      |
| Claim safety                    | Requires customer-group claiming    | Naturally customer-based   |
| Status updates                  | Every personalisation row           | One row per customer       |
| DB2 write volume                | High                                | Low                        |
| Retry handling                  | Row-group updates                   | Simple customer update     |
| Reconciliation                  | Requires `COUNT(DISTINCT customer)` | Direct                     |
| Partial customer risk           | Must code carefully                 | Much lower                 |
| Maintenance                     | One table                           | One additional small table |
| Performance at millions of rows | Acceptable but heavier              | Better                     |

# Practical recommendation

Since this is a finite migration and you want lower implementation complexity, you can avoid `CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK`.

Use this simplified design:

```text
One PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE table
Worker RUN_ID stored in the table
Claim X distinct customers
Update all rows for each claimed customer
Read by RUN_ID
Process by customer
Update all customer rows to final status
```

This is achievable and conceptually clean.

However, make these rules mandatory:

1. Never claim the first X personalisation rows.
2. Claim the first X **distinct customers**.
3. Update all rows for each selected customer.
4. Final MariaDB write must be idempotent.
5. Use a customer-completion marker or equivalent check in MariaDB.
6. Reset stale `IN_PROGRESS` rows for retry.
7. Every status update must include `RUN_ID`, `CLAIMED_BY`, and current status.

For roughly 3.8 million rows, the additional customer table is a performance optimization and control simplification—not an architectural necessity.


How much time will take ? Each record look 3 queries needed

Yes. If you execute **three separate database queries for every personalisation record**, the migration will likely take too long.

Assuming:

```text
Personalisation records: 3.8 million
Queries per record: 3
Total queries: 11.4 million
```

Typical three operations might be:

```text
1. Check MariaDB whether customer/data exists
2. Insert/update MariaDB
3. Update DB2 staging status
```

## Estimated time with record-by-record queries

| Average time per query | Sequential time for 11.4M queries | With 4 workers* | With 6 workers* |
| ---------------------: | --------------------------------: | --------------: | --------------: |
|                   2 ms |                         6.3 hours |       1.6 hours |       1.1 hours |
|                   5 ms |                        15.8 hours |       4.0 hours |       2.6 hours |
|                  10 ms |                        31.7 hours |       7.9 hours |       5.3 hours |
|                  20 ms |                        63.3 hours |      15.8 hours |      10.6 hours |

*This assumes perfect parallel scaling, which normally does not happen. Connection pools, commits, network latency, DB locks, indexing and application processing will add overhead.

Therefore, with six instances and realistic 5–10 ms average database query time:

```text
Likely duration: approximately 3–7 hours
```

That may not reliably fit a three-hour migration window.

# But it should not be three queries per record

The migration should be handled as:

```text
Per scheduler batch
    1 DB2 query to claim customers
    1 DB2 query to read all personalisation rows
    1 MariaDB query to check all customers
    Batch insert/upsert into MariaDB
    Batch status update in DB2
```

For example, claim:

```text
100 customers
Approximately 1,200–1,500 personalisation rows
```

Then execute:

```text
1. Claim 100 customers
2. Read approximately 1,200 rows from DB2
3. Check 100 customers in MariaDB using one IN query
4. Batch-upsert the missing rows
5. Batch-update DB2 statuses
```

This reduces database round trips dramatically.

# Record-level approach

For 1,200 personalisation records:

```text
1,200 × 3
= 3,600 database round trips
```

At an average of 5 ms:

```text
3,600 × 5 ms
= 18 seconds
```

That is only for one batch.

For 3.8 million rows:

```text
3,800,000 ÷ 1,200
≈ 3,167 batches

3,167 × 18 seconds
≈ 15.8 hours sequentially
```

With six workers, the theoretical value is around 2.6 hours, but actual duration will probably be longer.

# Batched approach

For the same 1,200 records, use approximately:

```text
1 claim operation
1 source-read query
1 MariaDB existence query
3–10 JDBC batch-upsert calls
1–2 DB2 batch-status updates
```

Approximately:

```text
7–15 database round trips per batch
```

Instead of:

```text
3,600 round trips
```

Even when the database work itself takes 300–800 ms per batch, the total time is much lower.

Example:

| Batch processing time | 3,167 total batches | With 4 workers | With 6 workers |
| --------------------: | ------------------: | -------------: | -------------: |
|               0.5 sec |              26 min |        6–7 min |        4–5 min |
|                 1 sec |              53 min |         13 min |          9 min |
|                 2 sec |         1 hr 46 min |         26 min |         18 min |
|                 5 sec |         4 hr 24 min |     1 hr 6 min |         44 min |
|                10 sec |         8 hr 48 min |    2 hr 12 min |    1 hr 28 min |

Actual results will depend heavily on MariaDB write speed and DB2 staging reads, but the batched design should comfortably outperform record-by-record processing.

# Recommended query pattern

## Query 1: Claim 100 distinct customers

```sql
SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM
(
    SELECT
        CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
        MIN(STAGE_ID) AS FIRST_STAGE_ID
    FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
    WHERE STATUS IN ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
    GROUP BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
)
ORDER BY FIRST_STAGE_ID
FETCH FIRST 300 ROWS ONLY;
```

Then conditionally claim customers. Even better, use a customer-level claim table, but your single staging-table approach can still work.

## Query 2: Read all claimed data

```sql
SELECT
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
ORDER BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID;
```

This is one DB2 query for the entire batch.

## Query 3: Check MariaDB for all customers

```sql
SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_MARKER
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN
(
    ?, ?, ?, ...
);
```

One query for 100 customers.

## Query 4: Batch insert/upsert

Do not call `jdbcTemplate.update()` inside a loop.

Use:

```java
mariaDbJdbcTemplate.batchUpdate(
    UPSERT_SQL,
    rows,
    500,
    (preparedStatement, row) -> {
        preparedStatement.setLong(
            1,
            row.customerSysGenId()
        );

        preparedStatement.setString(
            2,
            row.personalisationType()
        );

        preparedStatement.setString(
            3,
            row.personalisationKey()
        );

        preparedStatement.setString(
            4,
            row.personalisationValue()
        );
    }
);
```

With batch size 500:

```text
3.8M rows ÷ 500
= approximately 7,600 JDBC batches
```

Across six workers:

```text
approximately 1,267 batches per worker
```

If each batch takes 500 ms:

```text
1,267 × 0.5 sec
≈ 10.5 minutes per worker
```

If each batch takes two seconds:

```text
≈ 42 minutes per worker
```

Additional staging reads, claims and status updates will add time.

# Query 5: Update status by customer batch

Do not update each staging row separately from Java.

For migrated customers:

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET STATUS = 'MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

But this is safe only when the whole worker run succeeded.

When individual customers can succeed or fail differently, update by groups:

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET STATUS = 'MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN
      (?, ?, ?, ...)
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

Similarly:

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET STATUS = 'ALREADY_MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN
      (?, ?, ?, ...)
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

This turns 100 customer status updates into one or two statements.

# Estimated practical duration

For:

```text
3.8 million personalisation rows
Approximately 300,000 customers
6 worker instances
100 customers per scheduler batch
500 rows per JDBC write batch
```

A sensible expectation is:

| Implementation                          | Approximate duration |
| --------------------------------------- | -------------------: |
| Three queries per record                |           3–7+ hours |
| Per-customer queries, no batching       |          1.5–4 hours |
| Customer batching + record batch-upsert |        30–90 minutes |
| Well-tuned bulk loading                 |        15–60 minutes |

These are planning estimates, not guarantees. A pilot is required because database latency and MariaDB index-update cost dominate the result.

# Recommended pilot measurement

Run:

```text
10,000 customers
Approximately 120,000–150,000 records
```

Measure:

```text
Total elapsed time
Rows per second
Customers per second
Average DB2 read time
MariaDB batch-insert time
DB2 status-update time
Failure percentage
```

Then estimate:

```text
Full duration
=
3,800,000
÷ measured rows per second
```

To finish 3.8 million records in three hours, the minimum combined throughput is:

```text
approximately 352 records per second
```

To leave time for retry and reconciliation, target:

```text
500–700 records per second
```

With six workers:

```text
approximately 85–120 records per second per instance
```

That should be achievable with proper batch reads, batch upserts and grouped status updates. It is not safe to depend on three individual queries for each of the 3.8 million records.



How to optimize ? batch sql ? If 100000 per scheduler how it works Do we need to load all data first in key value pairs , all keys customer id fetch from Maria db then Exclude and use single update query for all those customers ? Other customers insert then success all add to a list and iodine status and failure one list and update status Or any other better solution? I want to use same staging table no additional table


Yes, your proposed approach is broadly correct:

```text
Claim a large batch
→ Read distinct customer IDs
→ Check MariaDB in bulk
→ Separate already-migrated and pending customers
→ Migrate pending customers
→ Collect success/failure IDs
→ Update DB2 statuses in batches
```

But **do not load and process all 100,000 personalisation rows as one transaction or one Java object graph**.

Use `100,000` as the **maximum logical workload assigned to one scheduler run**, then process that workload internally in smaller pages.

# Recommended approach using only the staging table

```text
PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
```

No `CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_WORK` table is required.

The staging table should contain:

```sql
STAGE_ID
CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
PERSONALISATION_TYPE
PERSONALISATION_KEY
PERSONALISATION_VALUE

STATUS
RUN_ID
CLAIMED_BY
CLAIMED_AT
LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT
RETRY_COUNT
COMPLETED_AT
ERROR_CODE
ERROR_MESSAGE
```

Recommended indexes:

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_STAGE_PENDING
ON PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
(
    STATUS,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    STAGE_ID
);
```

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_STAGE_RUN
ON PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
(
    RUN_ID,
    STATUS,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    STAGE_ID
);
```

```sql
CREATE INDEX IDX_STAGE_CUSTOMER
ON PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    STATUS
);
```

# What does “100,000 per scheduler” mean?

It should mean:

```text
One scheduler run may claim approximately 100,000
personalisation records.
```

It should not mean:

```text
Load all 100,000 records into memory
and execute one huge transaction.
```

Recommended internal processing:

```text
Scheduler run maximum:       100,000 rows
Customer claim chunk:        200–500 customers
MariaDB existence chunk:     500 customers
DB2 source read page:        2,000–5,000 rows
MariaDB JDBC batch:          500–1,000 rows
DB2 status update chunk:     200–500 customers
```

Example:

```text
RUN_ID:
DCE_0_20260731T143015123_A72F

Claimed records:
100,000 personalisation rows

Distinct customers:
Approximately 8,000 customers

Internally processed:
500 customers at a time
Approximately 6,000 personalisation rows at a time
```

# Full optimized flow

## Phase 1: Generate worker run ID

```java
String runId = runIdGenerator.generate();
```

Example:

```text
DCE_0_8F31A2_20260731T143015123_A72F
```

# Phase 2: Select candidate customers

Do not select the first 100,000 arbitrary rows.

Select distinct customers with their row counts:

```sql
SELECT
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    COUNT(*) AS PERSONALISATION_COUNT,
    MIN(STAGE_ID) AS FIRST_STAGE_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
WHERE STATUS IN ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
GROUP BY CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
ORDER BY FIRST_STAGE_ID
FETCH FIRST 10000 ROWS ONLY;
```

This may return, for example:

```text
10,000 candidate customers
120,000 personalisation rows
```

In Java, add customers until the total record count reaches approximately 100,000:

```java
List<Long> selectedCustomers = new ArrayList<>();
long selectedRowCount = 0;

for (CustomerCandidate candidate : candidates) {
    if (!selectedCustomers.isEmpty()
            && selectedRowCount
                + candidate.personalisationCount()
                > 100_000) {
        break;
    }

    selectedCustomers.add(candidate.customerId());
    selectedRowCount += candidate.personalisationCount();
}
```

This ensures that all rows belonging to one customer stay together.

# Phase 3: Claim customers atomically

Do this in smaller chunks, such as 200 customer IDs.

For each customer:

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS',
    RUN_ID = ?,
    CLAIMED_BY = ?,
    CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND STATUS IN ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING');
```

Use JDBC batch update:

```java
int[] results = db2JdbcTemplate.batchUpdate(
    """
    UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
    SET STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS',
        RUN_ID = ?,
        CLAIMED_BY = ?,
        CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
        LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
        UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
    WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
      AND STATUS IN ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
    """,
    selectedCustomers,
    200,
    (ps, customerId) -> {
        ps.setString(1, runId);
        ps.setString(2, workerId);
        ps.setLong(3, customerId);
    }
);
```

Two workers might initially select the same customer, but only one should successfully change that customer’s rows from pending to in progress. Db2 also supports `SKIP LOCKED DATA` for eligible isolation levels, which can reduce waits between concurrent workers, but the conditional status update must remain the final ownership check. ([ibm.com][1])

After claiming, derive the actual owned customers:

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

Do not assume all candidate customers were successfully claimed.

# Phase 4: Check MariaDB in bulk

Yes, fetch all claimed customer IDs and check MariaDB in chunks.

But do not query MariaDB once per customer.

For 500 customer IDs:

```sql
SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_MARKER
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN
(
    ?, ?, ?, ...
)
AND MIGRATION_VERSION = 'PERSONALISATION_V1';
```

Java:

```java
Set<Long> alreadyMigrated = new HashSet<>();

for (List<Long> chunk :
        partition(claimedCustomerIds, 500)) {

    alreadyMigrated.addAll(
        mariaDbRepository
            .findMigratedCustomerIds(chunk)
    );
}
```

Then calculate missing customers:

```java
List<Long> customersToMigrate =
    claimedCustomerIds.stream()
        .filter(id -> !alreadyMigrated.contains(id))
        .toList();
```

## Why use a migration marker?

Do not decide that a customer is completely migrated merely because one personalisation row exists.

Example:

```text
Source personalisation rows: 15
MariaDB rows:                 4
```

The customer exists in MariaDB, but the migration is incomplete.

A marker such as:

```text
CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
MIGRATION_VERSION
SOURCE_ROW_COUNT
MIGRATED_AT
```

is safer.

# Phase 5: Update already migrated customers in one batch

You do not need one update per personalisation record.

For chunks of 200–500 customer IDs:

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET STATUS = 'ALREADY_MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = NULL,
    ERROR_MESSAGE = NULL
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN
  (
      ?, ?, ?, ...
  );
```

For example, 3,000 already-migrated customers require:

```text
3,000 ÷ 500
= 6 update statements
```

Not 30,000 or 40,000 row-by-row Java updates.

# Phase 6: Read missing customer data in pages

Do not load all remaining records for the run into memory.

Read 200–500 customers at a time:

```sql
SELECT
    STAGE_ID,
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
    SOURCE_CREATED_AT,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN
  (
      ?, ?, ?, ...
  )
ORDER BY
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    STAGE_ID;
```

Then group that smaller result:

```java
Map<Long, List<StagePersonalisation>> recordsByCustomer =
    rows.stream()
        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
            StagePersonalisation::customerSysGenId,
            LinkedHashMap::new,
            Collectors.toList()
        ));
```

For example:

```text
500 customers
Average 12.6 rows/customer
Approximately 6,300 records in memory
```

That is safer than loading all 100,000 records.

# Phase 7: Insert into MariaDB

MariaDB supports `INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE`, which allows the write to remain idempotent during retries or concurrency. ([MariaDB][2])

```sql
INSERT INTO TARGET_PERSONALISATION
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT
)
VALUES
(
    ?, ?, ?, ?, ?
)
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE =
        CASE
            WHEN VALUES(SOURCE_UPDATED_AT)
                 >= SOURCE_UPDATED_AT
            THEN VALUES(PERSONALISATION_VALUE)
            ELSE PERSONALISATION_VALUE
        END,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT =
        GREATEST(
            SOURCE_UPDATED_AT,
            VALUES(SOURCE_UPDATED_AT)
        );
```

Use JDBC `batchUpdate`.

```java
@Transactional(
    transactionManager = "mariaDbTransactionManager"
)
public int migrateCustomer(
        long customerId,
        List<StagePersonalisation> records) {

    int[][] results =
        mariaDbJdbcTemplate.batchUpdate(
            UPSERT_SQL,
            records,
            500,
            (ps, record) -> {
                ps.setLong(
                    1,
                    record.customerSysGenId()
                );
                ps.setString(
                    2,
                    record.personalisationType()
                );
                ps.setString(
                    3,
                    record.personalisationKey()
                );
                ps.setString(
                    4,
                    record.personalisationValue()
                );
                ps.setTimestamp(
                    5,
                    Timestamp.from(
                        record.sourceUpdatedAt()
                    )
                );
            }
        );

    mariaDbJdbcTemplate.update(
        """
        INSERT INTO CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_MARKER
        (
            CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
            MIGRATION_VERSION,
            SOURCE_ROW_COUNT,
            MIGRATED_AT
        )
        VALUES
        (
            ?, 'PERSONALISATION_V1', ?,
            CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6)
        )
        ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
            SOURCE_ROW_COUNT =
                VALUES(SOURCE_ROW_COUNT),
            MIGRATED_AT =
                VALUES(MIGRATED_AT)
        """,
        customerId,
        records.size()
    );

    return records.size();
}
```

The transaction should normally be one customer:

```text
All rows for customer succeed
→ marker inserted
→ commit

Any row fails
→ rollback customer
```

This makes failure handling clean.

# Phase 8: Maintain success and failure lists

Yes, maintain lists—but only for the current internal page, not the whole 100,000-row scheduler run.

Example:

```java
List<Long> successCustomers =
    new ArrayList<>();

List<FailureCustomer> failedCustomers =
    new ArrayList<>();
```

For every customer:

```java
for (Map.Entry<Long, List<StagePersonalisation>> entry
        : recordsByCustomer.entrySet()) {

    Long customerId = entry.getKey();
    List<StagePersonalisation> records =
        entry.getValue();

    try {
        mariaMigrationService.migrateCustomer(
            customerId,
            records
        );

        successCustomers.add(customerId);

    } catch (TransientDataAccessException ex) {
        failedCustomers.add(
            new FailureCustomer(
                customerId,
                true,
                ex.getClass().getSimpleName(),
                ex.getMessage()
            )
        );

    } catch (Exception ex) {
        failedCustomers.add(
            new FailureCustomer(
                customerId,
                false,
                ex.getClass().getSimpleName(),
                ex.getMessage()
            )
        );
    }
}
```

After processing the current 500-customer page:

```text
Update success statuses
Update failure statuses
Clear both lists
Process next page
```

Do not wait until the entire 100,000-row run finishes to update statuses. Otherwise, a worker crash can leave a very large number of completed MariaDB customers still marked `IN_PROGRESS`.

# Phase 9: Update success status in chunks

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET STATUS = 'MIGRATED',
    COMPLETED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
    ERROR_CODE = NULL,
    ERROR_MESSAGE = NULL
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
  AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN
  (
      ?, ?, ?, ...
  );
```

Use customer chunks of 200–500.

```java
for (List<Long> successChunk :
        partition(successCustomers, 500)) {

    stageRepository.markMigrated(
        runId,
        workerId,
        successChunk
    );
}
```

# Phase 10: Failure status

Failures normally have different error messages. Therefore, one single `IN (...)` update is not enough unless every failed customer has the same error.

Use JDBC batch update, one update per failed customer:

```java
db2JdbcTemplate.batchUpdate(
    """
    UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
    SET STATUS =
        CASE
            WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < ?
                THEN 'RETRY_PENDING'
            ELSE 'FAILED'
        END,
        RETRY_COUNT = RETRY_COUNT + 1,
        RUN_ID =
        CASE
            WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < ?
                THEN NULL
            ELSE RUN_ID
        END,
        CLAIMED_BY =
        CASE
            WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < ?
                THEN NULL
            ELSE CLAIMED_BY
        END,
        CLAIMED_AT =
        CASE
            WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 < ?
                THEN NULL
            ELSE CLAIMED_AT
        END,
        LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = NULL,
        COMPLETED_AT =
        CASE
            WHEN RETRY_COUNT + 1 >= ?
                THEN CURRENT TIMESTAMP
            ELSE NULL
        END,
        ERROR_CODE = ?,
        ERROR_MESSAGE = ?,
        UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
    WHERE RUN_ID = ?
      AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
      AND CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
      AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
    """,
    failedCustomers,
    100,
    (ps, failure) -> {
        int maxRetries = 3;

        ps.setInt(1, maxRetries);
        ps.setInt(2, maxRetries);
        ps.setInt(3, maxRetries);
        ps.setInt(4, maxRetries);
        ps.setInt(5, maxRetries);

        ps.setString(6, failure.errorCode());
        ps.setString(
            7,
            truncate(
                failure.errorMessage(),
                1900
            )
        );

        ps.setString(8, runId);
        ps.setString(9, workerId);
        ps.setLong(10, failure.customerId());
    }
);
```

Although this is logically one update per failed customer, JDBC sends it as a batch and failures should normally be a small percentage.

# Recommended complete scheduler implementation

```java
@Scheduled(
    fixedDelayString =
        "${migration.scheduler-delay-ms:5000}"
)
public void execute() {

    String runId = runIdGenerator.generate();
    String workerId =
        workerIdentity.getWorkerId();

    ClaimResult claim =
        stageClaimService.claimCustomers(
            runId,
            workerId,
            100_000
        );

    if (claim.customerIds().isEmpty()) {
        return;
    }

    for (List<Long> customerPage :
            partition(
                claim.customerIds(),
                500
            )) {

        heartbeatService.update(
            runId,
            workerId
        );

        Set<Long> alreadyMigrated =
            mariaRepository
                .findMigratedCustomers(
                    customerPage
                );

        stageRepository
            .markAlreadyMigrated(
                runId,
                workerId,
                alreadyMigrated
            );

        List<Long> customersToMigrate =
            customerPage.stream()
                .filter(customerId ->
                    !alreadyMigrated
                        .contains(customerId))
                .toList();

        List<StagePersonalisation> rows =
            stageRepository
                .findRows(
                    runId,
                    workerId,
                    customersToMigrate
                );

        Map<Long, List<StagePersonalisation>>
            rowsByCustomer =
                groupByCustomer(rows);

        List<Long> successCustomers =
            new ArrayList<>();

        List<FailureCustomer> failures =
            new ArrayList<>();

        for (Long customerId :
                customersToMigrate) {

            try {
                List<StagePersonalisation>
                    customerRows =
                        rowsByCustomer
                            .getOrDefault(
                                customerId,
                                List.of()
                            );

                if (customerRows.isEmpty()) {
                    failures.add(
                        FailureCustomer
                            .nonRetryable(
                                customerId,
                                "NO_SOURCE_DATA",
                                "No source rows"
                            )
                    );

                    continue;
                }

                mariaMigrationService
                    .migrateCustomer(
                        customerId,
                        customerRows
                    );

                successCustomers
                    .add(customerId);

            } catch (
                TransientDataAccessException ex
            ) {
                failures.add(
                    FailureCustomer.retryable(
                        customerId,
                        ex
                    )
                );

            } catch (Exception ex) {
                failures.add(
                    FailureCustomer
                        .nonRetryable(
                            customerId,
                            ex.getClass()
                                .getSimpleName(),
                            ex.getMessage()
                        )
                );
            }
        }

        stageRepository.markMigrated(
            runId,
            workerId,
            successCustomers
        );

        stageRepository.markFailures(
            runId,
            workerId,
            failures
        );
    }
}
```

# Query count after optimization

Assume:

```text
100,000 personalisation records
8,000 customers
500 customers per internal page
```

Number of pages:

```text
8,000 ÷ 500 = 16 pages
```

Approximate DB operations:

## Claiming

```text
1 candidate customer query
Approximately 40 claim batch executions
```
