Assuming 200 customers per DB2 batch update.

## For 16 processing pages

Each page:

```text
1 MariaDB bulk existence query
1 DB2 staging-data read
1 DB2 already-migrated status update
Customer-level MariaDB transactions
1 DB2 migrated status update
1 JDBC failure batch
```

You are no longer doing:

```text
100,000 × 3
= 300,000 database calls
```

Instead, the major read/status round trips may be in the low hundreds. MariaDB customer transactions remain the main cost because preserving customer-level atomicity is valuable.

# Should all 100,000 customer keys be loaded first?

You may load the **distinct customer IDs**, but not all 100,000 personalisation objects.

For example:

```text
100,000 personalisation records
≈ 8,000 customer IDs
```

An `ArrayList<Long>` containing 8,000 IDs is small.

Recommended memory model:

```text
Keep:
8,000 customer IDs for the claimed run

Load:
Only 500 customers’ personalisation records
at a time
```

Do not keep:

```text
All 100,000 personalisation rows
All values
All errors
All success records
```

# Should you use one giant update for all customers?

Avoid one giant SQL with 8,000 values in an `IN` clause.

Use chunks:

```text
200–500 customer IDs per update
```

Reasons:

* Smaller SQL statement
* Lower parameter count
* Shorter lock duration
* Easier retry
* Less transaction-log pressure
* Reduced chance of statement timeout

# Recommended values

| Setting                           |  Initial value |
| --------------------------------- | -------------: |
| Logical records per scheduler run | 50,000–100,000 |
| Candidate customers fetched       |         10,000 |
| Customer claim JDBC batch         |            200 |
| Processing customer page          |        250–500 |
| MariaDB existence check           |            500 |
| MariaDB row batch                 |            500 |
| DB2 status customer chunk         |            500 |
| Scheduler fixed delay             |    3–5 seconds |
| Maximum retry                     |              3 |
| Stale-run timeout                 |     15 minutes |

I would initially start with:

```text
50,000 records per scheduler run
250 customers per processing page
500 rows per MariaDB JDBC batch
```

After the pilot confirms memory, DB2 locks, MariaDB CPU and throughput are healthy, increase to:

```text
100,000 records per scheduler run
500 customers per processing page
```

# Final recommended design

Using only your staging table:

```text
Scheduler
  │
  ├── Generate worker RUN_ID
  │
  ├── Select distinct customers with record counts
  │
  ├── Choose customers up to 100,000 total rows
  │
  ├── Claim all rows for those customers
  │
  └── Commit claim
       │
       ▼
Process 500 customers at a time
  │
  ├── Bulk-check MariaDB marker
  │
  ├── Bulk-update ALREADY_MIGRATED
  │
  ├── Read missing staging rows
  │
  ├── Group by customer
  │
  ├── Customer-level MariaDB transaction
  │
  ├── Collect successes and failures
  │
  ├── Bulk-update successes
  │
  └── Batch-update failures
```

Your success-list/failure-list idea is correct. The important improvement is to maintain those lists **per internal page of 250–500 customers**, update DB2 immediately after each page, and then clear the lists. This avoids large memory usage and limits recovery work if a PCF instance stops.

[1]: https://www.ibm.com/docs/en/db2-for-zos/12.0.0?topic=concurrency-improving-applications-that-tolerate-incomplete-results&utm_source=chatgpt.com "Improving concurrency for applications that tolerate ..."
[2]: https://mariadb.com/docs/server/reference/sql-statements/data-manipulation/inserting-loading-data/insert-on-duplicate-key-update?utm_source=chatgpt.com "INSERT ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE | Server - MariaDB"



Can split chunk and open async thread for each chunk and process ? Any risk ? Pros and cons ?

Yes, you can split a claimed scheduler batch into chunks and process those chunks concurrently using async threads.

That can improve throughput, but the concurrency should be **bounded and controlled**. Avoid creating one thread per chunk without limits.

# Recommended pattern

```text
Scheduler instance
    │
    ├── Claims up to 100,000 staging rows
    │   belonging to complete customers
    │
    ├── Extracts distinct customer IDs
    │
    ├── Splits them into chunks
    │
    └── Submits chunks to a fixed thread pool
            │
            ├── Thread 1 → customers 1–250
            ├── Thread 2 → customers 251–500
            ├── Thread 3 → customers 501–750
            └── Thread 4 → customers 751–1000
```

Each thread should independently:

```text
Bulk-check MariaDB
→ Read its staging rows
→ Process customer-by-customer
→ Collect success/failure IDs
→ Batch-update staging status
```

# Recommended concurrency

Assuming six PCF instances:

```text
DCE: 3 instances
DCW: 3 instances
```

Start with:

```text
2 async threads per instance
```

Total:

```text
6 instances × 2 threads
= 12 concurrent migration workers
```

After testing, you may increase to:

```text
3–4 threads per instance
```

But only if DB2, MariaDB and connection pools remain healthy.

Do not immediately configure:

```text
10 threads × 6 instances
= 60 concurrent workers
```

That can overload both databases.

# Suggested chunk sizes

For a logical scheduler run of approximately 100,000 personalisation rows:

```text
Distinct customers: approximately 7,000–10,000
```

Use:

| Setting                       |       Initial value |
| ----------------------------- | ------------------: |
| Async threads per instance    |                   2 |
| Customers per async chunk     |             200–500 |
| MariaDB existence-check chunk |             200–500 |
| MariaDB JDBC row batch        |                 500 |
| DB2 status update chunk       |             200–500 |
| Maximum scheduler claim       | 50,000–100,000 rows |

Example:

```text
8,000 customers
÷ 250 customers per chunk
= 32 chunks
```

With four threads:

```text
Thread 1 processes chunk 1
Thread 2 processes chunk 2
Thread 3 processes chunk 3
Thread 4 processes chunk 4

When a thread finishes,
it takes the next available chunk.
```

Use a thread pool queue rather than manually assigning an equal number of chunks to each thread.

# Important ownership rule

The main scheduler must claim the staging rows before starting async processing.

```text
PENDING
→ IN_PROGRESS
→ assign RUN_ID
→ commit DB2 claim
→ start async threads
```

Do not let async threads independently select unclaimed rows. Otherwise, threads within the same instance and workers in other instances may overlap.

Every status update must include:

```sql
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
```

This protects ownership.

# Spring Boot executor

Use a dedicated executor.

Do not use:

```java
CompletableFuture.runAsync(...)
```

without specifying an executor, because it uses the shared common fork-join pool.

Configure a bounded pool:

```java
@Configuration
@EnableAsync
public class MigrationExecutorConfiguration {

    @Bean(name = "migrationExecutor")
    public ThreadPoolTaskExecutor migrationExecutor() {

        ThreadPoolTaskExecutor executor =
            new ThreadPoolTaskExecutor();

        executor.setCorePoolSize(2);
        executor.setMaxPoolSize(2);
        executor.setQueueCapacity(50);

        executor.setThreadNamePrefix(
            "personalisation-migration-"
        );

        executor.setWaitForTasksToCompleteOnShutdown(true);
        executor.setAwaitTerminationSeconds(60);

        executor.setRejectedExecutionHandler(
            new ThreadPoolExecutor.CallerRunsPolicy()
        );

        executor.initialize();

        return executor;
    }
}
```

`CallerRunsPolicy` provides backpressure. When the queue is full, the submitting scheduler thread processes the work rather than dropping it.

# Async chunk service

Keep the async method in a separate Spring bean.

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
public class MigrationChunkProcessor {

    private final MariaDbRepository mariaDbRepository;
    private final StageRepository stageRepository;
    private final CustomerMigrationService customerMigrationService;

    @Async("migrationExecutor")
    public CompletableFuture<ChunkResult> processChunk(
            String runId,
            String workerId,
            List<Long> customerIds) {

        List<Long> migrated = new ArrayList<>();
        List<Long> alreadyMigrated = new ArrayList<>();
        List<CustomerFailure> failures = new ArrayList<>();

        try {
            Set<Long> existingCustomers =
                mariaDbRepository.findMigratedCustomers(
                    customerIds
                );

            alreadyMigrated.addAll(existingCustomers);

            List<Long> pendingCustomers =
                customerIds.stream()
                    .filter(id ->
                        !existingCustomers.contains(id))
                    .toList();

            List<StagePersonalisation> stagingRows =
                stageRepository.findRows(
                    runId,
                    workerId,
                    pendingCustomers
                );

            Map<Long, List<StagePersonalisation>>
                rowsByCustomer =
                    stagingRows.stream()
                        .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
                            StagePersonalisation::customerId
                        ));

            for (Long customerId : pendingCustomers) {
                try {
                    List<StagePersonalisation> rows =
                        rowsByCustomer.getOrDefault(
                            customerId,
                            List.of()
                        );

                    customerMigrationService
                        .migrateCustomer(
                            customerId,
                            rows
                        );

                    migrated.add(customerId);

                } catch (Exception ex) {
                    failures.add(
                        CustomerFailure.from(
                            customerId,
                            ex
                        )
                    );
                }
            }

            stageRepository.markAlreadyMigrated(
                runId,
                workerId,
                alreadyMigrated
            );

            stageRepository.markMigrated(
                runId,
                workerId,
                migrated
            );

            stageRepository.markFailures(
                runId,
                workerId,
                failures
            );

            return CompletableFuture.completedFuture(
                new ChunkResult(
                    migrated.size(),
                    alreadyMigrated.size(),
                    failures.size()
                )
            );

        } catch (Exception ex) {
            return CompletableFuture.failedFuture(ex);
        }
    }
}
```

# Scheduler orchestration

```java
public void processClaimedRun(
        String runId,
        String workerId,
        List<Long> customerIds) {

    List<List<Long>> chunks =
        partition(customerIds, 250);

    List<CompletableFuture<ChunkResult>> futures =
        chunks.stream()
            .map(chunk ->
                chunkProcessor.processChunk(
                    runId,
                    workerId,
                    chunk
                )
            )
            .toList();

    CompletableFuture.allOf(
        futures.toArray(
            CompletableFuture[]::new
        )
    ).join();
}
```

However, submitting all 32 or 40 chunks at once can fill the queue. That is acceptable only with a bounded queue and backpressure.

A safer method is to submit only a limited number at a time.

# Better bounded execution

```java
Semaphore permits = new Semaphore(4);

List<CompletableFuture<ChunkResult>> futures =
    new ArrayList<>();

for (List<Long> chunk : chunks) {
    permits.acquireUninterruptibly();

    CompletableFuture<ChunkResult> future =
        chunkProcessor
            .processChunk(
                runId,
                workerId,
                chunk
            )
            .whenComplete(
                (result, error) ->
                    permits.release()
            );

    futures.add(future);
}

CompletableFuture.allOf(
    futures.toArray(
        CompletableFuture[]::new
    )
).join();
```

The executor already limits active threads, so the semaphore is optional. It is useful when you also want to restrict queued work and memory usage.

# Transaction boundaries

Do not place one transaction around the whole async batch.

This will not work correctly:

```java
@Transactional
public void scheduler() {
    claimRows();
    startAsyncThreads();
}
```

The parent transaction does not automatically extend into async threads.

Recommended transaction boundaries:

## DB2 claim

```text
One short DB2 transaction
→ assign RUN_ID
→ commit
```

## MariaDB migration

```text
One MariaDB transaction per customer
```

## DB2 status update

```text
One short DB2 transaction per chunk/status batch
```

Do not use one transaction for all 100,000 rows.

# Risks

## 1. Database overload

The biggest risk is too much parallelism.

Every active thread may require:

* One DB2 connection
* One MariaDB connection
* CPU on both databases
* Transaction log capacity
* Index updates

With six instances and four threads each:

```text
24 simultaneous workers
```

If each worker uses both DBs, you may need approximately:

```text
24 DB2 connections
24 MariaDB connections
```

plus scheduler, health checks and application BAU traffic.

## 2. Connection pool exhaustion

Suppose each application has:

```text
Migration threads: 4
DB2 pool maximum: 4
MariaDB pool maximum: 4
```

Any extra scheduler or health-query connection may block.

Use some headroom:

```text
2 migration threads
DB2 pool: 6–8
MariaDB pool: 6–8
```

Do not make the executor pool larger than the usable DB connection capacity.

## 3. Duplicate processing

Duplicate processing can happen when:

* Two threads receive overlapping customer IDs
* Two application instances claim the same customer incorrectly
* A stale recovery job resets work while the original thread is still processing

Protection:

```text
Claim complete customers
Use RUN_ID ownership
Use status condition
Use MariaDB unique keys
Use idempotent upsert
Use migration-completion marker
```

## 4. Partial completion

One chunk may succeed while another fails.

That is acceptable if status is updated independently for each chunk/customer.

Do not treat the entire scheduler `RUN_ID` as all-or-nothing.

A single run may contain:

```text
MIGRATED: 7,500 customers
ALREADY_MIGRATED: 400
RETRY_PENDING: 80
FAILED: 20
```

## 5. Worker crash

If the application stops while async chunks are running, staging rows remain `IN_PROGRESS`.

Use:

```text
LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT
```

But be careful with the stale timeout. A large chunk may legitimately take several minutes.

Update heartbeat while processing:

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

Run every one or two minutes while the scheduler run is active.

Use a stale threshold such as:

```text
15–30 minutes
```

not two or three minutes.

## 6. Unbounded memory usage

If all chunks load staging rows simultaneously, memory usage increases.

Example:

```text
4 threads
× 500 customers
× 13 rows/customer
= approximately 26,000 personalisation records in memory
```

Usually acceptable.

But:

```text
20 threads
× 1,000 customers
× large values
```

can consume substantial heap.

## 7. Ordering

Async execution does not preserve customer ordering.

This is normally not a problem because each customer is independent.

Do not use async processing when customer A must complete before customer B.

## 8. Error handling

Exceptions inside async tasks are not automatically thrown in the scheduler thread unless you inspect or join the futures.

Always use:

```java
CompletableFuture.allOf(...).join();
```

and inspect failed futures.

Also make sure each customer exception is handled so one failed customer does not stop the whole chunk.

## 9. Thread-safety

Never share mutable lists between async threads.

Unsafe:

```java
List<Long> globalSuccessList = new ArrayList<>();
```

Each thread writing to it can corrupt state.

Use local lists inside each async chunk and return a result object.

## 10. Scheduler overlap

If the scheduler uses `fixedRate`, another invocation may start while the previous one is still processing.

Prefer:

```java
@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "...")
```

Even with `fixedDelay`, the scheduler method can return immediately after submitting async tasks. Therefore, wait for the futures before returning, or maintain an instance-level guard.

```java
private final AtomicBoolean running =
    new AtomicBoolean(false);
```

```java
@Scheduled(fixedDelayString = "5000")
public void execute() {

    if (!running.compareAndSet(false, true)) {
        return;
    }

    try {
        executeMigration();
    } finally {
        running.set(false);
    }
}
```

This prevents the same PCF instance from starting a second 100,000-row run while the first one is still active.

Other PCF instances may continue processing their own batches.

# Pros

| Benefit                        | Explanation                                      |
| ------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------ |
| Higher throughput              | DB and network waiting can overlap               |
| Better resource usage          | Threads can work while another query is waiting  |
| Shorter migration duration     | Independent customers process concurrently       |
| Flexible scaling               | Tune threads without changing business logic     |
| Failure isolation              | One chunk can fail without stopping others       |
| Fits multi-instance deployment | Each instance contributes controlled parallelism |

# Cons

| Drawback                      | Explanation                                        |
| ----------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| More operational complexity   | Thread pools, futures and shutdown must be handled |
| Higher DB load                | Parallel queries and writes can create spikes      |
| More connections              | Each active thread may use both data sources       |
| Harder debugging              | Logs from multiple chunks interleave               |
| Stale recovery complexity     | Must avoid resetting genuinely active work         |
| Partial-run outcomes          | One run can contain several final statuses         |
| Memory grows with concurrency | Each thread loads its own data page                |
| Risk of scheduler overlap     | Must guard against concurrent runs in one instance |

# Recommended configuration

For your initial production pilot:

```yaml
migration:
  scheduler:
    fixed-delay-ms: 5000
    maximum-records-per-run: 50000

  processing:
    async-threads: 2
    customer-chunk-size: 250
    maria-existence-chunk-size: 250
    maria-row-batch-size: 500
    db2-status-chunk-size: 250
    stale-timeout-minutes: 20
```

With six application instances:

```text
2 threads × 6 instances
= 12 concurrent workers
```

After measuring, increase one setting at a time:

```text
First:
50,000 → 100,000 logical rows per run

Then:
2 → 3 async threads per instance

Then:
250 → 500 customers per chunk
```

Do not increase all three simultaneously, because you will not know which change caused DB load or failures.

# Final recommendation

Use async chunking, but follow this model:

```text
Claim once at customer level
        ↓
Commit claim
        ↓
Split customer IDs into 250–500-sized chunks
        ↓
Submit through a bounded fixed thread pool
        ↓
Each chunk bulk-checks MariaDB
        ↓
Each customer migrates in its own MariaDB transaction
        ↓
Each chunk batch-updates DB2 status
        ↓
Wait for all futures
        ↓
Finish scheduler invocation
```

Start with **two async threads per application instance**. This gives meaningful parallelism without immediately creating excessive DB contention.



Pls note 100000 itself unique


Understood — the `100,000` refers to **100,000 unique customers**, not 100,000 personalisation rows.

That changes the sizing significantly.

Assuming roughly:

```text
100,000 customers
Average personalisation rows/customer ≈ 12–13
Total staging rows ≈ 1.2M–1.3M
```

So one scheduler run claiming 100,000 customers is a very large workload.

## Recommended handling

Do not load all personalisation data for those 100,000 customers into memory.

Use this flow:

```text
Claim 100,000 unique customers
        ↓
Keep only 100,000 customer IDs in memory
        ↓
Split into customer chunks
        ↓
Process chunks asynchronously
```

Suggested settings:

| Setting                             | Recommended starting value |
| ----------------------------------- | -------------------------: |
| Customers claimed per scheduler run |                    100,000 |
| Customers per processing chunk      |                    250–500 |
| Async threads per instance          |                          2 |
| MariaDB existence-check size        |        500–1,000 customers |
| MariaDB insert batch size           |             500–1,000 rows |
| DB2 status update size              |          250–500 customers |

For example:

```text
100,000 customers
÷ 500 customers/chunk
= 200 chunks
```

With 2 async threads:

```text
Thread 1 processes one chunk
Thread 2 processes another chunk
Completed threads pick next chunks
```

## MariaDB existence check

Yes, fetch customer IDs from MariaDB in bulk, but not all 100,000 in one `IN` query.

Split into chunks:

```text
100,000 customer IDs
÷ 1,000 IDs/query
= 100 MariaDB existence queries
```

Example:

```sql
SELECT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM CUSTOMER_MIGRATION_MARKER
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN (?, ?, ...);
```

Build:

```java
Set<Long> alreadyMigratedCustomerIds
```

Then:

```java
List<Long> customersToMigrate =
    claimedCustomerIds.stream()
        .filter(id -> !alreadyMigratedCustomerIds.contains(id))
        .toList();
```

Keeping 100,000 `Long` IDs in a `HashSet` is reasonable. Keeping 1.3 million full personalisation objects is not.

## Better memory approach

You do not necessarily need to first load all 100,000 existing-customer results into one global set.

Each processing chunk can independently do:

```text
Take 500 customer IDs
    ↓
Check those 500 in MariaDB
    ↓
Mark existing customers ALREADY_MIGRATED
    ↓
Read staging rows only for missing customers
    ↓
Migrate
    ↓
Update statuses
```

This is safer and simpler.

```text
100,000 customer IDs remain as lightweight IDs
Only approximately 500 customers’ data is loaded at once
```

## Async architecture

```text
Scheduler claims 100,000 customers
             │
             ▼
Split into 200 chunks of 500 customers
             │
             ▼
Bounded executor with 2–4 threads
             │
      ┌──────┴──────┐
      ▼             ▼
   Chunk 1        Chunk 2
      │             │
MariaDB check   MariaDB check
DB2 read        DB2 read
Migrate         Migrate
Status update   Status update
```

Do not create 200 threads. Create 200 tasks submitted to a fixed pool of perhaps 2 threads.

## Status lists

Your idea is correct, but keep the lists per chunk:

```java
List<Long> alreadyMigrated = new ArrayList<>();
List<Long> migrated = new ArrayList<>();
List<FailureCustomer> failed = new ArrayList<>();
```

For each 500-customer chunk:

```text
Bulk-check MariaDB
→ populate alreadyMigrated list
→ migrate remaining customers
→ populate migrated and failed lists
→ batch-update staging
→ clear lists
```

Do not maintain one enormous success/failure list for the full 100,000 customers and update only at the end. If the instance crashes near completion, thousands of successfully migrated customers may still remain `IN_PROGRESS`.

## One concern with claiming 100,000 customers

When one PCF instance claims 100,000 customers, those customers become unavailable to other instances until that worker finishes or stale recovery resets them.

Suppose there are six application instances:

```text
6 instances × 100,000 customers
= up to 600,000 simultaneously claimed customers
```

But only approximately 300,000 customers remain in your migration estimate. Therefore, the first few scheduler executions could claim almost the entire backlog.

That creates risks:

* Large recovery scope if an instance crashes
* Long `IN_PROGRESS` lifetime
* Large claim transaction/logging
* Uneven distribution between instances
* Harder operational control

## Better claim size

Even though the source query returns 100,000 unique customers, consider processing them as smaller logical claims:

```text
Claim 10,000–25,000 customers per RUN_ID
```

For example:

```text
25,000 customers
÷ 500 customers/chunk
= 50 chunks
```

With two async threads, the scheduler run is more manageable.

Recommended progression:

```text
Initial pilot: 5,000 customers/run
Next:          10,000 customers/run
Production:    20,000–25,000 customers/run
Maximum:       100,000 only after load testing
```

## If 100,000 per scheduler is mandatory

Then ensure:

1. Claiming is committed before async processing.
2. Customer IDs are split into 250–500-sized chunks.
3. Only 2–4 chunks execute concurrently.
4. Heartbeat is updated continuously.
5. Status is committed after every chunk.
6. Stale timeout is long enough, likely 30–60 minutes.
7. Recovery resets only genuinely abandoned runs.
8. The MariaDB and DB2 connection pools support the chosen concurrency.
9. The executor queue is bounded.
10. The application has graceful shutdown handling.

## Revised recommendation

```text
100,000 unique customers claimed
        ↓
Split into 200 chunks of 500
        ↓
2 async threads process chunks
        ↓
Each chunk:
    1 MariaDB bulk existence query
    1 DB2 staging-data query
    MariaDB batch upserts/customer transactions
    1 DB2 success update
    1 DB2 already-migrated update
    JDBC batch for failures
```

The key optimization is:

> Keep the 100,000 customer IDs only. Never load all corresponding personalisation rows together. Process personalisation data chunk-by-chunk.



Share the full implementation and code

I prepared a complete Spring Boot reference implementation based on these requirements:

* One DB2 staging table only
* Up to **100,000 unique customers per scheduler run**
* Customer-level claiming using `RUN_ID`
* Complete customer rows stay together
* Fixed, bounded asynchronous executor
* 500 customers per processing chunk
* Two worker threads per application instance by default
* MariaDB bulk existence checking as an optional setting
* MariaDB idempotent batch upsert
* Fast chunk-level MariaDB transaction
* Automatic customer-level fallback when a chunk fails
* DB2 success, already-migrated and failure status updates
* Retry count and terminal failure handling
* Heartbeat and stale-worker recovery
* PCF instance-based worker and run ID generation
* Hikari connection pools for DB2 and MariaDB

## Main processing flow

```text
Scheduler starts
    ↓
Generate RUN_ID
    ↓
Select up to 100,000 unique pending customers
    ↓
Conditionally claim every staging row for those customers
    ↓
Re-read customers actually owned by this RUN_ID
    ↓
Split customer IDs into chunks of 500
    ↓
Submit chunks to bounded two-thread executor
    ↓
Each chunk:
    ├── Optional bulk MariaDB existence check
    ├── Read DB2 staging rows for 500 customers
    ├── Batch-upsert all rows in one MariaDB transaction
    ├── If bulk transaction fails:
    │      retry customer-by-customer
    ├── Batch-update successful DB2 customers
    └── Batch-update failed DB2 customers
```

## Important optimization included

A straightforward customer-level implementation could cause:

```text
100,000 customers
= 100,000 MariaDB transactions
```

The supplied implementation instead uses:

```text
100,000 customers
÷ 500 customers per chunk
= approximately 200 normal MariaDB transactions
```

When a chunk succeeds, all its rows are migrated using JDBC batching in one transaction.

Only when a chunk fails does the application roll it back and retry individual customers to identify the problematic customer.

```text
Normal case:
500 customers → one MariaDB transaction

Failure case:
Bulk transaction rolls back
→ retry those 500 customers separately
→ isolate actual failures
```

This gives both high throughput and customer-level error identification.

## Key configuration

```yaml
migration:
  enabled: true
  scheduler-delay-ms: 5000

  max-customers-per-run: 100000
  customer-chunk-size: 500

  async-threads: 2
  executor-queue-capacity: 20

  claim-jdbc-batch-size: 200
  status-update-chunk-size: 500
  maria-upsert-batch-size: 500

  max-retries: 3
  heartbeat-interval-seconds: 60
  stale-timeout-minutes: 30

  skip-existing-customers: false
```

## Existing-customer check

The safe default is:

```yaml
skip-existing-customers: false
```

This means every claimed customer is sent through an idempotent MariaDB upsert. It prevents a partially migrated customer from being incorrectly skipped.

Setting this to:

```yaml
skip-existing-customers: true
```

performs a bulk query such as:

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM TARGET_PERSONALISATION
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID IN (?, ?, ...);
```

However, this is safe only when the presence of any MariaDB personalisation row guarantees the whole customer was previously migrated.

For example, this is unsafe:

```text
DB2 rows for customer:      15
MariaDB rows for customer:   4
```

A simple existing-customer query would skip that customer even though migration is incomplete.

Therefore, the recommended production setting remains:

```yaml
skip-existing-customers: false
```

The MariaDB upsert protects against duplicates.

## MariaDB batch upsert

```sql
INSERT INTO TARGET_PERSONALISATION
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY,
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE,
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT,
    MIGRATED_AT
)
VALUES
(
    ?, ?, ?, ?, ?, CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6)
)
ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE
    PERSONALISATION_VALUE =
        VALUES(PERSONALISATION_VALUE),
    SOURCE_UPDATED_AT =
        VALUES(SOURCE_UPDATED_AT),
    MIGRATED_AT =
        CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6);
```

Required unique key:

```sql
PRIMARY KEY
(
    CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID,
    PERSONALISATION_TYPE,
    PERSONALISATION_KEY
);
```

## Threading model

The implementation does not open one thread for each chunk.

For 100,000 customers:

```text
100,000 ÷ 500
= 200 chunk tasks
```

But only two tasks run concurrently per application instance:

```text
Executor threads: 2
Queue capacity:   20
Rejection policy: CallerRunsPolicy
```

When the queue is full, the scheduler thread processes a chunk. This creates backpressure instead of allowing unlimited queue growth.

With six application instances:

```text
6 instances × 2 threads
= 12 active chunk processors
```

That is the recommended initial production setting.

I could not run a Maven compilation in the execution environment because Maven is not installed there. The project should still be treated as a production reference that needs its table names, column types, DB2 timestamp syntax, driver versions and existing migration-service integration aligned with your actual environment.



how the scheduler know the aync thread completed ? and pick up next 2 chunks within interval assume 5 mins? how about race condition - next schedule start before the current one compelte


The scheduler must **wait for the submitted async tasks to complete before its method returns**. Otherwise, with `fixedDelay`, Spring considers the scheduler execution finished immediately after task submission and can trigger another run while the earlier chunks are still executing.

## The unsafe pattern

```java
@Scheduled(fixedDelay = 300_000)
public void migrate() {
    List<List<Long>> chunks = claimAndSplit();

    for (List<Long> chunk : chunks) {
        chunkProcessor.processAsync(chunk);
    }

    // Method returns immediately.
}
```

`processAsync()` returns a `CompletableFuture` immediately. The scheduler method finishes without waiting.

The timeline becomes:

```text
12:00 Scheduler claims 100,000 customers
12:00 Submits async tasks
12:01 Scheduler method returns

12:06 Previous async tasks may still be running
12:06 Next scheduler invocation starts
```

Even though database claiming should prevent the same customers from being claimed again, this can cause:

* More than two chunks running per instance
* Executor queue continuously growing
* Excessive DB connections
* Multiple large `RUN_ID`s active on the same instance
* Memory growth
* Complicated stale recovery
* Shutdown and monitoring difficulties

---

# Recommended solution

Use all three controls:

1. `CompletableFuture.allOf(...).join()` so the scheduler waits.
2. `fixedDelay`, not `fixedRate`.
3. `AtomicBoolean` to prevent same-instance scheduler overlap as an extra safeguard.

## Scheduler implementation

```java
@Component
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@Slf4j
public class PersonalisationMigrationScheduler {

    private final AtomicBoolean schedulerRunning =
            new AtomicBoolean(false);

    private final CustomerClaimService claimService;
    private final RunProcessor runProcessor;
    private final WorkerIdentityService workerIdentityService;
    private final MigrationProperties properties;
    private final MigrationWindowService windowService;

    @Scheduled(
        fixedDelayString =
            "${migration.scheduler-delay-ms:300000}"
    )
    public void execute() {

        if (!windowService.isMigrationWindowOpen()) {
            return;
        }

        if (!schedulerRunning.compareAndSet(false, true)) {
            log.warn(
                "Previous scheduler execution is still running on this instance"
            );
            return;
        }

        String workerId =
                workerIdentityService.workerId();

        try {
            ClaimResult claim =
                    claimService.claimCustomers(
                        workerId,
                        properties.maxCustomersPerRun()
                    );

            if (claim.customerIds().isEmpty()) {
                log.info(
                    "No migration work available for worker={}",
                    workerId
                );
                return;
            }

            log.info(
                "Worker={} runId={} claimedCustomers={}",
                workerId,
                claim.runId(),
                claim.customerIds().size()
            );

            RunResult result =
                    runProcessor.processAndWait(
                        claim.runId(),
                        workerId,
                        claim.customerIds()
                    );

            log.info(
                """
                Run completed:
                worker={}, runId={}, claimed={},
                migrated={}, alreadyMigrated={},
                retryPending={}, failed={}
                """,
                workerId,
                claim.runId(),
                claim.customerIds().size(),
                result.migrated(),
                result.alreadyMigrated(),
                result.retryPending(),
                result.failed()
            );

        } catch (Exception exception) {
            log.error(
                "Migration scheduler failed for worker={}",
                workerId,
                exception
            );
        } finally {
            schedulerRunning.set(false);
        }
    }
}
```

The scheduler method does not return until `processAndWait()` completes all chunks.

---

# How two async threads pick up the next chunks

Suppose:

```text
100,000 customers
Chunk size = 500 customers
Total chunks = 200
Executor threads = 2
```

You submit the 200 chunk tasks into a bounded executor.

```text
Thread 1 → Chunk 1
Thread 2 → Chunk 2

Thread 1 completes Chunk 1
Thread 1 automatically takes Chunk 3 from queue

Thread 2 completes Chunk 2
Thread 2 automatically takes Chunk 4 from queue
```

You do not need the scheduler to fire again to assign the next two chunks. The executor itself continuously pulls queued tasks.

The five-minute scheduler interval applies only **after the complete 100,000-customer run finishes**.

```text
Scheduler starts
    ↓
Claims 100,000 customers
    ↓
Creates 200 chunk tasks
    ↓
Two executor threads process all 200 tasks
    ↓
Scheduler waits for all tasks
    ↓
Scheduler method returns
    ↓
fixedDelay waits five minutes
    ↓
Next scheduler starts
```

---

# Run processor that waits for completion

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@Slf4j
public class RunProcessor {

    private final MigrationChunkProcessor chunkProcessor;
    private final MigrationProperties properties;

    public RunResult processAndWait(
            String runId,
            String workerId,
            List<Long> customerIds) {

        List<List<Long>> chunks =
                PartitionUtils.partition(
                    customerIds,
                    properties.customerChunkSize()
                );

        log.info(
            "Submitting {} chunks for runId={}",
            chunks.size(),
            runId
        );

        List<CompletableFuture<ChunkResult>> futures =
                new ArrayList<>(chunks.size());

        for (int chunkIndex = 0;
             chunkIndex < chunks.size();
             chunkIndex++) {

            List<Long> chunk = chunks.get(chunkIndex);

            CompletableFuture<ChunkResult> future =
                    chunkProcessor.processChunkAsync(
                        runId,
                        workerId,
                        chunkIndex,
                        chunk
                    );

            futures.add(future);
        }

        CompletableFuture<Void> allCompleted =
                CompletableFuture.allOf(
                    futures.toArray(
                        CompletableFuture[]::new
                    )
                );

        /*
         * Blocks the scheduler thread only.
         * Async migration threads continue processing.
         */
        allCompleted.join();

        RunResult result = new RunResult();

        for (CompletableFuture<ChunkResult> future : futures) {
            try {
                ChunkResult chunkResult = future.join();
                result.add(chunkResult);

            } catch (CompletionException exception) {
                /*
                 * A chunk-level unexpected failure should normally
                 * already have reset or failed its owned customers.
                 */
                log.error(
                    "Unexpected chunk failure for runId={}",
                    runId,
                    exception.getCause()
                );

                result.incrementFailedChunk();
            }
        }

        return result;
    }
}
```

The scheduler thread is blocked at:

```java
allCompleted.join();
```

But the two migration executor threads remain active.

---

# Async chunk processor

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@Slf4j
public class MigrationChunkProcessor {

    private final ChunkMigrationService chunkMigrationService;

    @Async("migrationExecutor")
    public CompletableFuture<ChunkResult> processChunkAsync(
            String runId,
            String workerId,
            int chunkIndex,
            List<Long> customerIds) {

        long startedAt = System.currentTimeMillis();

        try {
            log.info(
                "Starting runId={}, chunk={}, customers={}",
                runId,
                chunkIndex,
                customerIds.size()
            );

            ChunkResult result =
                    chunkMigrationService.processChunk(
                        runId,
                        workerId,
                        customerIds
                    );

            log.info(
                """
                Completed runId={}, chunk={}, customers={},
                durationMs={}
                """,
                runId,
                chunkIndex,
                customerIds.size(),
                System.currentTimeMillis() - startedAt
            );

            return CompletableFuture.completedFuture(result);

        } catch (Exception exception) {
            log.error(
                "Chunk failed runId={}, chunk={}",
                runId,
                chunkIndex,
                exception
            );

            return CompletableFuture.failedFuture(exception);
        }
    }
}
```

The `@Async` method must be in a separate Spring bean. Calling an `@Async` method from another method in the same object will bypass the Spring proxy and may execute synchronously.

---

# Bounded executor

```java
@Configuration
@EnableAsync
public class MigrationExecutorConfiguration {

    @Bean(name = "migrationExecutor")
    public ThreadPoolTaskExecutor migrationExecutor(
            MigrationProperties properties) {

        ThreadPoolTaskExecutor executor =
                new ThreadPoolTaskExecutor();

        executor.setCorePoolSize(
            properties.asyncThreads()
        );

        executor.setMaxPoolSize(
            properties.asyncThreads()
        );

        executor.setQueueCapacity(
            properties.executorQueueCapacity()
        );

        executor.setThreadNamePrefix(
            "migration-worker-"
        );

        executor.setWaitForTasksToCompleteOnShutdown(true);
        executor.setAwaitTerminationSeconds(120);

        executor.setRejectedExecutionHandler(
            new ThreadPoolExecutor.CallerRunsPolicy()
        );

        executor.initialize();
        return executor;
    }
}
```

Configuration:

```yaml
migration:
  scheduler-delay-ms: 300000

  max-customers-per-run: 100000
  customer-chunk-size: 500

  async-threads: 2
  executor-queue-capacity: 10
```

## How the bounded queue behaves

With:

```text
2 running threads
10 queued chunks
```

the scheduler can submit 12 chunks quickly.

When it attempts to submit chunk 13 and the queue is full, `CallerRunsPolicy` makes the scheduler thread process that chunk itself.

This creates backpressure, but it means you could temporarily have:

```text
2 executor threads
+ 1 scheduler caller thread
= 3 concurrently processing chunks
```

If you require a strict maximum of exactly two chunks, do not use `CallerRunsPolicy`.

Use `AbortPolicy` combined with controlled submission, or use `ExecutorCompletionService`.

---

# Better design: only keep two chunks in flight

For your case, this is the best approach. Do not queue all 200 chunks.

Submit only two chunks initially. Whenever one completes, submit the next chunk.

```java
@Service
@RequiredArgsConstructor
@Slf4j
public class RunProcessor {

    private final ExecutorService migrationExecutor;
    private final ChunkMigrationService chunkMigrationService;
    private final MigrationProperties properties;

    public RunResult processAndWait(
            String runId,
            String workerId,
            List<Long> customerIds) {

        List<List<Long>> chunks =
                PartitionUtils.partition(
                    customerIds,
                    properties.customerChunkSize()
                );

        int concurrency =
                properties.asyncThreads();

        CompletionService<ChunkResult> completionService =
                new ExecutorCompletionService<>(
                    migrationExecutor
                );

        int nextChunkIndex = 0;
        int submitted = 0;
        int completed = 0;

        /*
         * Initially submit only two chunks.
         */
        while (nextChunkIndex < chunks.size()
                && submitted < concurrency) {

            submitChunk(
                completionService,
                runId,
                workerId,
                nextChunkIndex,
                chunks.get(nextChunkIndex)
            );

            nextChunkIndex++;
            submitted++;
        }

        RunResult runResult = new RunResult();

        /*
         * Wait for any one chunk to complete.
         * After completion, immediately submit the next chunk.
         */
        while (completed < submitted) {
            try {
                Future<ChunkResult> completedFuture =
                        completionService.take();

                ChunkResult result =
                        completedFuture.get();

                runResult.add(result);
                completed++;

                if (nextChunkIndex < chunks.size()) {
                    submitChunk(
                        completionService,
                        runId,
                        workerId,
                        nextChunkIndex,
                        chunks.get(nextChunkIndex)
                    );

                    nextChunkIndex++;
                    submitted++;
                }

            } catch (InterruptedException exception) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                throw new IllegalStateException(
                    "Migration run interrupted",
                    exception
                );

            } catch (ExecutionException exception) {
                completed++;

                log.error(
                    "Chunk processing failed for runId={}",
                    runId,
                    exception.getCause()
                );

                runResult.incrementFailedChunk();

                if (nextChunkIndex < chunks.size()) {
                    submitChunk(
                        completionService,
                        runId,
                        workerId,
                        nextChunkIndex,
                        chunks.get(nextChunkIndex)
                    );

                    nextChunkIndex++;
                    submitted++;
                }
            }
        }

        return runResult;
    }

    private void submitChunk(
            CompletionService<ChunkResult> completionService,
            String runId,
            String workerId,
            int chunkIndex,
            List<Long> customerIds) {

        completionService.submit(
            () -> chunkMigrationService.processChunk(
                runId,
                workerId,
                chunkIndex,
                customerIds
            )
        );
    }
}
```

This produces the exact behaviour you asked about:

```text
Start:
Thread 1 → Chunk 1
Thread 2 → Chunk 2

Chunk 2 completes first:
Thread 2 → Chunk 3 immediately

Chunk 1 completes:
Thread 1 → Chunk 4 immediately
```

The scheduler does not wait five minutes between chunks.

The five-minute delay starts only after all 200 chunks finish and the scheduler method returns.

---

# Executor bean for `CompletionService`

```java
@Configuration
public class MigrationExecutorConfiguration {

    @Bean(destroyMethod = "shutdown")
    public ExecutorService migrationExecutor(
            MigrationProperties properties) {

        return new ThreadPoolExecutor(
            properties.asyncThreads(),
            properties.asyncThreads(),
            0L,
            TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS,
            new LinkedBlockingQueue<>(
                properties.asyncThreads()
            ),
            new ThreadFactoryBuilder()
                .setNameFormat("migration-worker-%d")
                .build(),
            new ThreadPoolExecutor.AbortPolicy()
        );
    }
}
```

Because `RunProcessor` submits only the configured concurrency, the queue remains small.

You can also use Spring's `ThreadPoolTaskExecutor`, but `ExecutorCompletionService` works most naturally with `ExecutorService`.

---

# Race condition: next schedule before current completes

## Within the same PCF instance

Use:

```java
private final AtomicBoolean schedulerRunning =
        new AtomicBoolean(false);
```

This prevents overlap inside one JVM.

But when the scheduler waits for all chunks and uses `fixedDelay`, overlap should not normally occur anyway. The atomic flag is defence in depth.

## Across different PCF instances

Each PCF instance runs its own scheduler independently:

```text
DCE instance 0 scheduler
DCE instance 1 scheduler
DCW instance 0 scheduler
...
```

They can start at the same time. This is expected.

Database claiming prevents customer overlap:

```sql
UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
SET STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS',
    RUN_ID = ?,
    CLAIMED_BY = ?
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND STATUS IN ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING');
```

Only one worker should receive a positive update count for the customer's rows.

For stronger customer-level claiming without another table, use a transaction and lock the candidate customer's staging rows before updating them, or derive ownership based on the conditional update and then re-read:

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
WHERE RUN_ID = ?
  AND CLAIMED_BY = ?
  AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS';
```

The worker processes only customers visible under its own `RUN_ID`.

---

# Important race in the earlier staging-table approach

Suppose two instances simultaneously attempt to claim customer 123, which has ten staging rows.

Without proper locking, a theoretical risk is:

```text
Worker A updates some rows
Worker B updates remaining rows
```

Usually an `UPDATE ... WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ? AND STATUS='PENDING'` obtains locks as it updates qualifying rows, but you should not rely on application timing.

The safer pattern is:

```sql
SELECT STAGE_ID
FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
  AND STATUS IN ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
FOR UPDATE;
```

Then update all rows in the same short DB2 transaction.

```java
@Transactional(transactionManager = "db2TransactionManager")
public boolean claimOneCustomer(
        long customerId,
        String runId,
        String workerId) {

    List<Long> stageIds =
            db2JdbcTemplate.queryForList(
                """
                SELECT STAGE_ID
                FROM PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
                WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
                  AND STATUS IN
                      ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
                FOR UPDATE
                """,
                Long.class,
                customerId
            );

    if (stageIds.isEmpty()) {
        return false;
    }

    int updated = db2JdbcTemplate.update(
        """
        UPDATE PERSONALISATION_MIGRATION_STAGE
        SET STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS',
            RUN_ID = ?,
            CLAIMED_BY = ?,
            CLAIMED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
            LAST_HEARTBEAT_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP,
            UPDATED_AT = CURRENT TIMESTAMP
        WHERE CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID = ?
          AND STATUS IN
              ('PENDING', 'RETRY_PENDING')
        """,
        runId,
        workerId,
        customerId
    );

    return updated == stageIds.size();
}
```

However, claiming 100,000 customers this way means 100,000 small transactions or locks, which can be expensive.

A more scalable single-table option is to assign customers using fixed logical partitions based on customer ID:

```text
PARTITION_ID = MOD(CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID, 60)
```

Each scheduler invocation claims complete partitions or a subset of one partition. This reduces workers competing for the same leading customers.

---

# Recommended scheduler configuration

For the complete-run waiting model:

```yaml
migration:
  scheduler-delay-ms: 300000
  max-customers-per-run: 100000
  customer-chunk-size: 500
  async-threads: 2
```

Actual timing:

```text
00:00 Scheduler starts
00:00 Claims 100,000 customers
00:00–00:45 Two async workers process all chunks
00:45 Scheduler method returns
00:45–00:50 fixed delay
00:50 Next scheduler starts
```

It does **not** mean:

```text
00:00 Start first two chunks
00:05 Scheduler starts next two chunks
```

The executor handles the next chunks continuously.

## Better than a five-minute scheduler

For bulk migration, five minutes may unnecessarily idle the worker after a run completes.

A smaller delay is better:

```yaml
scheduler-delay-ms: 5000
```

Then:

```text
Run finishes
→ wait five seconds
→ claim next available workload
```

The DB and executor concurrency settings—not the scheduler delay—should control load.

# Final recommended pattern

```text
Each PCF instance:
    @Scheduled(fixedDelay = 5 seconds)
            ↓
    AtomicBoolean guards same JVM
            ↓
    Claim unique customers in DB2
            ↓
    Split into 500-customer chunks
            ↓
    Keep exactly 2 chunks in flight
            ↓
    When either finishes:
        immediately submit next chunk
            ↓
    Wait until all chunks complete
            ↓
    Scheduler method returns
            ↓
    Five-second fixed delay
            ↓
    Next scheduler execution
```

Across PCF instances, DB2 status-and-run-ID claiming prevents overlap. Within each instance, waiting for all chunk futures plus the atomic guard prevents the next schedule from starting before the current run finishes.

The extended implementation includes strict async completion handling, monitoring, retriggering, sanity execution, and reconciliation.

[Download the full Spring Boot implementation](sandbox:/mnt/data/personalisation-migration-monitoring.zip)

## How async chunk completion works

For a run containing 100,000 unique customers:

```text
100,000 customers
÷ 500 customers per chunk
= 200 chunks
```

With two async threads:

```text
Thread 1 → Chunk 1
Thread 2 → Chunk 2

Chunk 1 completes
Thread 1 immediately takes Chunk 3

Chunk 2 completes
Thread 2 immediately takes Chunk 4
```

`ExecutorCompletionService` keeps exactly two chunks active. The scheduler waits until all 200 chunks finish.

```text
Scheduler starts
→ claims customers
→ submits two chunks
→ continuously replaces completed chunks
→ waits for all chunks
→ scheduler method returns
→ fixedDelay begins
→ next scheduler execution starts
```

Therefore, a five-minute interval does **not** apply between chunks. It begins only after the complete run finishes.

## Scheduler overlap protection

The implementation has three safeguards:

1. `AtomicBoolean` prevents a second scheduler execution in the same application instance.
2. `fixedDelay` starts only after the current scheduler method returns.
3. DB2 conditional claiming prevents overlap across DCE/DCW application instances.

A worker processes only rows matching:

```sql
RUN_ID = :runId
AND CLAIMED_BY = :workerId
AND STATUS = 'IN_PROGRESS'
```

MariaDB also uses an idempotent upsert and business unique key as the final duplicate protection.

## Operational endpoints

### Sanity test — first 500 available customers

```http
POST /internal/migration/sanity?limit=500
```

This uses the full production flow:

```text
Claim
→ split into chunks
→ MariaDB upsert
→ DB2 status update
→ wait for completion
```

### Manual processing

```http
POST /internal/migration/process?limit=10000
```

Maximum allowed:

```text
100,000 unique customers
```

### List recent batches

```http
GET /internal/migration/runs?limit=50
```

Returns:

* `RUN_ID`
* Worker/DC and instance
* Start time
* Last activity time
* Customer count
* Staging-row count
* Migrated rows
* Already-migrated rows
* Failed rows
* In-progress rows

### Monitor one batch

```http
GET /internal/migration/runs/{runId}
```

Example information:

```json
{
  "runId": "DCE_0_abcd_20260801T010000_xyz",
  "totalCustomers": 100000,
  "totalRows": 1250000,
  "hasInProgress": true,
  "stale": false,
  "activity": {
    "startedAt": "2026-08-01T02:00:00",
    "lastHeartbeatAt": "2026-08-01T02:12:00",
    "lastActivityAt": "2026-08-01T02:12:01",
    "inProgressRows": 15000
  },
  "statusCounts": []
}
```

### Retrigger a stopped run

```http
POST /internal/migration/runs/{runId}/retrigger
    ?includeFailed=true
    &force=false
    &processNowLimit=500
```

Behaviour:

* `MIGRATED` remains unchanged.
* `ALREADY_MIGRATED` remains unchanged.
* `IN_PROGRESS` is reset to `RETRY_PENDING`.
* `RETRY_PENDING` is reset for processing.
* `FAILED` is included only when `includeFailed=true`.
* The rows are claimed under a new `RUN_ID`.

When the heartbeat is recent, the endpoint rejects the retrigger unless:

```text
force=true
```

This prevents an operator from accidentally resetting an active worker.

### Pending count

```http
GET /internal/migration/pending
```

Returns the number of distinct customers still in:

```text
PENDING
RETRY_PENDING
```

## Reconciliation endpoints

### Fast row-count validation

```http
GET /internal/migration/verify?offset=0&limit=500
```

For each selected customer, it compares:

```text
DB2 staging row count
versus
MariaDB target row count
```

Possible results:

```text
MATCHED
MISSING
COUNT_MISMATCH
```

### Exact composite-key validation

```http
GET /internal/migration/verify/keys?offset=0&limit=100
```

This compares:

```text
CUSTOMER_SYS_GEN_ID
+ PERSONALISATION_TYPE
+ PERSONALISATION_KEY
```

It reports:

* Missing MariaDB keys
* Unexpected MariaDB keys
* Source key count
* Target key count

This is stronger than comparing totals alone.
